# Task registry

One place to look, and one way to name a task in conversation.

## The ID scheme, and why it is shaped this way

`AREA-localid`. The area prefix is new; **the local id is whatever that document already called it**, so
nothing is renumbered and every existing cross-reference inside those documents keeps working.

| prefix | area | source of truth |
|---|---|---|
| `AN-` | anomaly guard | `docs/aiops/aiops-backlog.md` |
| `TG-` | `[LongFact]` release gate | `docs/test-gate-backlog.md` |
| `MS-` | metric-source seam (replay) | `docs/specs/anomaly-guard-metric-source-seam-plan.md` |
| `PS-` | PSI CPU-pressure channel | `docs/specs/anomaly-guard-psi-cpu-channel-plan.md` |
| `RS-` | .NET runtime signals | `docs/specs/anomaly-guard-runtime-signals-plan.md` |
| `XC-` | cross-cutting, no plan of its own | this file |
| `NR-` | NASA Power of 10 follow-ups | `ROADMAP.md` §NASA Power of 10 |
| `AV-` | agentic / interop / vision backlog | `ROADMAP.md` §Agentic / interop / vision backlog |
| `TT-` | audio / TTS backlog | `ROADMAP.md` §Audio / TTS backlog |
| `AL-` | adoption / launch roadmap | `ROADMAP.md` §Adoption / launch roadmap |
| `LG-` | llama.cpp scope-gap snapshot | `ROADMAP.md` §llama.cpp scope-gap snapshot |
| `MO-` | Mixture-of-Experts post-launch track | `ROADMAP.md` §Post-launch track #1 — MoE |
| `LT-` | LoRA / QLoRA training track | `ROADMAP.md` §Active track (anomaly LoRA) + §Deferred → LoRA training + GGUF→training bridge |
| `QT-` | quantization / format coverage | `ROADMAP.md` §Deferred — Qwen / Llama / quantization track |
| `PB-` | performance backlog | `ROADMAP.md` §Performance backlog |
| `MK-` | market-driven priorities | `ROADMAP.md` §Market-driven priorities |
| `MT-` | medium-term features & distribution | `ROADMAP.md` §Medium-term |

The problem it solves: three plan documents each have a "Task 1", so "do task 3" was ambiguous. `MS-3`
is not.

**Ids are never reused, and one collision has already happened here.** The rule-trace task filed on
2026-08-12 was written as `AN-D12`, a number `ErrorRate`'s unit defect had held since 2026-08-09 — so for
a day this file carried two different tasks under one id, one `OPEN` and one `DONE`, which is exactly the
ambiguity the scheme exists to remove. The newer one was renumbered to `AN-D14` on 2026-08-12; `AN-D12`
stays with the older task because `docs/aiops/aiops-coverage-map.md` already points at it by that name.
**Before filing, take the highest id in the series rather than the highest one visible on screen** — the
collision came from reading a table that had scrolled.

**Status vocabulary**, chosen so that the uncomfortable states have names:

| status | means |
|---|---|
| `DONE` | finished and, where a gate applies, it passed |
| `PART` | genuinely half-done, with the remaining half named |
| `OPEN` | not started |
| `FAILED` | ran, did not meet its own criterion |
| `UNGATED` | **code is committed but never verified or reviewed** — the state that otherwise hides |
| `DEFER` | deliberately not now, with a recorded reason |

**The status cell MUST BEGIN with one of those six words.** Anything after them is free text and is where the
detail belongs — `DONE — REFUTED 2026-07-05, …` is right; `REFUTED` alone is not.

**This is enforced by `Scripts/check_task_statuses.py`, and it is enforced because the file had already
rotted.** Swept 2026-08-17: **23 of 197 rows carried a status outside the vocabulary** — `IMPLEMENTED`,
`REVIEWED`, `MEASURED`, `DECIDED`, `CLOSED`, `AUDITED`, `SUPERSEDED`, `REJECTED AS WRITTEN`, `REFUTED`, and
one that opened with `(a) and (b) DELIVERED`. Every one of them was *true*; not one of them was
*answerable*. **The cost is not tidiness.** `XC-47`'s note ended in "DONE 2026-08-14" while its cell said
`OPEN`, so it was proposed as work and cost a re-verification pass before anyone noticed; `XC-69` asked for
a test that had been committed, mutation-proved and shipped in `93ff7c6`; and every "what is open" answer
given from this file — including several this month — quoted a number that was wrong. **A registry whose
status field cannot be read by a machine is a registry with no status field.**

Four of the twenty-three turned out to be genuinely `PART` rather than finished, and each says so in its own
words (`AN-C1` "Open: the margin is 1.4%", `XC-69` "the test was run only on Windows", `NR-5` "fixes OPEN",
`PB-12` "OPEN WORK: the 455/2059 figure is written in FIVE places"). That is the other half of the point:
flattening them all to `DONE` would have been the same defect facing the other way.

---

## Anomaly guard — `AN-`

| id | status | task | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| `AN-A1` | **FAILED, and the criterion itself is the finding** | 24-hour run on a frozen configuration | 10.63/day against a 1-9/day criterion, plus one cycle failure; tail contaminated by a test run on the same box. **Worked out properly 2026-08-10, and it changes the plan rather than the schedule.** Incidents-per-day is a count of rare events, so its uncertainty is governed by the number of EVENTS, not by the run's length in hours — 288 cycles a day do not turn 11 events into 288 observations. Exact Poisson interval for 11 is **[5.49, 19.68]**, which straddles the bound, so the run was never capable of a verdict (implementation checked against the published value for k=11 rather than trusted). **Power, one-sided, alpha 5%, 80%:** a true rate of 3/day needs 0.96 days to demonstrate 'below 9'; 5/day needs 2.7; 7/day needs 12.3; **8/day needs 52.5 — undecidable in practice.** Inverting it gives the number that decides the plan: **with a single 24-hour run the true rate must fall to <= 3.09/day**. So the acceptance target is not '<= 9' — it is **<= 3/day**, chosen because it is the largest rate one day can demonstrate, and that is what `AN-D1` and `AN-D9` have to reach. Keeping 9 means budgeting three or more days per attempt. **Switching the metric does NOT rescue it**: 'share of time with an incident open' looks like 288 observations a day and is not — cycles are autocorrelated, one incident spans dozens, so the effective sample is the number of episodes, ~11 again. It is still worth reporting beside the rate because an incident open for twenty hours counts as one opening and is a permanently red screen (`AN-D1`), but as a **cost** measure, not as a way to make the decision cheaper **`BLOCKED` 2026-08-14 by `overfit-aiops-statistician`, its first verdict — and the headline is that THE RATE IS THE WRONG ESTIMAND.** Read straight out of this run's own log and **verified independently by the main session: an incident was open for 280 of 298 cycles — 94.0% of the run.** Eleven openings but only **9 occupancy episodes**, mean 156 minutes, the longest **10.8 hours**; the gaps average 10 minutes. **So "10.63 incidents/day" describes a screen that was red 94% of the time, and cutting the rate to 3/day with the same duration profile still leaves ~80% occupancy.** Meeting the criterion would not deliver a quiet guard. Same shape as `AN-D4b`: measured correctly, describing the wrong thing. **Three defects, each with arithmetic.** (1) **The bar was moved from 9 to 3 without redoing the power calculation.** `≤3.09/day` is the largest true rate one day can demonstrate to be **below 9** — it is a target conditional on the criterion staying 9. Against a 3/day *criterion*, one day requires **zero** incidents and reaches 80% power only at **λ ≤ 0.22/day**, a 48x cut from 10.63; and nothing decides "≈3 against a bar of 3", which needs ~113 expected events ≈ **38 days**. The clock-free invariant worth keeping: demonstrating true = bar/3 needs **~3** expected events, bar/2 **~10**, ⅔ bar **~32**, 0.8 bar **~113**; days = events ÷ true rate. **Decidable alternatives**: keep the 9/day bar and one day decides λ≤2.30 at a strict 5% (`k≤3`), or ≤3.09 at 5.5% (`k≤4`) — the row's number is the `k≤4` answer and **the pass rule must say which region it uses, because at exactly 4 incidents they disagree**. Two days decide λ≤4.08, three λ≤5.09, seven λ≤6.28. (2) **The rate is not a property of the configuration.** 11 incidents at 14.17 req/s against 40 at 21.46 req/s on an unchanged healthy config — **3.64x the count for a 51% traffic rise, log-log elasticity 3.11**. That makes it a Cox process, so **two runs at different traffic levels are not replicates** and a bar in incidents/day is undefined until the protocol pins and reports the load profile. Within this run the Poisson model does hold (inter-arrival CV 0.954, dispersion 0.82-1.68), so the interval is valid for that day's λ; it is the between-run λ that is unpinned, and no artefact measures it — the other `fp-run-guard-log-*` files are **the same run truncated**. (3) **The rate is unstable in run length**: at 23.67 h the log reads 9.13/day (passing ≤9), at 24.83 h **10.63/day** — a 16% move from 1.16 h more data, while occupancy moved 93.7% → 94.0%. **`AN-F1` does NOT help a cold run, which refutes what I expected**: `AnomalyGuard.cs:1529` overwrites the seasonal expectation with `common` unconditionally on a successful decomposition, and this run **already took that branch** — `MinimumHistoryDays = 2` and the PVC held five hour-buckets from one day, so seasonal was empty. The 11 → 33 measurement is cold-versus-warm on one window and this run was the cold arm. **Channels went 12 → 18 (+50%), direction UP**, though bounded by the grouping rule: `IncidentTracker` matches on the primary subject, so at 94% occupancy most added firing raises **findings**, not openings. **Estimate: a repeat lands at or above 10.63/day.** **The cheapest experiment that unblocks all of this, and it costs nothing on top of a run already planned: record the RAW METRIC WINDOW during the next run, not just the guard log.** `Tests/Anomalies/Diagnostics/LabFixtureRecorderDiagnostics.cs` already does it — ~8-10 MB for 24 h — but **one gap must close first, verified by the main session: it loops `for (var m = 0; m < (int)MetricIndex.Count; m++)` (`:262`), so it records built-ins only and cannot capture the six custom channels whose rate is in question.** With that fixed, every future threshold change is evaluated on identical data in seconds, with no cluster and no waiting. **Refuting observations named**: a repeat with ~11 openings but occupancy well below 94% would collapse the estimand finding — and the single 130-cycle episode is 46% of all occupied time and **was not examined**, so the 156-minute mean rests on one observation; post-fix, a cold and a warm replay of the same window must now give the **same** count, and if they still diverge the `AN-F1` reading is wrong in the direction that helps; and the elasticity rests on **two points**, so a third traffic level near 11 incidents would restore λ as a property of the configuration |

Bonus: a plain `rollout restart` lifts findings 0→6, and the guard counts `pods=24` mid-rollout |
| `AN-B1` | DONE | client-readiness documentation | folded into `aiops-client-readiness.md` |
| `AN-C1` | **PART — MEASURED 2026-08-10 — the answer is DO NOT MOVE IT** | the heap floor was an extrapolation; it is now measured from both ends on a live fleet | **Healthy end**: peer gap p90 6.5 MB, p99 7.4, max **8.6** on a fleet rolled 50 min earlier and **9.7** on one 19 h old — so the population spreads with age and the aged max sits ABOVE the 9.52 MB floor. **Fault end**: a 100 KB/s leak injected into a mid-distribution pod (gap +1.02 -> +26.24 MB in 6 min) WAS caught — but by `GcGen2HeapBytes` (floor 0.93 MB) in the very first cycle, and by `MemoryWorkingSetBytes` (floor 9.52) only **30 minutes later**. **The decisive evidence is a healthy pod**: `peer-trace` shows `lzf4n` with `effect=1 p=0` — perfect rank separation — held back from being reported by absGap 9.39 MB against the 9.52 floor. A **1.4% margin**. So raising it (15 MB was proposed from the healthy end alone, and that proposal is **withdrawn**) lengthens an already 30-minute detection lag; lowering it buys false positives in the same second. Two channels move by a similar absolute amount under one fault while their floors differ 10x, which is why gen2 is what actually detects a leak here. Open: the margin is 1.4% and will not survive a fleet with a different spread |
| `AN-C2` | DEFER | `GcPauseRatio.minTrendChange` | one finding is not evidence enough to arm a gate |
| `AN-C3` | **DONE** | **`FloorCalibrator` expressed "enough data" in the wrong unit, and it cost a false positive.** The gate was `Samples >= 30`, and one sample is one pod in one window — so on twelve replicas it was satisfied after **three cycles, fifteen minutes**. Twelve replicas at the same instant are twelve views of one moment; a maximum needs moments. Measured 2026-08-09: a `LockContentions` floor from a three-minute window put the healthy peak at 0.0514/s, the same population over twenty minutes at **0.0952/s**, and the short-window floor reported on an unfaulted pod within the hour. Now `Samples >= 30 && Windows >= 24` — two hours at the default cadence, reasoned (overlapping 20-minute windows at a 5-minute step are ~4 apart before they are independent, so 24 gives about six independent looks at the maximum) and **stated as reasoned, not measured**, because nothing here has shown where the maximum settles. The count is persisted, and a state file predating it restores as *meeting* the minimum rather than zero — assuming zero would suspend every proposal for two hours on an upgrade, which is the no-floors state measured at 209 false incidents a day. The guard now says *why* it is not proposing instead of going quiet. Three mutations, all caught — **after a fourth exposed one of my own tests as unfailable**: it observed exactly `MinimumWindows`, which is also the legacy fallback, so both sides read 24 and removing the persistence entirely left it green |
| `AN-D1` | **PART — implemented and mutation-proven, blocked on two things** | peer reports a fixed pod property as a recurring anomaly | diagnosed 2026-08-06; corroborated 2026-08-08 (272 of 298 cycles carried exactly one finding, not a noise distribution). Plan and `docs/adr/0001-*` signed; gate implemented on both `RunPeer` and `RunCustomPeer`; **five mutations run, five red**, restores git-verified; 25 tests; the `LearnedState` compatibility test reads a REAL pre-change payload. **Blocker 1 — the floor is unmeasured**: `MinAbsoluteGapChange` ships required and un-defaulted because the only clean window available was 1.2 h against a ring spanning 8 h, and `outlier cyc` was 0 for every pod, so the distribution measured described gaps the tracker never folds. A +64 MB fixed offset was injected 2026-08-10T06:51Z to open an 8-hour calibration window. **Blocker 1a, found at 08:47Z while re-screening that window, and it is a precondition nobody had stated**: the window is not calibratable as it stands, because the quantity being calibrated is MOVING. Measured from 06:55Z, after the injection settled: the gap runs 61.3 -> 52.7 MB at **-3.94 MB/h**, which over the 8-hour ring is a fitted change of ~31 MB — so `max x 1.25` would put `MinAbsoluteGapChange` near **39 MB** and blind the channel to any leak smaller than that, against a 9.52 MB detection floor. The number would be measured correctly and describe the wrong thing. **The cause is not the offset decaying**: the target's own level rose +15.1 MB while the population median rose +23.7 MB, so the gap narrows because the fleet grows faster than the target, not because the injected bytes are released. The gap is already relative to the median, so common-mode is removed and what remains is a real, sustained divergence in growth rate. **Precondition for any future attempt: the population's own memory level must be flat over the calibration window, and that has to be checked rather than assumed** — a window chosen only for the absence of injected faults is not enough. Two measurement errors of mine on the way, both recorded because they are cheap to repeat: I first screened the window with CPU/latency thresholds borrowed from a different measurement (they flagged it contaminated; the memory gap was verified undisturbed at all four flagged timestamps, 48.4-49.2 MB with +-1 MB neighbourhood), and I then fitted the drift from a sample five minutes BEFORE the injection, so the regression measured the step rather than the drift and reported +26 MB/h. **Blocker 2, found 2026-08-10 by the `AN-D9` developer while reading for a different task, and it is the sharper one**: `MinAbsoluteGapChange` **cannot be expressed in the config at all** — `CustomMetricBinding` carries the property, but `AnomalyGuardConfigFile.CustomEntry` has only `minGap` and `minTrendChange`, and `AnomalyGuardConfigReader` never sets it. Harmless while `PeerNovelty` is null, and **fatal the day it is enabled**: `AnomalyGuard.RestoreNovelty` throws for any custom binding whose value is zero. So the feature is un-shippable until the config surface catches up, and no test covers that — the gate was proven in-process with options set in code, never through the file a deployment actually uses **HALF OF BLOCKER 2 IS ALREADY CLOSED — this row was stale, found 2026-08-12 by running protocol step 1 before dispatching anyone.** The CUSTOM side was fixed on 2026-08-10: `AnomalyGuardConfigFile.CustomEntry` carries `minGapChange` and `AnomalyGuardConfigReader.cs:145-146` sets `MinAbsoluteGapChange` from it, with the reason written into the file's own doc (*"Added 2026-08-10 because the property existed and the file could not express it"*). **The BUILT-IN side is still open and is the actual task.** `AnomalyGuardConfigReader` builds and returns exactly three per-metric arrays — `gap`, `trend`, `step` (`:175-177`, `:201`) — and there is no fourth for the change-in-gap floor; **nothing anywhere in `Sources` assigns `AnomalyGuardOptions.MinAbsoluteGapChange`**, so it can only be set in code. Consequence, quoted rather than described (`AnomalyGuard.cs:319`): `if (options.MinAbsoluteGapChange is not { } floors \|\| floors.Count < (int)MetricIndex.Count) throw` — the first deployment to enable `PeerNovelty` from a file fails at startup. That failure is deliberate and documented (*"starting loudly beats suppressing quietly"*), so this is not a silence defect; it is a feature that cannot be turned on through the artefact a deployment uses. Work: a `minGapChange` field on the built-in `thresholds` entry, a fourth array through the reader, and — per the protocol — a fixture proving a file that sets it produces the floors, a fixture proving a file that does not still refuses to start with the gate on, and the missing-data case distinguished from both **BLOCKER 2 CLOSED 2026-08-12 — and it exposed BLOCKER 3, which is larger.** `ThresholdEntry.minGapChange` shipped, threaded through `AnomalyGuardConfigReader` as a fourth per-metric array and into `AnomalyGuardOptions.MinAbsoluteGapChange`. **The fourth element is `double[]?`, null when the file declares nothing — deliberately not a zero-filled table**, because `RestoreNovelty` tests existence and length only, so a zero-filled table would clear the check and start the gate with its floor off; null is what preserves the refusal. Presence is `value > 0.0`, not "the key was written": `Quantity(...)` returns 0.0 for absent, for an explicit `"0"` and for an unreadable value alike, and all three must mean not-configured — the `AN-D4b` trap. **The registration assigns CONDITIONALLY** (`MinAbsoluteGapChange = gapChange ?? given.Guard.MinAbsoluteGapChange`), unlike the three floors beside it, because an unconditional assignment would erase a table a host set in code and turn a working gate into a startup refusal; that has its own test. 7 tests plus 4 arity fixes, **4 mutations, 4 caught**, added lines all covered. Suite 2590/0/273. **BLOCKER 3, found by the developer while verifying my brief and confirmed independently: `AnomalyGuardOptions.PeerNovelty` is itself not settable from the config file either** — every reference in `Sources` is a read (`AnomalyGuard.cs:314`, `:572`) or the tracker's own persistence in `LearnedState`; no reader assigns it. So "the first deployment to enable the gate from a file fails at startup" was never reachable: **a file cannot enable the gate at all**, only a host passing `AnomalyGuardServiceOptions.Guard` in code. A `peerNovelty` profile key on the config file is the remaining vehicle work. **BLOCKER 3 CLOSED 2026-08-13.** `peerNovelty` is a **named profile** in the file, not a bag of numbers — decided by reading `PeerNoveltyOptions`, which exposes `PerShift`/`Daily`/`Weekly` as static presets with an `IsValid` that rejects `default` and a doc explaining why no `Balanced` exists. Mapped by a private `TryNovelty` mirroring the existing private `TryKind`, so **no new public surface**; `PeerNovelty = novelty ?? given.Guard.PeerNovelty` keeps the conditional shape blocker 2 established, so a host that set a profile in code is not erased by a file that names none. **An unknown name is reported and leaves the gate OFF, never rounded to a default** — the reader's own documented policy, and the safe direction, since a guess would switch a suppression gate on from a typo while OFF merely leaves the guard as noisy as today. **The drift gate is the part worth copying**: a reflection test walks `PeerNoveltyOptions`'s public static presets and fails if one is not nameable from the file, asserting `presets.Count >= 3` BEFORE the loop so it cannot pass vacuously — a fourth preset added later would otherwise be silently un-nameable, which is this task's own defect one level up. 12 tests; **5 mutations, 5 caught**, including one whose Theory name would have printed a false survival under the pre-2026-08-13 mutate template. **`M1`'s extra victim is the compatibility evidence**: making an absent key select `Daily` also breaks the plain config-file overload every existing deployment uses, because that config has no `minGapChange`. Suite **2609/0/273**, verified independently by the main session; `Cli` builds exit 0, so the AOT `JsonSerializerContext` generator picked up the new property. **Nothing is switched on**: `peerNovelty` appears in no shipped config (checked under `k8s/**`, zero hits). **What remains for the gate to be usable is blocker 1 and it is a measurement, not code** — the floor value is still uncalibrated, so the first operator to name a profile chooses a floor with nothing behind it. The guard refuses to start without one, which is the right failure, but it does not check the number is sensible. **RESIDUAL SILENCE RISK this change does not remove, and it wants a decision**: an operator who sets `minGapChange` on ONE metric gets a table, the guard starts, and the other twelve built-ins run the novelty gate with their absolute floor at **zero** — only the relative gate binds. `RestoreNovelty` checks the table's LENGTH, not per-entry positivity, while custom channels ARE individually required to be positive. Making it symmetric needs the reader to know which metrics are mapped, which `ReadThresholds` does not. Pre-existing semantics, deliberately unchanged here. **A harness defect was also found and fixed in `overfit-mutate`**: `[Theory]` victims return as `MyTest(arg: 3)` truncated at the first space, so the template's exact-membership check reported two CAUGHT mutations as survived — an error whose direction manufactures findings, since a green mutation is defined here as a defect. **Not done, recorded as decisions**: protocol step 9 does not apply (nothing deployed, no cluster contacted, no shipped config touched); no AOT publish (`AotSmokeTest` does not reference `Anomalies` at all); no live arm — every positive is a fixture; no CHANGELOG entry, because `Anomalies` is `IsPackable=false` and this adds no published surface |
| `AN-D2` | **DONE** | answered 2026-08-08 and re-confirmed 2026-08-09 with the OOM channel live. The row's claim was half right: the peer family IS blind to a single OOMKill, but the guard is not. Yesterday only `ContainerRestarts` caught it and `OomEventsRate` contributed nothing; today the incident is **led by `OomEventsRate`**. See `AN-D6` (the injector could not produce an OOM) and `AN-D8` (the channel read a dead series) — the row was written when neither worked |
| `AN-D6` | **DONE** | `POST /fault/oom` produces a real OOMKill again — verified 2026-08-08 on a live pod: `restartCount 0 -> 1`, `reason=OOMKilled`, `exit 137`, **in under 5 s**. Three causes were fixed, and the second is the one that makes this non-obvious: (1) only 2 bytes of each 64 MB chunk were touched, so the cgroup — which charges RESIDENT memory — saw 37 MB of a 512 MiB limit while VmSize reached 6.65 GB; (2) **managed allocation cannot reach a cgroup limit at all**, because the container-aware GC caps the heap at 75% of it (384 of 512 MiB) and throws a managed `OutOfMemoryException` first — so the allocation is now native (`Marshal.AllocHGlobal`, no unsafe), outside the GC heap; (3) the throw was swallowed by a detached `Task.Run` with no continuation, and is now logged. The loop is bounded at 4 GiB (8x the limit) and **logs an error if it finishes**, because an injector that fails silently is what created this task. `/fault/clear` now cancels and frees, so it is a real undo. Deployed by replacing ONE pod, not rolling the Deployment — A5 measured that twelve fresh pods cost 0,1,0,1,6,4,3 findings over seven cycles |
| `AN-D7` | **DONE** | `blind=1` identified 2026-08-08: it is **`CpuThrottleRatio`**, which has **0 series** in the `lab` namespace because `container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total` only exists on containers with a CPU limit and no `lab-workload` pod has one. Known, documented in `k8s/README.md`, and correctly counted — the counter is doing its job. Not the OOM channel: that one has 14 series and binds fine, see `AN-D8` |
| `AN-D8` | **DONE** | `OomEventsRate` reads the series that carries an OOM, and it **fires**. Verified end to end 2026-08-09 on the live lab: `POST /fault/oom` -> `OOMKilled` exit 137 in 5 s -> the channel moved 0 -> 1.14 on the killed pod with **all eleven others at 0** -> the guard opened an incident **led by `OomEventsRate`** (*"Held above zero for 12% of the window, 7 of 60 observations, peak 1.14"*) with `ContainerRestarts` beside it. **A second defect was found and fixed after the first deploy**: a join returns NO SERIES for pods whose right-hand side is absent, which on a healthy cluster is every pod — so the first fix turned the channel from *always zero* into *always blind* (`blind` 1 -> 2, `Blind on OomEventsRate` every cycle, measured within twenty minutes). The `or <same counter> * 0` tail fills those pods with an explicit zero; `blind` is back to 1. Verbatim `query` bindings and `InertChannels()` shipped with it |
| `AN-D3` | **DONE 2026-08-11 — REFUTED, both halves now shown** | a CPU rise on every replica at once is invisible to all four families | The reading half was settled 2026-08-10: `AnomalyGuard.RunTrend` builds the cross-peer common component and tests it separately against `WorkloadSubject()`, and `DecomposeCommonMode` is on by default. **The detection arm the row still owed is now measured, live.** Fixture arm (`Tests/Anomalies/FleetWideCpuStepTests.cs`, 6 arms through the real `RunCycle`, 12 pods, the deployed 80-sample window): a 2.5x fleet-wide step reports against the workload with `NamesAPod` false on every row; a flat fleet is quiet; +30% (just over the 25% relative gate) is caught and +10% refused; a step on ONE replica is NOT reported as a workload step — the refuting arm, since a contaminated common component would have made the finding mean something else. Mutation removing `ObserveLevelShift` reds exactly the three positive arms. Live arm: `/fault/cpu?msPerRequest=3` on all 12 replicas at 09:42:26Z, held 30 min (fleet median 0.0011 -> 0.0023 cores, dropping the moment it was cleared). **Replayed through `CrossPeerBaseline` + `LevelShiftDetector` on the four windows the guard actually judged** (`FleetWideCpuStepReplayDiagnostics`): 09:57 window **Anomalous** — "the level rose from 0.001457 to 0.002338, 61%, delta 0.78, p 6.45E-10, this is a step not a drift"; 10:07 **Healthy**, both halves at the new level, the documented blind spot confirmed on live data rather than assumed. **Two things this cost, both worth more than the verdict.** (1) The guard's log prints finding lines only when an incident CHANGES STATE, so the cycles that mattered reported `findings=3` and said nothing about what those findings were — the detection was invisible from outside and had to be reconstructed. Raised as `XC-16`. (2) The window at 09:47 was `Healthy` for a reason that has nothing to do with the detector: its first half held a 0.0070-core post-reboot spike, so the halves showed a FALL during a rise. The pre-fault period must be flat in the quantity being judged, not merely free of injected faults — the same precondition `AN-D1` was blocked on, met here by accident of waiting rather than by design. Not checked: whether a cleaner start would have fired at 09:47 or 09:52 instead of 09:57 |

Cause: I bound it with `kind: Ratio`, and `MetricMap.Build` renders that as `{name}{selector}` — no `rate()`, no division — so the channel queried the raw cumulative counter and the trend family saw a perfect climb every window. **A config entry overrode a correct built-in template with a broken one**, the same trap as `OomEventsRate` the day before, in the opposite direction. | `AN-F1` | **DONE — mechanism found, fixed, and the fix now has a test that can fail** | seasonal history tripled the incident rate (11 -> 33 opened, 296 -> 517 findings) on an identical 298-cycle window | **Mechanism** (`AnomalyGuard.cs:1287-1310`): `var expectation = seasonal;` … `if (expectation.IsEmpty) { expectation = common; }`. With history the per-pod trend was judged against the seasonal expectation, without it against the cross-peer common component — **not substitutes**. `common` removes what the replicas do together right now; `seasonal` removes what the workload usually does at this hour. Substituting the second for the first leaves every common-mode movement inside each pod's own series, so a fleet climbing together produces one finding per pod. **Measured at the detector with the real `CrossPeerBaseline`, twelve pods**: +15% shared climb gives **0 against `common`, 12 against `seasonal`**; flat fleet gives 0/0, which is the arm that would have refuted the explanation. **This is the day-one noise mechanism** — `WarmUpGrace` measured a fresh replica climbing 13-17% over its first 10-20 minutes, and a rollout puts the whole fleet into that movement at once. **Fix**: `expectation = common` unconditionally when the decomposition succeeds, seasonal only as fallback — which is **exactly the shape `RunCustomTrend` already had**, so this makes two structurally parallel methods agree rather than inventing a policy. My first instinct, *compose the two*, was **wrong** and the architect refuted it: both are point estimates in the metric's own units, so adding them would double the signal's magnitude. The workload-level arm is unchanged — judging the common component against the seasonal expectation is the composition done correctly one level up. **The finding that outlived the fix**: the first mutation of the fix was caught by **nothing** — not `SeasonalExpectationTests`, not the whole `Anomalies` filter — because every existing seasonal test has a history that MATCHES the climb, so both references agree and the distinguishing case was never exercised. Closed by two new guard-level tests: a fleet climbing together under a history that does not explain it (mutation turns it red), and the control that one diverging replica is still named, without which the first is satisfied by a guard that reports nothing. Also added: `overfit_guard_trend_seasonal_only_total`, because the fallback to seasonal below three pods was a silent reference switch and its consequence looks like a fleet-wide fault. Suite 2311/0/272 |
| `AN-F2` | **DONE — REFUTED** | the `+1.00` premise has no artefact and is not reproducible (measured +0.38 per pod per window, the level the detector judges); `fixed cost = 0` does not follow from a correlation, which is intercept-invariant — the generator's own `0.45 + 0.040 x traffic` gives exactly +1.00 at 52% fixed cost. ROADMAP reason 2 void; reasons 1 and 3 stand, so the not-shipped decision holds. **New blocker for any retry**: the injected fault is work-proportional by construction, and a fixed-term step is the only regime separating affine from division — the 11/11 tie is experiment design, not a result |
| `AN-F5` | **DONE** | lab-fixture gate rebuilt: per-channel verdicts (a recording can be valid for CPU and rejected for latency), fleet-excursion detection with scrape-level excision to NaN, and the gate globs every `test_fixtures/lab/*.csv` instead of the default name. Threshold measured, not chosen: 3.38x clean vs 90.85x inside the excursion, gate at 5.0. **M7 is the evidence**: the same corruption through the pre-change enumeration failed 0 of 14 tests. Suite 2478/2209/269/0 |
| `AN-F4` | **DONE** | the discriminating fault shape now exists and was measured 2026-08-09. A fixed-term step, sized to the **same mean effect** as the work-proportional one so only its relation to work differs, is the one column where the two treatments part: `CpuUsageRatio` raw 3/4, **divided 4/3, affine 4/4** — division loses a window on the fixed step exactly as the mechanism predicts (a constant spread across a varying denominator). `GcPauseRatio` catches 0 in every cell, unchanged. **Direction confirmed, magnitude not**: one window of nine, overlapping 75%, is at the resolution limit. What this changes is that **a longer recording is now worth taking** — before it provably was not, because no length could break a tie the experiment could not express |
| `AN-F3` | **DONE** | the calibrated floors lived only in a gitignored file | Floors were rescued earlier; the reconciliation is now finished, and **the row's own premise was wrong**. Checked 2026-08-10: `guard.lab-workload.json` and the deployed ConfigMap are **identical** across `metrics`, `customMetrics` and `thresholds` — `DeployedConfigParityTests` enforces that, so those two were never the open half. And `guard.lab.json` does **not** target a superseded deployment: `overfit-server` is running **3/3** in namespace `overfit`, with `overfit-server-degraded` alongside it and a manifest at `k8s/overfit/deployment.yaml`. What was actually wrong is narrower and worse: that file had **no `workload` field**, and `AnomalyGuardConfigFile.Workload` documents exactly what blank costs — a maintenance window naming a workload can never match, so an operator declaring one for a rollout is paged through it; and the tracker keys a pod-less subject on the workload, so every deployment-level finding in the namespace collapses onto `"overfit/"`, one identity shared by unrelated problems. Fixed by setting `workload: overfit-server`, with the reason written into the file's own comment. **Nothing caught this because every existing check reads the sections with entries in them** — a missing scalar at the top of a file is not an entry that can be wrong, it is an entry that is not there. Closed by `ShippedConfigScopeTests`: every shipped config must name `namespace`, `podRegex` and `workload`, or declare `scopes` instead. Mutation: deleting the field turns it red. Suite 2308/0/271 |

Observed 2026-08-09 immediately after an eight-minute CPU fault that burned ~2 cores on the same machine as the test run; **3 of 3 green in isolation** seconds later. It asserts on wall-clock, so a loaded box moves the thing it measures. Two ways out and they are different decisions: give it a tolerance wide enough to survive a busy machine, or make it fake the clock — the second is better and matches `ValueStopwatch`/virtual-clock work already done for the guard. Recorded rather than dismissed because this repository's rule is that one red is only dismissible once it can be NAMED, and this one now can **Reproduced again 2026-08-10** at the end of a run of back-to-back suites, and passed twice in isolation immediately after — the same load dependence, now seen on a second day. |

| `TG-T13` | **DONE — SUPERSEDED 2026-08-16 — `XC-54` is a DUPLICATE of this row that I filed on 2026-08-15 without checking the series. The shared subject is fixed; this row's own preferred fix is not. See the note at the end** | `DecodePoolIdleBurnTests.Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` measures the WHOLE PROCESS, so it fails on other tests' threads | Fails inside the gate and **passes alone**, measured 2026-08-14: in the `LanguageModels.Diagnostics` area it reported **14.85** effective cores at idle, and **17.30** after an unrelated fix, while running by itself it is green. The arithmetic says it cannot be the pool: `_decodePoolSize` is `min(max(1, workers-1), 10)` = **10 threads on this box**, and ten threads cannot burn seventeen cores — at least seven belong to something else in the same process. The assertion reads `Process.TotalProcessorTime` across a 3-second sleep, and the gate runs **52 tests in one process**, so any neighbour that leaves a thread running is charged to this test. **Same shape as `TG-T12`** (green in isolation, red on a loaded box) but a different test and a different mechanism: T12 is a timeout starved of CPU, this one is a measurement with the wrong scope. **The fix is to scope the measurement, not to relax the bound**: sum the decode pool's own threads (they are named `OverfitDecode-*`) or take the delta on `ProcessThread` entries owned by the pool, so the number means "is the pool spinning" rather than "is anything in this process busy". **What it must keep**: the bound exists because a serving container burned 100% CPU at idle in the field, so whatever replaces it has to still fail on a pool that does not park. **Do not read the two suite numbers as a regression** — 14.85 and 17.30 came from different neighbours in different runs, and neither is a measurement of the pool **NOTE 2026-08-16.** `XC-54`, filed by the main session on 2026-08-15, is **this row again** — same test, same mechanism, discovered independently by the `[LongFact]` gate and written up without anyone checking whether the series already held it. It even measured the same quantity to a different value (14.57 cores, against this row's 14.85 and 17.30). **The header of this file says to check the series before filing, and `XC-42` already records me breaking that rule once.** Treat this row as the original and `XC-54` as its duplicate. **What is fixed**: `XC-54` shipped an exclusive xunit collection plus a canary and a three-valued verdict, with a park mutation reddening at 10.02 cores against a 0.00 baseline. **What is NOT fixed is precisely what this row asked for**: it prescribes *scoping the measurement* — summing the pool's own threads, which are named `OverfitDecode-{i}` (`OverfitParallel.cs:460`) — whereas the delivered fix makes the *environment* quiet instead. Those differ in a way that matters: a quiet window makes process CPU a good proxy for the pool's CPU, but the test still cannot distinguish *the pool is spinning* from *something else on this box is busy*, and it is now green in the only configuration where it can run. **The residue is real and is the useful half of this row.** Also note the delivered test's own recorded bias, which scoping would remove: external load makes a starved spinner accumulate **less** CPU per wall-second, so a heavily loaded machine can produce a false GREEN |
| `TG-T14` | **OPEN** | eight `Anomalies` acceptance tests fail in the area and pass under a narrow filter — and they have been failing since at least 2026-08-13, unreported | **They did not regress: they were already failing.** `Tests/bin/longfact-timings.log` records `2026-08-13T10:42:11Z … Anomalies … total=620 passed=604 failed=8 skipped=8 TIMEOUT` with the **same eight names**, i.e. before any of the 2026-08-14 `OverfitParallel` work. Nobody broke this recently. **The reason nobody knew** is that the gate never finished: the 2026-08-13 run hit the per-area timeout (`exit 124`, 63.31 min) and no earlier complete run exists in the log at all. A failure sat behind a tool that did not reach its own finish line. **The eight**: `AnomalyGuardTests.NamesTheDeliberatelyDegradedReplica`, `AnomalyGuardTests.RunsAgainstTheRecordedLabWindow`, `Diagnostics.SyntheticClusterCalibrationSearch.FitsTheGeneratorToTheRecordedLabWindow`, `…MultiStartDescentShowsWhetherTheLandscapeNeedsAPopulation`, and four `IncidentLifecycleAcceptanceTests` (`AnIncidentDoesNotSurviveTheSubjectItWasAbout`, `NoCycleBothOpensAndResolves`, `RemovingThePodMakesItsExclusiveMetricVisiblyBlind`, `TheFault_OpensOnce_AndResolvesOnce`). **They are disjoint from the eight lab-skipped tests** — verified name by name, and the arithmetic agrees (604+8+8=620), so 'the cluster is down' is NOT the explanation. **The messages** (2026-08-15, own TRX so they survived): the calibration pair reports *refusing to calibrate against a window that failed its own gate* — `CpuThrottleRatio` absent from all 12 replicas, and healthy p95 median **49 ms against a required 350-1800 ms**; `RunsAgainstTheRecordedLabWindow` gets `Assert.Single() Failure: The collection was empty`; the other five throw `ArgumentOutOfRangeException`. All fail in **0.002-0.22 s**, so this is not timing or contention. **The context dependence is the live finding.** All eight **pass** under `--filter` over their three classes (17 tests, 4m12s, verified test-by-test from the TRX that the eight were among the seventeen) and **fail** under the area filter (617 tests, 6 min). The fixture on disk is untouched — `git status` clean, mtime 2026-08-01 — so the polluting state is **in-process**, not the file. **Leading hypothesis, not yet confirmed**: a shifted metric-channel registry. One mechanism explains all three failure shapes — if a neighbour registers channels into static state, the same CSV parses to different indices, so `CpuThrottleRatio` reads NaN ('absent'), a collection comes back empty, and an index read throws. `CLAUDE.md` already warns that `MetricIndex` (13 channels) and `MetricSnapshot.FeatureCount` (12 features) must not drift. **In flight when this row was written**: a serial run (`xUnit.parallelizeTestCollections=false`) to split *order-dependent* from *concurrency-dependent* — green means two collections raced on shared statics, red means a neighbour leaves state behind and order alone decides. Results land in `.claude/anomalies-serial.log` and `Tests/bin/anomalies-serial.trx`. The harness also prints whether the runner accepted the switch, because an unknown RunSettings key is ignored **silently** and a green result from a dead lever means nothing. **SERIAL ARM RAN 2026-08-15 AND DID NOT SPLIT THE HYPOTHESIS — read this before repeating it.** With `xUnit.parallelizeTestCollections=false` the area came back **9 failed / 600 passed / 8 skipped in 6m05s**: the same eight **plus** `GptAnomalyLoRATargetComparisonTests.LoRATargetStages_OnAnomalyTask_AllFlattenBenign_AndKee…`. So it is not green, and concurrency alone is not the explanation — **but the run does not prove order-dependence either, because the harness could not confirm the switch was live**: it printed no runner line mentioning parallel/unknown/invalid, and an unrecognised RunSettings key is ignored **silently**. Treat this arm as INCONCLUSIVE and, when repeating it, first establish that the runner acknowledged the setting (or disable parallelism a way that is observable, e.g. a checked-in `xunit.runner.json` or a collection attribute) before reading any verdict from it. **What did strengthen**: three area runs produced **8, 8 and 9** failures over an unchanged test set while the narrow filter produced **zero** — a varying count at fixed input is itself evidence of order sensitivity. **Unexplained and worth a glance**: the same area took **63 min on 2026-08-13 and 6 min on 2026-08-15**, same machine, same 620/617 tests |

Gated after the fact 2026-08-08: the gate returned **BLOCKED** because nothing drove `Blind` or `Failed`, closed in one round. **One thing still unverified**: the `[LabFact]` replay has not run under the now-required `OVERFIT_REPLAY_START_UTC` |
| `MS-4` | **DONE** | live-loop regression guard: 4 tests drive `ExecuteAsync` itself, which nothing did. Pinned — the loop keeps cycling, a source that throws every time does **not** end it, stopping ends it, and stopping returns before the next tick. Two mutations, both caught. **Two harness findings worth more than the tests**: (1) an unbounded `await StopAsync` HUNG instead of failing under the regression it exists to catch — a hang has no message and blocks everything behind it, so every wait is bounded now; (2) killing the harness mid-mutation left the source mutated, and the next run read that as its baseline and reported *restore verified* against it — the harness now refuses to start unless the target is clean against HEAD |

## PSI channel — `PS-`

`ANALYSIS_READY`. Four questions open; the recommendation is config binding first, enum member after the
floor is measured.

| id | status | task |
|---|---|---|
| `PS-1` | **DONE — DECIDED 2026-08-11 — stays a custom channel, no `MetricIndex` member** | decide the vehicle: `CustomMetricBinding` now, `MetricIndex` member later | The question was overtaken by its own siblings: `PS-2` and `PS-3` shipped `CpuPressure` as a custom binding and verified it end to end, with the deployed guard opening incident 132 by itself. So this is no longer "which vehicle" but "do we promote later", and the answer is no. **The reason usually given for not promoting is wrong and worth retiring**: `MetricIndex` has 13 members while `MetricSnapshot.FeatureCount` is 12, and that constant is the autoencoder's contract (`12 x 4 = 48` inputs, "changing this requires rebuilding the weights"). The two are already decoupled, so a fourteenth member would not touch the learned models. **The real cost is measured, on the running guard today**: `ErrorRate` is a `MetricIndex` member with no binding in the lab config, and every single cycle reports `blind=1` because of it — permanently, on a cluster where nothing is wrong. PSI needs cgroup v2 and kernel support and is not universal, so promoting `CpuPressure` would hand that same permanent `blind` to every cluster that cannot supply it, degrading the guard's own coverage self-report (`GuardCycleResult.IsPartiallyBlind`) as a side effect of adding a channel most deployments would never see. A custom binding is absent when unconfigured; an enum member is missing. **What would change the decision**: a way to say "not applicable on this cluster" that is distinct from "blind" — worth having independently, since `ErrorRate` is in exactly that state and has been for months. **One asymmetry this leaves, found while deciding**: custom channels have no CONFIGURED step floor. `ConfiguredFloorSource.MinAbsoluteLevelShift(string)` goes straight to the calibrator, and `CustomMetricBinding` carries `MinGap`, `MinTrendChange` and `MinGapChange` but no step. That is the one genuine argument for promotion, and the honest answer to it is to add `minStepChange` to custom bindings — the symmetric completion of `AN-D4b` — rather than to move the channel |
| `PS-2` | **DONE** | CPU pressure bound as the custom channel `CpuPressure` (`container_pressure_cpu_waiting_seconds_total`, Counter, LoadIndependent, Infrastructure, floor 0.00004). All four criteria, written down before measuring: 12 PSI series on the watched pods; the binding read back out of the deployed ConfigMap; **the guard evaluates it — `blind=0`**, which is the check that matters because the throttle channel had series in Prometheus and no query for hours; and quiet on healthy — **0 rows in a replay of a clean window**. `waiting` not `stalled`: measured side by side at 0.48682 vs 0.48681 under starvation, so for a single-container cgroup they are the same event and binding both would duplicate one signal. Separation healthy-to-starved is **16485x**, the sharpest of any channel here, with peers under 0.00005 throughout. The absolute rule is `PS-3` and deliberately not armed yet |
| `PS-3` | **DONE** | absolute rule armed on `CpuPressure`: threshold **0.01**, breach fraction **0.25**. Both margins measured, not chosen — healthy peak across twelve pods **0.00005** (200x below) and starved minimum sample **0.167** (16x above); three orders of magnitude of empty space, so the exact value inside it is not load-bearing. Breach fraction follows `ForCpuThrottling`'s measured shape: persistence, not height, separates a starved pod from a busy one. **Both arms, and the live guard as the third witness**: healthy window 21:52Z — 0 pods over the gate raw, 0 `CpuPressure` rows in replay; starved window 22:14Z — one pod at 88% of the window raw, `CpuPressure=15` rows in replay, peers **0 of 11**; and the deployed guard opened incident 132 by itself, logging *"CpuPressure [Infrastructure] severity 0.467 — Held at or above 0.01 for 47 % of the window"* next to `CpuThrottleRatio` on the same pod. **Silence risk**: the rule needs 20 samples (`AnomalyGuardConfigReader.BuildRule`, `MinimumSamples: 20`) and `RecentSamples` derives that count as `ceil(RecentWindow / window.Step)` — at the deployed 15-minute recent window this holds only while the window step stays at or under 45 s. Raise the step and the channel goes permanently `WarmingUp`, which reads exactly like a quiet cluster. **This is not hypothetical: it is what happened to the first two attempts at this task** — a 2-cycle replay could not reach a verdict, and its silence was recorded first as a detector failure, then as a healthy negative arm |

## .NET runtime signals — `RS-`

`ANALYSIS_READY`. **None of the five instrument names exist in the repo**; exposing them needs
`MeterListener` code this repository has never written. Three spikes ordered first.

| id | status | task |
|---|---|---|
| `RS-1` | **DONE** | both halves settled. **Names**: confirmed live for both instruments, and two traps a name check cannot see — `http.server.active_requests` is PUSHED (deltas, must be summed) while `dotnet.monitor.lock_contentions` is **OBSERVABLE**, reporting nothing until `RecordObservableInstruments()` is called and giving an absolute value rather than a delta; it read a clean permanent **0** through sixteen contending threads before that was fixed. Callbacks need registering for `int` AND `long` or one on the wrong width is silently never invoked. **Overhead**: measured (`MeterListenerOverheadBenchmark`, `[SimpleJob]` because the shared `InvocationCount=1` would leave nanosecond work measuring timer noise). Per request pair **0.60 ns -> 7.00 ns**, ratio 11.62 (RatioSD 0.25); polling every observable costs **15.7 ns** and 72 B, once per fifteen-second scrape. **The ratio is alarming and the magnitude is nothing**: +6.4 ns against a ~40 ms request is 0.000016% of it, so the lab's baseline did not move and the floors calibrated before this change stand. Baseline is the same instrument with NO listener, not an empty method, so the change is not charged for the counter's own existence. **Stated limit**: the synthetic counter carries no tags and the real one does, so this is a lower bound — the conclusion survives a 100x underestimate |
| `RS-2` | **DONE** | bind `dotnet_gc_committed_bytes`. **The row was stale, not the work** — found 2026-08-09 by `overfit-analyst` while writing the metrics catalogue: the series is emitted (`Demo/LabWorkload/WorkloadMetrics.cs:178`) and bound as the custom channel `GcCommittedBytes` in both `k8s/anomaly-guard/guard.lab-workload.json` and the deployed ConfigMap. It was done and never written down, which is the same divergence recorded on 2026-08-08 in the other direction |
| `RS-3` | **DONE** | the hung-request blind spot is closed, and every link is measured. Workload exposes ASP.NET Core's own `http.server.active_requests` through a `MeterListener`; 12/12 pods report it; the guard binds it as a custom channel (`blind=1`, only `CpuThrottleRatio`). **Fires**: replaying the recorded stall window gives `ActiveRequests=5` rows. **And it leads latency by two minutes** — with requests held open the channel went 5 -> 40 while the pod's p95 still read 48.75, the healthy bucket-geometry value, because a request only enters the histogram when it FINISHES. When p95 finally moved it pinned to 30000 ms, the top bucket edge, so it carries no magnitude either: the latency family saturates at both ends (`AN-F2` is the other one) while the in-flight count is monotonic. Peers stayed at 0-1 throughout. **The scrape is subtracted at the source** — before that every pod read a flat 1.0 and `InertChannels()` would have called a working signal a conclusive defect. Floors measured, not chosen: at rest 0 on all twelve, ten-minute maxima 0/1/2, gate at 3 over 25% of the window |
| `RS-4` | **DONE 2026-08-10 — the open half is measured** | `LockContentions` deployed, bound, and separating hugely under fault (0.076 -> 11.6/s, **152x**, peers flat). Premise corrected in the plan: GC stays quiet but **CPU does not** (8x, 2.6x above peers), so this is the sharper channel rather than the only sighted one; likely a spin-before-block artefact of the fault, **not measured**. **New, and it is mine**: the floor is too tight. 0.064 came from a peak of 0.0514 x1.25 measured over only ~3 minutes on pods that had just started, and within the hour it produced a **false positive on an unfaulted pod** — *"Held at or above 0.064 for 35% of the window"* — in the same cycle as the `RS-5` verification. **Recalibrated the same day**: over a settled 20-minute window the healthy per-pod peak is **0.0952/s**, so healthy replicas routinely exceeded the old 0.064 and the false positive was inevitable. Floor now 0.119 (0.0952 x 1.25), still ~97x below the fault, deployed and read back from the cluster. **The lesson is the window, not the number** — three minutes of a freshly rolled population describes the rollout, not the workload. Remaining: reproduce contention that blocks without spinning, to settle whether CPU is genuinely blind to it **Settled by varying ONE variable, 2026-08-10.** The row asked whether CPU is genuinely blind to contention that blocks, and flagged its own 8x CPU reading as *likely a spin-before-block artefact, not measured*. Two arms of the same fault on one pod, 8 threads: **200 us hold -> CPU 0.9985 cores against peers' 0.0019 (the pod pinned at its whole quota), lock 10,530/s**; **50,000 us hold -> CPU 0.0023 against 0.0015, an absolute difference of 0.0008 cores — inside the 0.0014-0.0019 baseline scatter — while lock still ran 11.5/s against the 0.119 floor, 97x over**. So the spin explanation is CONFIRMED: 8 threads at 200 us fit inside `lock`'s spin budget and burn; at 50 ms the budget is exhausted, they block, and CPU goes quiet. The fault was designed correctly — *the critical section sleeps* — and what moved CPU was the runtime's lock, not the author's intent. **Consequence: `LockContentions` is the ONLY sighted channel for blocking contention, not merely the sharper one**, which is a stronger case for it than this row carried. **Method note worth keeping**: the run's own automated verdict called this wrong, because it gated on a RATIO (`1.5x`) of a quantity near zero. At 0.002 cores a 1.5x ratio is 0.0008 cores and means nothing — the same wrong-unit error as sizing a CFS limit from average cores. Gate on the absolute difference against measured baseline scatter |
| `RS-5` | **DONE** | `dotnet.exceptions` deployed, bound as `Exceptions`, and **fires**: *"Held at or above 0.0167 for 44% of the window (12 of 27 observations)"* on exactly the injected pod. **The one channel of the three where the plan's premise holds completely.** Over eight minutes with half of requests throwing-and-catching: exceptions 0.37 -> 0.99/s, **peers 0.000 throughout**, **5xx 0.000 throughout**, **p95 flat at 48.8-48.9 ms**, no response other than 200. `ErrorRate` cannot see this by construction — it counts 5xx RESPONSES and a caught exception never becomes one. Needed a new fault: `POST /fault/throw?rate=` throws and catches, unlike `/fault/errors` which returns a 500 without ever throwing. Floor derived differently because the healthy maximum is **exactly 0** on all twelve pods, which makes the usual `max x 1.25` say nothing: expressed as exceptions per rate window instead — one exception in 2 minutes is 0.0083/s, so the floor sits at two, 0.0167/s, about 56x below the injected signal |
| `RS-6` | **DONE — REFUTED as specified** | `kestrel.queued_connections` / `rejected_connections` cannot be delivered by a pull exporter on the pod they describe, and the reason is structural rather than an implementation problem. Both instruments publish (5 of 5 subscribed). But Kestrel only queues or rejects when it is AT a concurrency limit, so **without `MaxConcurrentConnections` both series are permanently zero** — and **with** it, saturation blocks the `/metrics` scrape too. Measured 2026-08-09 with the limit as the only variable: no limit, 40 holders -> **6/6 scrapes succeed**, queued always 0; limit 8, same 40 holders -> **0/6 scrapes**, nothing observable at all. A channel that goes dark exactly when it has something to report is worse than no channel. Instruments are left exposed (they cost nothing and read 0) but **not bound**, and no limit is set on the lab. Redirect: `AN-D5x` |

That is exactly the entry that shipped. Reading that enum first also corrected my diagnosis: `MetricMap.Build` is right and my config was wrong, which is not what the `XC-8` row assumed. An explicit `query` exempts an entry, because hand-written PromQL is a deliberate act a reviewer can read. | `SEC-1` | **DONE — durable fix shipped; two manifest gaps split out** | **`POST /ack` now requires a bearer token and FAILS CLOSED without one.** It suppresses a finding for a caller-chosen duration, so an unauthenticated one is worse than no endpoint: the guard keeps running, keeps looking healthy, and reports nothing. Secret comes from `OVERFIT_GUARD_ACK_TOKEN` (declared in `OverfitEnvironment`, per `OVERFIT024`), read once at startup; unset means **503 with the reason**, wrong token means 401 — deliberately different, because an operator holding a valid token needs to know the guard was never given one rather than hunting for a mistake of their own. `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/suppressions` are untouched: the first two are the scrape, and the third is the transparency guarantee — a mute nobody can enumerate is indistinguishable from a detector that stopped working. **The decision was extracted to `GuardAckAuthorization` in `Sources/Anomalies` for a specific reason**: `GuardMetricsEndpoint` lives in `Sources/Cli`, which the test project does not reference and which exposes no internals, so the code deciding whether a stranger may silence an incident had no coverage and no way to get any. 14 tests; **three mutations, three caught** — fail-open on an unset secret, `StartsWith` instead of constant-time equality (the one a reviewer misses: it passes every obvious test and accepts any token beginning with the secret), and a case-insensitive secret. Comparison is `CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals`, because a short-circuiting compare leaks the prefix to anyone who can time the response. Manifest carries the env var from a Secret with `optional: true` — required would stop the guard starting over a feature most deployments never use, trading a detector outage for a convenience — validated with `--dry-run=client`, **not applied** while the `AN-D1` calibration window is open. The `NetworkPolicy` stays and stays inert on this cluster; it is defence in depth for a customer with Calico/Cilium and was never the control. **Split out, still open**: no `automountServiceAccountToken: false` and no `securityContext` on the guard Deployment — see `XC-12` |

Fixed by mirroring `RunTrend`'s per-pod `IsWarmingUp` skip. Two tests: the young pod stays quiet, and **a control asserting the identical climb on an established pod is still caught** — without it the first would pass on a fixture that reported nothing at all. One mutation: removing the gate turns the young-pod test red and leaves the control green. Suite 2305/0/271. **Also closes an `AN-D9` limitation**: its persistence gate covers `RunCustomPeer` only, so a coverage RAMP was reported by the trend family on the first cycle — and what produces a ramp is a pod being replaced, which is exactly the case the grace exists to absorb |

The built-in half was already right and its own comment argues the case: *"the names are the whole actionable part, and they are cheap because the window already knows."* Fixed by walking `_options.Guard.CustomMetrics` after the built-in loop, using the existing `MetricWindow.PodsReporting(string)` overload. **No unbound case to skip**, unlike the built-ins: a custom channel exists BECAUSE somebody wrote a binding, so every silent one is actionable by definition — the built-in check exists only because the enum carries members no deployment has bound. Two tests, and the second is what makes the first mean anything: a channel nobody reported is named, **and one every pod reported is not** — without that control the positive would pass on a guard that named everything every cycle. One mutation: removing the loop turns the positive red and leaves the control green. Suite 2307/0/271 |

So an `emptyDir` at /tmp is exactly the required surface and it holds nothing worth keeping. Checked in both directions after applying: `touch /probe-readonly` -> "Read-only file system", /tmp alive, /state written by a real cycle. `runAsNonRoot`: **the row's premise was false** — a pod-level `runAsUser` overrides the image's user, so the root image was never the obstacle; applied at uid 1000 with zero restarts. The genuine hazard was the PVC, and it needed the capability check the lab discipline demands: /state is mode 777 so CREATING a file always works, while a non-root process reopening the existing root-owned 0644 files would fail **silently** — the guard keeps cycling and stops persisting the seasonal baseline and floor calibration, the failure mode that looks like success. Verified across a full cycle rather than at startup: incidents.json 470 -> 436 B and learned-state.txt 421,849 -> 423,050 B, both now ubuntu:ubuntu. `fsGroup` did NOT produce that — it left the directory root:root — the files are rewritten rather than updated in place, so they inherit the writer; fsGroup is kept because it is what makes a FRESH volume writable. The change in that session touched `Sources/Anomalies` logging only — a different assembly and a different concern — so it is not a regression from it. **Recorded rather than dismissed for the same reason as `XC-10`**: a zero-allocation assertion is a claim about the whole process, so anything that allocates on another thread during the measurement can break it, and xUnit runs classes in parallel. That is a mechanism, not a shrug. Third intermittent red of the day; the other two (`XC-10` and one `ConfigSurfaceCompletenessTests` case) were both traced to a tree being edited concurrently, which this one was not. The change in that session touched only `Sources/Anomalies` configuration reading — a different assembly from the failing test — so it is not a regression from it. **Why it is recorded rather than dismissed as flake:** the test's own name says the kernel path is *pinned*, which means it depends on state something else could unpin, and xUnit runs classes in parallel by default. That is a mechanism, not a shrug, and it is the same class as `TG-T12` (`CircuitBreakerTests.Timeout_FiresWhenWallTimeExceeded` failing when the box is loaded, also not on any known-flaky list). | `XC-15` | **DONE 2026-08-11** | the decision trace now covers TREND (built-in and custom) and RULE, beside the peer path it already had | **Two new records rather than one reused**: `PeerDecisionTrace` is shaped by the peer comparison (relative gap, absolute gap, effect size) and a trend has none of those, so `TrendDecisionTrace` carries slope, Kendall tau, autocorrelation and the change floor, and `RuleDecisionTrace` carries the threshold, the breach fraction and the usable-sample count — two gates that call for opposite fixes. Both surface the detector's own `Reason` sentence, which already existed on `TrendResult` and `SustainedThresholdResult` and simply never reached a log. **The row worth building it for is `warmingUp=True`**: a pod inside the grace was skipped with a bare `continue` and left no evidence, so "tested and healthy" and "never tested" were the same silence — and during a rollout that is every pod at once. | `XC-16` | **DONE 2026-08-11** | a finding on an ONGOING incident left no line in the log | Two separate defects in `LoggerIncidentSink`, both quoted from the code before changing it. **(1)** Line 144 sent every finding of an already-open incident to `continue`. Negative arm: with Debug removed, 0 such lines while the guard kept cycling, and the running pod's imageID was compared against the freshly built one (`e0d5e842d88b`, equal) so the silence is the threshold and not a stale binary. Debug was removed afterwards — it is an operator action, not a deployed state, and the repo manifest is unchanged so no config drift was left behind |
| `XC-17` | OPEN | the measurement guard's refusal is unreadable through `dotnet test` after the xunit v3 migration | Measured 2026-08-11 by holding `Global\DevOnBike.Overfit.MachineMeasurement` externally and running both ways. **The protection is intact** — the suite does not run. The DIAGNOSIS is not. Running the built exe directly (v3 makes the test project an executable) is exactly right: exit code **2** and the full sentence. Through `dotnet test`, the VSTest bridge launches that exe as a child and speaks JSON to it, so `Environment.Exit(2)` lands inside the bridge's first probe and the operator gets `Catastrophic failure: Test process did not return valid JSON (non-object)` plus "no tests available", with exit code **1**. Two consequences: a script keying on exit code 2 is broken, and a human reads something unintelligible instead of the sentence written for them. Candidate fixes, none free: report a single synthetic failing test instead of exiting (legible AND non-zero, but needs a custom discoverer); take the mutex in a hand-written entry point with `XunitAutoGeneratedEntryPoint=false` (same protocol problem, but cleaner); or document `dotnet run --project Tests` as the supported way to see the reason. **Do not fix it by removing the refusal** — the file records that an exception here was once swallowed by the runner, which then ran the whole suite green **MEASURED 2026-08-11, and it retires a stale premise.** `MeasurementExclusion` uses `Environment.Exit(2)` because a 2026-08-03 measurement on xunit **2.9.3** showed an exception from the framework constructor was CAUGHT by the runner, which then fell back to the default framework and ran the whole suite green. Re-measured on v3 by mutating `Exit` to `throw` while holding the mutex: **v3 does NOT swallow it** — the suite did not run. So the original reason for `Exit` no longer applies. **But `throw` is still not the fix**: the message dies either way, because the child process's stdout must be JSON and the bridge reports its own `Catastrophic failure: Test process did not return valid JSON` instead. Both arms produce "no tests available" and exit 1. So the remaining options are unchanged — a synthetic failing test, a hand-written entry point, or documenting the direct exe run — and the cheapest real improvement is a marker file the refusal writes, since a script can then read the reason even though a human cannot see it in the console |
| `XC-18` | **DONE 2026-08-11** | xunit v3's analyzers flag 77 pre-existing test-quality issues | Split by what each rule is actually worth, not swept uniformly. **`xUnit1031` (7 sites, blocking on a task inside a test — the real deadlock risk): 5 fixed, 2 suppressed per site with a reason.** The five were `GetAwaiter().GetResult()` on HTTP calls in the redaction-gateway tests; their test methods are now `async Task` and their `WaitForHealth` poller is async with `Task.Delay` instead of `Thread.Sleep`. The two in `OverfitResourcePoolTests` are NOT a defect and were not "fixed": `Assert.False(waiter.Wait(200))` is the only way to state "this waiter is still parked because no slot is free", and rewriting it as an await would assert that the waiter eventually completes — which it does whether or not the pool blocks at all, so the test would pass with the behaviour removed. **`xUnit1012` fixed**: the theory parameter is genuinely nullable, since `LongFact` carries an optional runtime string, so the signature was wrong rather than the data. **`xUnit1051` (69 sites, 13 files) suppressed project-wide with a reason and a trigger**, not swept: the rule's benefit scales with how long a test runs and this suite is 22 seconds by policy, with everything long behind `[LongFact]`. Sixty-nine mechanical edits to make a 22-second run more interruptible is a poor trade — and leaving 69 permanent warnings is worse than either, because a warning nobody can act on is how build output becomes wallpaper (the same reasoning as the prerelease accept-list in `Directory.Build.targets`). **Revisit trigger written into the csproj**: if a test HANGS rather than fails. `XC-10`, `XC-13` and `XC-19` are three non-reproducing failures already on record, and if that class turns out to be tests that stop responding, cancellation responsiveness is exactly the lever this suppression would be hiding. Build now emits **zero** xUnit warnings; suite 2450/0/273 |
| `XC-19` | OPEN | `Gpt1QLoRATests.QLoRA_Q8Base_AllLinear_HeadFFNAttention_ReduceLoss` failed once and did not reproduce | 2026-08-11, in the first full-suite run after the xunit v3 migration and the XC-6 work. Did not reproduce: **2 further full-suite runs and 2 isolated runs, all green**. Evidence for the next reader rather than a shrug. The test IS seeded — `seed: 7` for the model, `seed: 99` for `FineTune` — and yet consecutive isolated runs reported **5.471 -> 3.544** and **5.019 -> 3.050**, so the non-determinism is not in the seeds; the likely source is parallel floating-point reduction during training, where the partitioning differs run to run and float addition is not associative. The assertion is `last < first - 0.5f`, an ABSOLUTE margin against a loss that starts near 5 and falls by about 2, so it normally has ample room and a failure means the run trained far less than usual. **Not captured**: the actual failure message — the run was filtered to the summary and the detail is gone, which is itself the lesson. Same shape as `XC-10` and `XC-13`, and the three together are now enough to ask whether the suite has a class of load-sensitive numerical tests rather than three unrelated flakes. Cannot be attributed to xunit v3 either way: earlier v3 full runs were green |

Tests 9 → 17; `AnalyzerHarness` needed `System.Console.dll` in its reference set, without which every new negative would have been vacuously green. Suite **2578/0/273**, verified independently by the main session, not only reported. **Scope limit, stated rather than hidden**: the census only ever saw the **26 projects in `Overfit.sln`**, and 8 `.csproj` on disk are outside it — one of them, `Demo/VoiceClone`, carries an OVERFIT040 pragma whose "0 sites" therefore means NOT MEASURED, not void, so it was left alone. | `XC-26` | OPEN | the biggest held request thread in the server is invisible to the rule that exists to find it | `OverfitInferenceService.CompleteChat` blocks a request thread for **up to 30 seconds** — `RentTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)` into `_pool.TryRent(RentTimeout, cancellationToken, out lease)`. So the analyzer is silent precisely where the worst case is — the familiar shape, arriving through the tool meant to find it.
