Text written at `OverfitResourcePool.cs:98-134` (header now records a DECISION, the old "filed as XC-26, which is where the choice belongs" deferral removed), above `CompleteChat`, and on `IOpenAiInferenceService`. **No performance claim was written**: the plan's proposed "cannot raise chat throughput" was dropped as UNVERIFIED by its own §6, and replaced with the structural facts plus the sentence that any statement about what an async rent would gain belongs to a measurement. **Three corrections came out of writing it.** (1) The plan cited `Cli/Commands.cs:256` for the default pool size; `:256` is a `return 1` in a model-not-found branch — the real sites are `Program.cs:96-101` and `Commands.cs:245`, construction at `:289`. Fed back into `overfit-architect`'s definition: cite the symbol and quote the text, the line is a hint. (2) The block claimed the 30 s rent was *"the largest held thread in the server"* — it is the largest wait **before any work starts**; the same thread is then held for the generation, unbounded. Left as a marked `CORRECTION`, not a silent rewrite. (3) `:132` said the analyzer is silent at *"the more expensive of the two sites"* — unsupported, since it is the same block one frame up; corrected by the main session to what is true and worse: **the rule is silent exactly where a fix would have to be made** (`CompleteChat` is what would become task-returning) and loud where the wait cannot be fixed alone. **One open question the architect left was answered while writing**: `ChatCompletionExchange.Handle` is `public static void` with no `async` in the file, so the generation genuinely runs on the calling thread — previously only asserted by a neighbouring comment. Still unmeasured, as §6 says: no load test, no benchmark, nothing about thread starvation **SIGNED 2026-08-12 — option (b): accept the block, write the constraint at the site. `TryRentAsync` deferred, not refused.** Plan: `docs/specs/xc-26-blocking-rent-plan.md`. **The reason is scope and irreversibility, and explicitly NOT allocation or AOT** — that is stated because rejecting it on those grounds would be a wrong reason for a defensible answer: `SemaphoreSlim.WaitAsync` completes synchronously when a slot is free, a `ValueTask<Lease?>` returning a synchronously-available result allocates nothing, the state machine boxes only when the wait actually suspends (i.e. when we were already about to wait seconds), rent happens once per REQUEST not per token, and async state machines touch none of the six banned symbols. What kills (a) is that **an async rent buys back only the queue, never the generation**: `AspNetResponseSink.cs:28-30` already records that the request thread that entered the endpoint is held for the whole generation by design, so throughput stays bounded by `pool.Size` either way — an unmeasured benefit against a permanent cost, since `out lease` cannot cross an `await`, so the shipped library would gain **its first async primitive** and `IOpenAiInferenceService.CompleteChat` (`void`) would have to become task-returning. Public API cannot be withdrawn; deciding not to add it can be reversed. Blast radius resolved rather than read: **15 references to `TryRent`, exactly ONE production caller** (`OverfitInferenceService.cs:92`), one `<see cref>`, 13 test sites that are synchronous by design. Default pool size is **1** (`Cli/Commands.cs:256`), so at any concurrency above one every request but one queues here for up to 30 s. **Two statements in the reasoning are marked UNVERIFIED and deferred to `overfit-perf-claim-auditor`**: that an async rent cannot improve chat throughput, and that threads parked in `TryRent` can starve unrelated endpoints — plausible, never observed on this server, and precisely why (a) is deferred rather than taken. **The recommendation that outlives the decision**: `HasAsyncSibling` can only ever see a blocking API the BCL has already paired, so **every first-party blocking primitive this repository writes is invisible to OVERFIT040 forever, and its callers with it** — `TryRent` is the first instance, not a special case. Proposed as an `[OverfitBlocking("…")]` attribute treated as equivalent to "has an async sibling", with two honest weaknesses named: OVERFIT040 is `suggestion` globally and a build does not print suggestions, so the report would land where nobody looks without its own id and severity; and the attribute is public API in `Main`, so it needs an ADR and the standing analyzer contract. Filed as `XC-29`. Four blocking questions remain open in the plan, the sharpest being whether concurrent multi-user serving is in scope at all — if it is not, (a) should be closed permanently rather than deferred. **Not run**: no build, no test, no benchmark, no load test; the hypothetical async shape was never AOT-published; `ChatCompletionExchange.Handle` was not read, so the thread-held-for-generation claim rests on `AspNetResponseSink`'s own comment plus `EnableSynchronousIO` |
| `XC-28` | OPEN | **the pool's mean queue wait excludes every long wait by construction, and is then dropped before anyone could read it** | Found 2026-08-12 by the `XC-26` architect and **verified independently in the source before filing**, because it is the kind of claim that must not be relayed. Two defects, one on each side of the boundary. (1) `OverfitResourcePool.TryRent` adds to `_waitTicksTotal` at `:130`, which is **after** the timeout path has already returned at `:127` — so a request that waited the full 30 s and was rejected contributes **zero ticks and zero to the denominator**, and a cancelled wait throws and contributes nothing either. The mean at `:77-79` is `waitTicks / _totalRented`, i.e. successful rents only, diluted by uncontended near-zero rents. **An operator therefore reads a healthy mean wait on a server that is timing out** — the subsystem's own silence-reads-as-health shape, arriving through a metric rather than a detector. (2) `PoolMetrics.MeanQueueWaitMs` is computed and then **dropped at the boundary**: `Server.AspNet/Services/PoolStatus.cs:12` carries `Size, Active, Available, RejectedTotal, PeakActive` and no wait at all, so nothing exports it. **The one quantity that would settle `XC-26` is measured, corrupted, and thrown away.** Fixing (1) is not simply moving the `Add` above the early return — a rejected wait and a successful one are different quantities, and averaging them together hides the rejections a second time; the honest shape is to account for them separately (waited-and-got-it versus waited-and-gave-up) and to say which is which at the metric's name. Any threshold reasoned from today's number is void |
| `XC-29` | OPEN | first-party blocking primitives are invisible to `OVERFIT040` by construction, and always will be | Raised by the `XC-26` architect, 2026-08-12. `SynchronousIslandAnalyzer.HasAsyncSibling` resolves `{Name}Async` on the called method's own containing type, so the rule can only see a blocking API the **BCL** has already paired with an async twin. Everything this repository writes itself — `OverfitResourcePool.TryRent` is the first case — is permanently outside its reach, **and so is every caller of it**, which is where the cost actually lands: `OverfitInferenceService.CompleteChat` holds a request thread for up to 30 s and the rule is silent. Proposed shape: an `[OverfitBlocking("reason")]` attribute in `Main`, alongside `OverfitHotPathAttribute`, that `HasAsyncSibling` treats as equivalent to having an async sibling; precedent for an attribute in `Main` driving analyzer behaviour already exists in `OverfitPerfAnalysis`. **Two weaknesses to solve rather than discover**: OVERFIT040 sits at `suggestion` globally and a build does not print suggestions, so a new report needs its own diagnostic id and severity or it lands where nobody looks (`XC-25` measured exactly this trap); and the attribute is public API, so it takes an ADR plus the standing analyzer contract — release-tracking entry, `.editorconfig` severity, and a test, since a rule with no test has only been shown not to fire on clean code. Related: `XC-26` (the decision this came out of), `XC-25` (the census method) |
| `XC-30` | **DONE 2026-08-13** | the delivery pipeline could record a plan as reviewed with no durable trace that anything was verified | From the user's review of `.claude/**` on 2026-08-13. **`overfit-delivery` carried TWO status sequences** — one without `VERIFIED`, one with it — so a coordinator following the first could write `REVIEWED` into a plan whose evidence stage never ran, and the result would be indistinguishable from one that passed. Fixed to a single sequence with `GATES_PASSED` added, plus the rule that the sequence is defined once and never restated (the duplicate is what drifted). **The scalar status was the deeper problem and is now backed by a gate manifest**: `verifier / reviewer / mutation-proof / performance / security / leak-scan / AOT / API-compatibility / release-readiness`, each `PASS | FAIL | INCONCLUSIVE | NOT_REQUIRED`, with **`NOT_REQUIRED` required to carry its reason on the same line** and a missing line meaning "nobody asked", never "not needed". `Scripts/plan_gate_check.py` reads and enforces it. **`overfit-leak-scan` was a seatbelt not bolted to the car** — the agent existed, told you to run it before any push, and no pipeline step named it; now a conditional PR gate and unconditional at release. **`overfit-assertion-quality` folded in as the verifier's technique** rather than a parallel process that would produce a second opinion nobody adjudicates, and the verifier now reports `mutation proof: PASS / NOT_REQUIRED / MISSING` explicitly, with five named conditions under which `MISSING` blocks. **One claim in the review was refuted**: `overfit-verifier` already knew `overfit-mutate` (`:152`, `:167`). **`Scripts/check_agent_definitions.py` added and wired into `/check`** — it self-tests against four seeded corruptions before trusting a clean run, because the incident it exists for (a `0x01` byte silently unregistering `overfit-developer` for two days) is invisible to every build and every test. **The checker's first run found real debt, recorded rather than papered over**: three plans written this week carry no `STATUS:` line at all and were therefore invisible to the stalled-plan check (fixed at source — the architect must now write it and seed the manifest), the architect's `SIGNED` and the pipeline's `APPROVED` had drifted apart (accepted as an alias, since a checker that flags a synonym gets ignored), and **no verifier or reviewer ran on `XC-22`, `XC-25` or `XC-26`** — a deliberate shortcut while working at pace, and exactly the shortcut the manifest exists to stop anyone from leaving unsaid |
| `XC-31` | **DONE 2026-08-13 — written; unproven until it issues its first verdict** | nobody holds an independent verdict on whether a detector's statistics mean what they are assumed to mean | Proposed by the user 2026-08-13 as `overfit-aiops-statistician`, read-only, triggered by any change to a detector, baseline, threshold, floor, calibration, anomaly score, peer comparison, seasonality, canary comparator or sample-size gate. **The gap is structural: `overfit-architect` today signs off on statistical methodology it also chose.** Four incidents say this is where the guard's real defects live, and every one of them was caught late and by accident: `AN-A1` — the acceptance criterion was undecidable by construction, since 11 events give a Poisson interval of 5.49-19.68 and one day can only ever demonstrate ≤3/day; `AN-F2` — a `+1.00` correlation with no artefact behind it, and the conclusion drawn from it did not follow anyway, because correlation is invariant to the intercept; `AN-D4b` — a floor measured correctly that described the wrong quantity; `RS-4` — an automated verdict gated on a RATIO of a quantity near zero, where 1.5x of 0.002 cores is 0.0008 and means nothing. The checklist the user drafted is the right scope: data semantics (gauge/counter/histogram/rate), stated assumptions, sample size, effect size, false-positive budget, cold start, missing data, seasonality, baseline contamination during a fault, and **the refuting observation**. Verdict vocabulary `STATISTICALLY_SOUND / BLOCKED / INCONCLUSIVE`. **It must not be allowed to become a rubber stamp**: like `overfit-perf-claim-auditor`, it owns its verdict and nobody else issues one **WRITTEN 2026-08-13** as `.claude/agents/overfit-aiops-statistician.md`, read-only, `model: opus`, verdicts `STATISTICALLY_SOUND / BLOCKED / INCONCLUSIVE` with `INCONCLUSIVE` declared first-class and never rounded to a neighbour. It carries the four incidents above **with their arithmetic**, because a checklist without them is a list of good intentions: the Poisson interval that made `AN-A1` undecidable, the intercept-invariance that voided `AN-F2`'s conclusion, the percentage-of-level that described the wrong quantity in `AN-D4b`, and the ratio-near-zero in `RS-4`. Ten checks in dependency order — data semantics, unit-of-the-mechanism, sample size and whether a verdict is reachable at all, effect size against resolving power, false-positive budget, cold start, missing data, seasonality and which reference, contamination, and **the refuting observation, whose absence is a `BLOCKED` on its own**. Two rules deliberately built in: a `BLOCKED` must carry the cheapest experiment that would lift it, and it must distinguish *the detector is wrong* from *the experiment could not decide* — different verdicts, different remedies, and conflating them has cost days here. It does **not** run the lab; reading recorded windows, fixtures and logs is its half, injecting and deploying is not. Registers cleanly (`Scripts/check_agent_definitions.py`: 12 definitions, 0 that would not register). **The honest status is that it is untested**: no verdict has been issued yet, and the role will only be shown to work when it blocks something a human then agrees should have been blocked. The first real chance is `AN-D1` blocker 1 — the uncalibrated floor — which is exactly the shape it was written for |
| `XC-32` | **DONE 2026-08-13** | a new metric or channel can be added before anyone has said what it is | Proposed 2026-08-13 as the skill `overfit-aiops-signal-contract`: name, instrument type, unit, PromQL, scope, expected labels, cardinality budget, aggregation, scrape cadence, retained window, minimum samples, **missing-data semantics**, reset semantics, healthy and anomalous expectation, whether the mechanism can reach a verdict at all, and what the refuting arm looks like — written **before** an analyst or architect marks a channel READY. **Four channels in this repository failed in four different ways that this contract would have caught at design time**: `OomEventsRate` bound to a series that reads 0 for a pod Kubernetes reported OOMKilled; `CpuThrottleRatio` rendered without `rate()` because a config entry overrode a correct template, making the trend family see a perfect climb every window; `ErrorRate` deployed as errors-per-second against a contract documenting a `[0,1]` fraction; `ScrapeCoverage` needing `Query` and `Calibrated` fields that did not exist. Every one presented as a working channel **WRITTEN 2026-08-13** as `.claude/skills/overfit-aiops-signal-contract/`. A fill-in contract block — instrument, unit, query, scope, labels and cardinality, aggregation, cadence, minimum samples, missing-data and reset semantics, healthy and anomalous expectation, **separation stated in the unit at the top**, verdict reachability and the refuting arm — with the instruction to write `UNKNOWN` rather than omit a line, because a blank is the failure the skill exists to prevent. **The four questions that kill a channel before it is written** are the part that earns its place: can the mechanism reach a verdict at all (the `MinimumSamplesPerPeer` = 30 arithmetic, and `RecentSamples` = `ceil(RecentWindow / step)` making a channel permanently `WarmingUp`); **can it report while the thing it detects is happening** (`RS-6`, measured with the limit as the only variable — no limit 6/6 scrapes and a permanently-zero counter, with the limit **0/6 scrapes and nothing observable at all**); does absence read as health; and does the environment actually produce it (`CpuThrottleRatio` had 0 series for weeks because CFS accounting only exists on a container with a quota). Also settles where a channel should live — **custom binding by default**, because an unbound enum member reports `blind` every cycle for ever, while retiring the wrong reason usually given for it (`MetricIndex` 13 vs `FeatureCount` 12 are already decoupled) — and records two one-line traps: do not bind two names for one event (`waiting` vs `stalled` measured at 0.48682 vs 0.48681 under starvation), and a configured binding overrides a correct built-in template, which is how two of the four broke |
| `XC-33` | **DONE 2026-08-13** | replaying a real incident and scoring the whole detector is not a standard procedure | Proposed 2026-08-13 as `overfit-aiops-replay`, reporting: incident detected, detection delay, first useful verdict, false positives before the incident, status after it, whether the detector returned to healthy, how many `WarmingUp`/`InsufficientData` cycles, whether the affected scope was right, rollout correlation, and old-versus-new ReplicaSet distinguished. **Most of the machinery already exists and this is wiring, not building**: `MS-3`'s replay driver with its discriminated `GuardCycleOutcome`, `LabWindowValidator` and the per-channel fixture gate from `AN-F5`. Note `MS-3`'s own open item — the `[LabFact]` replay has never run under the now-required `OVERFIT_REPLAY_START_UTC` — which this task would close as a side effect **WRITTEN 2026-08-13** as `.claude/skills/overfit-aiops-replay/`. Nine reported lines, each attached to the incident that put it there: **detection delay** (`AN-C1` — two channels on one fault, 1 cycle against 30 minutes), **false positives before onset** (a detector that fires late after an hour of noise has not detected anything), **`WarmingUp`/`InsufficientData` counts as a silence audit** (`PS-3` — a window step over 45 s makes a rule permanently `WarmingUp`, which reads exactly like a quiet cluster), **scope** (`AN-D3` — a fleet-wide step must name the workload with `NamesAPod` false), and **old-vs-new ReplicaSet** (median 1 finding against 14, the control that made the original table mean anything). Rests on the measured fact that replay is faithful — `AN-A1`'s window reproduced all 11 incidents in the same 11 cycles, 288 cycles in 4.8-5.9 s — and on `AN-F1`'s 11-against-33 as the demonstration that **a configuration change gets a number instead of an opinion**. Two rules that stop a replay lying: **read the returned decisions, never the log**, because the guard prints finding lines only when an incident changes state (`XC-16`); and **the recording must be admissible first** — per-channel validator verdicts, and a window flat in the quantity being judged rather than merely free of injected faults. Closes with what a replay **cannot** do: it cannot prove the chain (a channel bound to a dead series replays perfectly and detects nothing — `AN-D8`), cannot discover a fault shape nobody recorded (`AN-F4`), and does not rule on whether its own numbers mean what they appear to — that is `XC-31`'s verdict |

Names the surface by family (GGUF, safetensors, hand-rolled ONNX protobuf and its `.data` sidecars, tokenizer metadata, WAV/MP3, HTTP/Prometheus/Kubernetes JSON), requires the fix to be mutation-proven afterwards, and carries `overfit-ciso`'s embargo: an unfixed parser vulnerability is never disclosed publicly |

| `XC-44` | **DONE 2026-08-13 — the answer is "no candidates", and the coverage gap beside it is the real finding** | should more of the tree move from `StringBuilder` to `ValueStringBuilder`? | **Inventory: 71 constructions across `Sources/**`** (Main 37, Anomalies 21, Server.AspNet 5, Cli 3, Mcp 3, Server 1, Analyzers 1), ~47 per-call and ~24 one-shot. **Zero migration candidates**, because the type's two criteria are conjunctive — a string must really be produced AND the build must take several growth steps — and the population fails one or the other: the one-shot half outright, and most per-call sites because they **pre-size to the answer** (`input.Length`, 96, 128, 2048) and take 0-1 growths. Numbers in `docs/measured-baselines.md`; the comment is corrected in place. **The finding worth more than the migration question: for the whole per-call `StringBuilder` population, the intersection of "a rule can see it" and "the rule is loud" is EMPTY.** `OVERFIT010` sees it (name + `System.Text`) and is `suggestion` **everywhere**, with no directory override anywhere in `.editorconfig` and an entry in `WarningsNotAsErrors`; `OVERFIT006` is escalated to `warning` in `Kernels/`, `Intrinsics/` and `Ops/` but **structurally cannot see a `StringBuilder`** — it registers only on `InterpolatedString` and `Binary` — and **none of the 71 sites live in those three directories** in any case. So all 71 are invisible to every build and to CI, which is a decision nobody has made. **One `XC-29`-family site found in passing and it is not fixable with this type**: `StopSequenceDetector.cs:74-75` calls `_buffer.ToString()` **per generated token** on the chat decode path, growing with the reply — invisible to `OVERFIT010` (reused field, correctly exempted) and to `OVERFIT006` (not interpolation), and **structurally impossible** to move to `ValueStringBuilder`, because a `ref struct` cannot be a class field. Same shape per SSE chunk at `StreamingResponseScanner.cs:29` and `StreamingRestorer.cs:22`. **Method limit stated by the author**: ~30 of the 71 sites were opened and read; the rest were classified from the enclosing member and the capacity argument, and the analyzer's own list (`-p:ReportAnalyzer=true`) was not obtained **REOPENED THE SAME DAY BY ITS OWN MEASUREMENT — filed as `XC-45`.** The inventory rejected most per-call sites because they **pre-size to the answer** and therefore take 0-1 growth steps, applying the type's stated second criterion ("several growth steps"). **The benchmark refutes that criterion**: the advantage at zero growths (0.42, 0.50) is *larger* than at six (0.63-0.67). So those sites are candidates again. Full write-up with both tables, the refuted prediction and the caveats: `docs/valuestringbuilder-vs-stringbuilder.md` |
| `XC-45` | OPEN — **a second pass with a narrower question, not a migration** | the sites rejected for "does not grow enough" are candidates again, but the win has to be visible in the enclosing operation | Follows from `XC-44`'s own measurement, 2026-08-13. **Do not sweep.** The question is not "is `ValueStringBuilder` faster" — that is settled and it is, at every length measured — but **"which per-call, string-producing sites sit on a path where nanoseconds and bytes are visible at all"**. Two paths qualify on frequency: chat decode (per token) and the gateway request path. **The trap to avoid is the one the inventory already documented**: at several candidate sites the `StringBuilder` is not the dominant allocation — `Redactor` allocates a `List`, a `Dictionary` and one interpolated placeholder per matched span; `JsonLinesAuditSink` allocates two strings per category plus a `ToString("o")` and a `ToString` per int. **A 20 ns saving inside a 40 ms request is not a finding**, so the measurement must be of the enclosing operation, not of the builder. **Two structural blockers no measurement can move**: a `ref struct` cannot be a class field, which rules out every reused-buffer shape including `StopSequenceDetector`; and it cannot be captured by a lambda or local function, which rules out the `ChatSession.EmitToken` shape (reasoned from the language rule, **not compile-verified**). **Also still open from `XC-44`**: the analyzer's own site list was never obtained (`-p:ReportAnalyzer=true`), so 41 of the 71 classifications rest on a regex over the enclosing member rather than on `OVERFIT010` **MEASURED 2026-08-13, and the answer splits.** Redaction, measured as the **enclosing operation** rather than the builder: `Redactor.Redact` costs **1486.72 ns / 4168 B** at 2 matches and **8185.00 ns / 20064 B** at 16, while the string assembly inside it is 62.64 ns / 1672 B and 498.48 ns / 9504 B. So the builder is **4-6% of the time and 40-47% of the bytes**, and migrating it would save **0.8-2.8% of the operation's time and 21-25% of its allocations**. **That is a GC-pressure change, not a speed change**, and it must be described that way — "a quarter fewer bytes per request" is true, "a faster gateway" is not. **A second finding was raised and then REFUTED by reading the code, which is the part worth keeping.** The chat-detokenize benchmark shows superlinear allocation — 2.10 KB/token at 64 tokens against **6.62 KB/token at 256** — and `XC-44` had ranked `StopSequenceDetector` as its top worry, describing it as allocating a string per token *"growing with the reply"*. **Both readings are wrong for the shipped path.** `ChatSession` uses `IncrementalDetokenizer` and turns only the **delta** into a string (`ChatSession.cs:272`); its comment states the whole-reply-per-token shape is what that class replaced. And `StopSequenceDetector.Append` returns `piece` immediately when no stop sequence is configured (`:69-72`), and otherwise **clears its buffer every call and retains only the longest held suffix** (`:97-98`), which is bounded by the longest stop sequence — so the per-call string is `delta + bounded suffix`, **O(1) per token, not O(n)**. The benchmark measured the naive path its own method name names ("no zero-alloc decode"), which the product no longer uses in chat. **Had this been filed on the numbers alone it would have entered the registry as "quadratic allocation in chat decode" — a false alarm with a benchmark behind it.** |

So a script, a CI step or an agent that keys on the exit code **cannot distinguish a failed benchmark run from a real one** — and the failure mode is the worst kind: a green signal over an empty result. This is the same shape as `XC-17` (a refusal whose message the caller cannot see) arriving through the benchmark host instead of the test host. **Fix is small and belongs beside the mutex check already in `Program.cs`**: if BenchmarkDotNet reports zero executed benchmarks, exit non-zero with a distinct code — the host already owns exit 2 for "someone else is measuring", so a third code costs nothing and makes "nothing ran" legible. **Do not conflate it with a benchmark that legitimately has no matching filter**: `--filter` matching nothing is a user error and should say so, which is the same exit path but a different message **DONE 2026-08-14.** `ExitCodeFor(summaries, args)` in `Sources/Benchmark/Program.cs`: exit **0** when at least one report has measurements or the invocation was informational (`--list`/`--info`/`--help`/`--version`, which BenchmarkDotNet answers with zero summaries by design), otherwise a new **exit 3**, distinct from the existing 2 for "someone else is measuring". Four arms run against the built binary, including the real scenario — generated builds forced to fail, BDN printing `executed benchmarks: 0`, now rc **3** where the old unconditional `return 0` gave 0. **The developer refuted its own first version and that is the part worth keeping**: it asserted "No benchmark matched the filter" whenever summaries were empty, and `--cli` pointed at a nonexistent file produced the same empty result — so **the exit code was right and the message lied**, blaming the filter for a rejected command line. (3) *The mutation this row named as its own acceptance criterion* — reverting the CAS to a bare increment — **was run and did redden**, one of ten mutations in `XC-50`'s pass, all caught. The one worth remembering is that swapping the CAS for a non-atomic read-modify-write is **indistinguishable single-threaded** and was caught only by the concurrency test, which is precisely why this row insisted the assertion must not be weakened to make it pass. **What does NOT close with it.** The stress harness this row sketched — rapid back-to-back dispatches with two bodies and contexts, one path delayed — was **deliberately rejected** during `XC-50` on the `TG-T12`/`TG-T13` reasoning, and the client-level coverage it was reaching for now lives in `XC-52`(a) sequential and `XC-52`(b) soak. Anyone arriving here looking for dispatcher coverage should read `XC-52`, not resurrect this row. **The transferable part**: this row was written asking for a *test*, and what actually fixed it was a *structural change that made the test possible and the defect unrepresentable*. When a row says "X cannot be tested because everything is private", the first question is whether the boundary is in the right place, not how to reach through it |
| `XC-50` | **DONE — REVIEWED 2026-08-14 — implemented, `VERIFIED`, and reviewed with no correctness finding. Open items are follow-ups, not defects** | `_decodeChunkCount` is not generation-tagged while the claim word is | Found by `overfit-architect` on 2026-08-14 while signing the `XC-49` plan, hand-traced, and **verified independently by the main session against the working tree**. `ForDecode` writes `_decodeChunkCount = chunkCount` for the NEW dispatch (`OverfitParallel.cs:626`) **before** it rewrites the descriptors (`:629-639`) and **before** it republishes the claim word with the new generation (`:644`). In that window the claim word still carries generation `G` while the count already belongs to `G+1`. A straggler still draining `G` — every worker's drain loop ends with one `TryClaimDecodeChunk` that is expected to fail — passes the tag check (the word still says `G`), then compares its exhausted index against **`G+1`'s** count. **If the next dispatch has more chunks than the last, that comparison wrongly succeeds**, the CAS succeeds because the word is unchanged, and the worker executes a descriptor being overwritten and decrements `G+1`'s completion counter. Torn descriptor plus early completion — exactly the class the CAS was added to remove, in a narrower window. **The precondition is ordinary, not exotic**: `chunkCount = min(poolSize, totalWork)` and `totalWork` differs per dispatch, so attention over 9 heads followed by an FFN over 1536 outputs satisfies it. **The comment at `:641-643` asserts the opposite and is wrong** — it reasons about a worker that has SEEN the new generation; the hole belongs to one still using the old one. Fix the comment with the code. **Candidate fix, NOT a decision — it belongs to the architect**: publish the claim word for the new generation FIRST, before the count and the descriptors, so a straggler tagged `G` fails the tag check immediately; a worker cannot claim as `G+1` before `_decodeGen` is published, which still happens last. Whatever is chosen, the invariant to state and test is that **the exhaustion bound must be as generation-tagged as the claim itself**. **Route**: needs its own delivery pass; `XC-49`'s seam is invariant under any fix here, so that task proceeds unchanged and this one does not block it **IMPLEMENTED 2026-08-14, then `BLOCKED` by `overfit-verifier` — one green mutation, fix loop round 1 running.** Fix as signed: `(generation | count | index)` packed 32/16/16 into the single CAS word, `_decodeChunkCount` deleted, protocol moved to `Sources/Main/Runtime/DecodeChunkClaim.cs` as an `internal static` type that cannot reach the dispatcher's private fields — the invariant became a compile error rather than a review habit. Suite 2631/0/274. **Step R reproduced the defect as an executed fact** before any fix: `TryClaim returned true with index 4 (word 0x0000000700000005)`, the CAS firing on a straggler taking the next generation's chunk. **Four findings that outlive this row.** (1) **The blocking one**: `Publish` writes three fields and only two were pinned — mutating it to preserve the incoming index instead of zeroing it left all three tests GREEN, and `_decodeClaim` is a static word reused across dispatches that always carries the previous dispatch's exhausted index, so 'publish zeroes the index' is a live production requirement. Under that mutation the suite does not redden, it **hangs** on `ForDecode`'s untimed spin inside the lock; the orphan burned 2268 CPU-seconds over 37 minutes and then failed every build with `OVERFITMEASURING`. (2) **AC1's defect assertion has no independent oracle** and cannot get one: post-fix the defect state (one generation's tag with the next's bound) is *unrepresentable*, so every mutation reaching it also reddens AC2 or AC3. A green AC1 promises 'the publication sequence produces a state the claim refuses', not 'the claim refuses for a reason nothing else covers'. That is evidence the fix is structurally sound, not that the test is weak — but it bounds what the suite can promise. (3) **The lost-CAS retry branch is UNASSERTED, not uncovered — and the stronger claim was wrong.** The verifier reported it 'never taken' from a branch-rate of 0.833; that figure came from the three-test filter. Measured again on the **full suite: branch-rate 1.000 in 3 of 3 samples** — real `ForDecode` dispatches do lose the race, so the branch executes; nothing arranges it and no assertion depends on it. The developer caught this before shipping the false sentence into a source comment. **The transferable rule: a coverage figure without its arm is not evidence**, and on a concurrent path 'unasserted' and 'uncovered' are different claims — a branch reached only by a race reads as covered under the full suite and partial under a filter. The plan rejected a stress harness (`TG-T12`/`TG-T13` reasoning), so this stays unasserted deliberately, with the note that the plausible regression there — hoisting the `Volatile.Read` out of the loop — presents as a **livelock**, the same silent shape as the finding above. (4) **The plan's §8 mutation table is wrong in two rows**: M1 does not drive AC1's defect assertion (it breaks `Publish` for both publishes, so the tag is never written and AC1 dies at its capability guard) and it reddens AC2 as well; M3's 'AC3 only' is also too narrow. Corrected here rather than left to be re-derived. **One deliberate assertion change, reviewed and kept**: `Assert.Equal(4, drained)` became a range check so AC1 is not sensitive to the count (AC2's subject). The verifier re-ran both count mutations and confirmed AC2 alone catches each, so the old assertion bought a redundant data point and cost victim isolation; the guard that replaced it is load-bearing, because without it one mutation leaves AC1 green and vacuous |

M5 (the completion decrement removed) is the one the plan predicted as *"victim: nobody, outcome: hung"* — it is now a named red. **(c) WAS NOT OPEN and this row is why the main session thought it was**: the signed plan decided it on 2026-08-14 — no progress deadline, on **memory safety** rather than any threshold, because every caller's `context` is a stack local inside a `fixed` block and returning early pops that frame while a worker may still write through those pointers. The paragraph above still asks the question. **A decision lives in the signed plan; this registry carries status. This is not hypothetical on this repository: the decode dispatcher's completion wait is an untimed spin **inside a lock**, so a liveness defect on that path hangs rather than reddens; during `XC-50` a single mutation produced an orphaned test host that burned **2268 CPU-seconds over 37 minutes** and then failed every subsequent build with `OVERFITMEASURING`, a message about a concurrent measurement that did not exist. The mutation harness now passes `--blame-hang --blame-hang-timeout` for exactly this reason; an ordinary CI run does not. **Fix is a runner flag, not hot-path semantics** — `--blame-hang --blame-hang-timeout <n>m` plus `timeout-minutes` on the job — and it is deliberately NOT the same decision as `XC-52`(c), which asks whether the production spin should carry a progress deadline that throws. This row makes the failure legible; that one makes it impossible **DELIVERED 2026-08-17, and BOTH things I assumed about `--blame-hang` were wrong.** **(1) The bound is an INACTIVITY timer on the test host, not a per-test limit.** I measured the slowest individual test (19.92 s in a full run) and set a 5 s bound expecting it to fire; **it did not**, and the collector said *"All tests finished running, Sequence file will not be generated"* — 2689 tests across xunit's parallel collections keep completing, so nothing ever starves the timer. So the per-test maximum is **the wrong unit**, which is the trap `CLAUDE.md` names in as many words, walked into anyway and caught only because the probe had a firing arm. **The right unit is the largest gap between consecutive test COMPLETIONS on a healthy run, and that is 2.34 s here** (computed from TRX start/end stamps). Shipped bound **5m** — ~128x headroom, and the value the local mutation harness already uses. **(2) It does NOT name the hung test, and `--blame` does not add that either** — both print the same "Sequence file will not be generated". Proven by constructing real starvation (one test, 1 s bound): the run exits 1 with *"The specified inactivity time of 1 seconds has elapsed"*, a process tree, and two hang dumps — **and no test name**. So the honest claim is that this **bounds the burn and makes the failure MODE legible**, turning six hours of silence into minutes plus dumps; the identity of the test comes from the console's last xUnit lines and from the dump. The row's "makes the failure legible" oversold it and this sentence is the correction. **Dumps are uploaded on failure** (`if: failure()`, `if-no-files-found: ignore`) because a dump left on the runner is not evidence. **`timeout-minutes` 45 / 60 / 20** on `build-and-test` / `aot-guard` / `analyzer-guard`, and these are explicitly **uncalibrated backstops, not thresholds** — nobody here can read the jobs' real durations while `gh` is unauthenticated (`XC-5`), so they are generous multiples of local timings whose only job is to be far below GitHub's 360. Tighten them once someone reads the run history. **Verified locally, both shipped command lines**: the Windows one green, and the Linux one — the combination I trusted least, because `--collect:"XPlat Code Coverage"` and Blame are two data collectors in one host — ran **2689/0/277** and still produced a 9.38 MB `coverage.cobertura.xml`, so the collectors coexist. **NOT verified**: dump collection on Linux goes through the runtime's `createdump` and every arm here was Windows |

The GEMM coefficient is **301 GFLOP/s = 84% of this box's 359 GFLOP/s single-core FMA ceiling**, so the kernel is close to done. The gather coefficient is **0.964 ns per element, about 4.8 cycles**, which is a scalar loop reading one element behind two bounds comparisons. Across VGG-16 that is **81.7 M elements = 78.8 ms of 204.4, or 39%**. **The fix is that it need not be a gather at all**: at `stride = 1` and fixed `(ky, kx)`, consecutive output positions read consecutive input addresses, so the interior of every panel is a contiguous copy — one `Vector512` load and store against 32 scalar iterations with a branch each. Edges stay scalar. **Sizing, and it is an estimate not a result**: 4x on the gather removes 59 ms of 204 and takes the ratio to ONNX Runtime from 2.00x to 1.38x; the achievable speedup of the vectorised form is NOT measured. **Do not start with the K=27 first layer** — the model misses it by 80%, so something else dominates there and it is a separate question. |

Do not reopen as a parallelism task** | **convolution scales 8.07x across 32 threads where the machine allows 14.30x, and three layers account for most of the loss** | Measured 2026-08-19 with `Scripts/ProfHarness` at 1 and 32 threads, per layer. **Nodes 24/26/28** (14x14 output, K=4608) reach **830 GFLOP/s and scale 3.9x**, where **node 21** with the same K on a 28x28 output reaches **2077 GFLOP/s and scales 8.3x**. The difference is work-item count: 196 output columns is **6.1 panels** of 32 for 32 workers, so `ResolveMBlocks` splits M into 5 and produces ~35 items of uneven size. Cost at 32 threads is **3.32 ms**; at node 21's rate it would be ~1.3 ms, so the item is worth **~2.0 ms of 34.5**. **Node 2** (224x224, K=576) is the opposite shape - it scales best of any layer at 10.33x but reaches only **927 GFLOP/s**, and its im2col gather moves ~115 MB, so it is gather-bound rather than parallelism-bound; **~1.9 ms**. **Node 0** has **K=27** (3 input channels x 3x3), so the micro-kernel's inner sweep is 27 deep and setup dominates: **149 GFLOP/s**, **~0.9 ms**. Three different defects; **do not fix them with one lever**. **What is NOT established**: whether the 14.30x FMA ratio is the right ceiling for a gather-bound layer - it is the ceiling for arithmetic, and node 2 may be bounded by memory well below it. Name the mechanism per layer before choosing a target. |
| `XC-86` | **DONE 2026-08-19 — -1.99 ms on VGG-16 (-5.7%) and -2.15 ms on the 60.9 MB CNN (-8.4%), ABAB with the canary moving the wrong way to fake it. Three mutations, all caught. Fifteen `ReluActivation` nodes cost **1.72 ms at 32 threads and 1.85 ms at 1** - a 1.08x speedup that reads as "never parallelised". **Checking the unit reverses the conclusion.** Node 1 covers 64x224x224 = 3.21 M elements, so it reads 12.8 MB and writes 12.8 MB in 0.39 ms = **65.9 GB/s against this box's measured 90.8 GB/s DRAM read ceiling**. It is at 73% of the memory ceiling; there is no parallelism to win. **The available fix is fusion, not threads**: apply `max(0, x)` in the convolution's store epilogue so the tensor is written once instead of written, read and written again. That removes the pass entirely - **~1.7 ms of 34.5** - and it is what MLAS does. Requires the graph importer to recognise a Conv->Relu pair and set a flag on the conv node; **the two must still be separable**, because a Relu whose input is consumed by anything else cannot be fused away. **Not measured**: how much of the 1.72 ms is L2-resident rather than DRAM, which sets how much fusion actually returns. |

At the measured 90.8 GB/s ceiling it would be **4.53 ms**, so the item is worth **~2.9 ms of 34.5** - and it is **26.4% of the whole VGG-16 budget**, second only to convolution. Candidate levers, none measured: more concurrent read streams than the current row split produces, non-temporal loads that do not pollute L2 with weights read once, and large pages. **State the mechanism before choosing**: 55.4 GB/s may be limited by stream count, by page walks or by the row split's granularity, and those call for different work. **Do not assume the 90.8 GB/s figure applies** - it was measured with a pure read loop, and a GEMV also writes and computes. |

Every Overfit-vs-ORT ratio published in `README.md` divides by this number, so a 29% drift in the denominator is a 29% error in the published ratio, in whichever direction happens to be current. **The canary discipline does not cover it**: the canary proves ORT and Overfit moved together *within* one process, which is exactly what would happen if the box changed between sittings. **What to establish**: whether the drift tracks thread count, power state, or ORT's own arena warmup - and whether Overfit's number moves with it or independently. Until it is explained, quote the ratio with the sitting it came from. |

Suite 2733/0/281, five consecutive** | **a fifth intermittent test, and its own comment claims a margin the failure disproves** | `Tests/TestSupport/Prepare/RealEstateFullCycleTests.Training_And_Prediction_Should_Work_EndToEnd` asserts `finalLoss < 2.0f` after 100 epochs and failed at **2.0235**. It reproduces only inside the full suite: six runs of the test alone, three at `OVERFIT_CONV_KBLOCK=128` and three at 0, were all green, and it failed on an unmutated tree, so **the conv work of `XC-78` is not implicated**. **The cause is visible in the source rather than in the timing**: the `LinearLayer` weights are initialised unseeded, so every run trains a different model, and the comment at line 90 — *loss converges well below the 2.0 threshold long before this* — is refuted by a 2.0235 outcome. **The threshold sits at the edge of the outcome distribution, not clear of it.** Fix direction is to seed the initialisation so the test is deterministic; lowering the threshold instead needs the distribution measured over many seeds first, because a threshold moved to accommodate one observed failure is a threshold with no evidence behind it. **Not measured**: how often it fails. **A SIXTH one found 2026-08-18 with the same signature**: `NeuralNetwork_TrainsOnXORProblem_AndConvergesToCorrectPredictions` failed once in a pair of full-suite runs minutes apart and passed the second time. **So the pattern is confirmed as a family, not an incident** — convergence-threshold tests over unseeded initialisation. Fix the family, not the instances: seed the initialisation. **FIXED 2026-08-18, and the family was four times larger than the two observed instances.** A census of tests that train, assert a convergence threshold, run in the default suite and do NOT call `MathUtils.SetSeed` found **nine files**: `TrainableLlamaBlockTests`, `EndToEndTrainingTests`, the three `Forecasting` suites, three `TinyShakespeare` suites and `RealEstateFullCycleTests`. **Only two had ever been seen failing** — the rest were sampling the same distribution and had simply been lucky. **`MathUtils.SetSeed` already existed**; nothing needed building. It is `[ThreadStatic]`, so the call goes in each class's constructor, which xUnit runs on the thread that then builds the model. **Determinism is proved, not assumed**: `RealEstateFullCycleTests` reports a final loss of **0.9010 in three consecutive runs, identical to four figures** — five green suite runs would only have shown an absence of bad luck. **And the threshold was never the problem**: 0.9010 against a `< 2.0f` bound is comfortable, so the observed 2.0235 was an unlucky draw rather than a bound set too tight. **The comment in each file says not to answer a post-seeding failure by trying seeds until one passes** — a reproducible failure is a finding, and widening a bound needs the outcome distribution measured over many seeds first. **Not done**: the `[LongFact]` members of those classes inherit the same seed, which is right, but none of them has been run to confirm they still pass under it. |

Suite 2733/0/279** | **every dense layer allocates a second, full-size copy of its weights, whether or not anything ever reads it** | `LinearLayer.cs:54` allocates `_weightsTransposed` of `inputSize * outputSize` floats in the **constructor**, unconditionally, and `ForwardInference` passes it on every call. Importing it costs **0.29 MiB managed and 1.23 MiB working set** from a 1,458-byte file — the kept copy is proportionate, and the branch that keeps it still works. **So both sides of the predicate are now measured**: 471.6 MiB removed where nothing could read it, kilobytes retained where something does. `DenseWeightResidencyDiagnostics` carries both as `[LongFact]`, so the next change to the weight layout can be re-measured rather than re-argued. **Still not measured**: whether any consumer outside this repository passes an output-major span expecting the length check to fire — that validation is now conditional, so such a caller would get a silent skip instead of an exception. |
| `XC-81` | **PART 2026-08-17 — no config route exists (both documented switches refuted); target (1) `ForwardInputMajorVector4` DONE at 1.28×, VGG 80.43 → 76.74 ms. Targets (2)–(5) open and unmeasured** | **`Vector<float>` is 256-bit on this AVX-512 machine, so every kernel written against the portable `Vector<T>` runs at half the hardware width** | **The measurement that shapes the whole task**: `Vector<float>.Count == 8` while `Vector512.IsHardwareAccelerated` and AVX-512 support are both true. **The documented switch does not work on .NET 10** — tried and refuted, both routes: `DOTNET_PreferredVectorBitWidth=512` as an environment variable leaves it at 256, and the runtimeconfig property `System.Runtime.Intrinsics.PreferredVectorBitWidth` leaves it at 256 **while verifiably present in the generated `.runtimeconfig.json`**. So there is no one-line configuration fix, and **the only route to 512-bit is an explicit `Vector512` path in our own code**, gated on `CpuFeatures.HasAvx512` with the portable version kept as the fallback — the shape `Conv2DGemmKernels`, `Q4KGemvKernel`, `Q6KGemvKernel` and (since `XC-80`) `LinearKernels` already use. *Stated as "not reachable by the two documented routes", not as "impossible" — a third route may exist and was not looked for.* **The consequence for planning is the uncomfortable part: the win cannot be measured before the work is done.** There is no arm to run; each file has to be rewritten to find out what it was worth. **The only real evidence is one instance**: `XC-80` rewrote `ForwardOutputMajorTiled` and measured **1.76–1.96×**. **Do NOT extrapolate that to 103 sites** — in the same row an intensity change that was 4× on paper delivered 1.25×, and a kernel already bound by something else gains nothing from wider lanes. **Inventory**, portable `Vector<float>` occurrences in `Sources/Main`: `LinearKernels.cs` 33 (the wide-output `ForwardInputMajorVector4` — still 256-bit, and it is what VGG-16's FC layers run on, 16.56 ms of an 83 ms model), `Adam.cs` 28, `Conv2DKernels.cs` 20, `OpenAiEsStrategy.cs` 13, `TensorMath.Activations.cs` 5, `TensorMath.Losses.cs` 4. Seven files already carry an explicit `Vector512` path. **Rank by measured cost on a real workload before touching any of them**, one file per change with its own A/B, because the fallback path must stay tested — on an AVX-512 box it is otherwise **unreachable from a test**, which is why `XC-80` gated on an environment variable (`OVERFIT_LINEAR_AVX512`) rather than a test-settable static. Any file done here needs the same. **RANKED 2026-08-17 by measurement, so the order is not a guess.** **(1) `ForwardInputMajorVector4` — same file, wide-output path, still 256-bit, and it is the clear first target.** It carries VGG-16's FC layers (**16.56 ms of an 83 ms model, ~20%**) and it is what `MLNetSingleInferenceBenchmark`'s 3-layer MLP runs on, where we measure **8.365 µs against ONNX Runtime's 8.910 — only 1.07×**. If the narrow path's 1.76–1.96× carries over at all, that single rewrite turns a 1.07× edge into something near 1.9× and takes a fifth off VGG's FC time. *Same run, worth recording because it is unflattering and within-process: **ML.NET is faster than both of us on that MLP** at 7.697 µs.* **(2) `Adam.cs` — the 512 conversion is REVERTED, and the reason recorded on 2026-08-17 was WRONG. Corrected 2026-08-18.** The sequence is the finding, so it is written out in full.

An explicit 512-bit loop was added to `StepAdamWRange`. It built clean and the suite stayed green — then a mutation TRIPLING the weight decay inside it left the suite green too. A parity test was written (`AdamWVectorParityTests`) and **three mutations escaped that as well**, including a control mutation to the portable loop. That was written up as *"neither vectorised branch executes for a plain single-parameter AdamW step"* — an unreachable-code-path conclusion, reached by inference from four escapes.

**It took one probe to refute.** An unconditional `throw` at the top of the vector branch reddened the test immediately. `Adam.Step()` -> `StepAdamWState` (`UseAdamW` defaults **true**) -> `size 2053 < ParallelElementThreshold 65_536`, so one serial range -> `StepAdamWRange(0, 2053)` -> `length 2053 >= VectorThreshold 512`. The path was never in doubt once anyone looked. **The defect was in the test, and it was arithmetic.**

Three independent reasons the old test could not fail, each sized against its own tolerance:

1. **At `t = 1` the update does not depend on `Beta1` or `Beta2` at all.** The bias corrections are exactly the factors just applied: `mHat = (1-b1)g / (1-b1) = g` and `vHat = sqrt((1-b2)g^2 / (1-b2)) = |g|`, so the whole step collapses to `lr * sign(g)`. Swapping the betas is a mathematical no-op that **no tolerance could ever catch**. Fixed by running 5 steps.
2. **`WeightDecay * LearningRate` was 1e-5, asserted at 1e-4.** Tripling the decay moves the weight by 2e-5 relative — a fifth of the assertion. Fixed by sizing the product at 1e-3 per step and tightening the tolerance to 1e-5.
3. **`Epsilon` was 1e-8 against gradients near 0.5** — a 2e-8 perturbation. Now 1e-4, which is also the value this project's QLoRA fine-tuning actually needs.

**The rewritten test then caught a real difference between the two modes.** Its AdamW arm passed and its plain-Adam arm failed by 3e-3 = 5 steps of `wd * lr`, because `UseAdamW = false` is not the same rule with a flag off: it **couples** the decay into the gradient before the moments (`g' = g + wd*w`, classic L2, so the decay also feeds the second moment), while AdamW decouples it onto the weight afterwards. The reference was corrected, not the kernel.

**Mutation battery now: 5 applied, 5 caught** — decay x3, beta swap, epsilon dropped (vector loop), epsilon dropped (scalar tail), coupled-L2 dropped. Baseline green and restore green on both sides, so the harness was capable of a verdict.

**The 512 conversion itself stays reverted, on the original grounds and only those.** At 0.36 FLOP/byte — against 1.00 for the register tile that gained 1.76-1.96x and 0.50 for the streaming GEMV that gained 1.28x — the expected win is ~1.1x, which is *inside* this benchmark's own +-5-9% error bars. **That is a measurement-resolution argument, not a coverage argument**; the coverage argument was mine and it was false. Whoever retries this needs a tighter instrument, and now has a test that will notice. Original measurement stands: **109.5 us per AdamW step at 1M parameters** (Coupled 98.7 us), ~16 MB moved per step, i.e. bandwidth-bound.

**The transferable lesson is about the inference, not about Adam.** Four escaped mutations were read as evidence about *reachability* when they were evidence about *sensitivity*, and the two are told apart by a probe that costs one build. **When mutations escape, prove the code runs before concluding it does not** — an escaped mutation says the test cannot see the change, and says nothing about why.
| `XC-80` | **PART 2026-08-17 — register tile + explicit 512-bit path shipped. Against ORT: batch 16 was a 1.25× loss and is now a 1.70× WIN, batch 64 is a tie, batch 256 is 1.12× behind (was 2.05×). Open: batch 256 still loses, and the 256-bit `Vector<T>` finding is unaddressed in 6 other files** | **batched dense inference runs B independent GEMVs where ONNX Runtime runs one GEMM, so the weights are re-read once per row and the arithmetic intensity is ~0.25 FLOP/byte** | **Measured, both engines in one process, `Linear(784→10)`**: batch 1 Overfit **232 ns** vs ORT 1,919 ns (**we win 8.27×**); batch 16 3,838 vs 3,078 (**ORT 1.25×**); batch 64 14,547 vs 7,184 (**ORT 2.02×**); batch 256 48,716 vs 23,751 (**ORT 2.05×**). **The shape of it is the finding**: per row Overfit is FLAT at ~190–240 ns from batch 1 to 256, while ORT falls 1,919 → 93 ns/row. A flat per-row cost means the batch buys us nothing at all. **Not fixed by `XC-79`, and it cannot be**: that layer holds 31 KB of weights, so it never reaches the parallel threshold at any batch size — this is a *kernel structure* problem, not a *parallelism* problem, and adding workers to it would be answering the wrong question. **The arithmetic.** `outputSize` 10 is below `InputMajorVectorizedOutputThreshold`, so each row runs ten `TensorPrimitives.Dot` calls of length 784. Every dot re-reads the whole input vector and its own weight row, so one row costs 15,680 FLOP against ~62 KB of L1 traffic — **0.25 FLOP/byte**, and at ~200 ns/row that is close to this core's L1 load bandwidth. We are not compute-limited, we are re-reading. **The fix is a register tile over (batch rows × outputs)**, the same structure `Conv2DGemmKernels.MicroKernel8x32Avx512` already uses: hold `MR×NR` accumulators across the input contraction so each input element and each weight element is loaded once per tile instead of once per (row, output) pair. Intensity becomes `MR·NR / (2(MR+NR))` — **1.00 FLOP/byte at 4×4 (4× today) and 1.33 at 4×8 or 8×4 (5.3×)**, bounded by the register file: 4×4 needs 16 vector accumulators, 4×8 needs 32, which is the whole AVX-512 register set and will spill. **Start at 4×4 and measure before widening.** **Acceptance**: batch 1 unchanged at ~232 ns (it is the published 8.3× number and it must not move), per-row cost falling with batch instead of flat, and the batch 16/64/256 arms beating ORT or at least not losing. **Do not confuse this with `XC-79`**: that one added parallelism above a byte threshold and left the per-row kernel alone; this one changes what a single thread does per row and needs no threads at all. **PART 2026-08-17 — the tile is shipped and it helps, but it does NOT fix the shape, and the reason why is a bigger finding than the tile.** `ForwardOutputMajorTiled` holds a 4×4 register tile (sixteen named locals, not an array — an array lives in memory and this is a register tile or it is nothing) over (batch rows × outputs), with partial tiles handled by clamping the row and column bases so the inner loop stays branch-free and reads in bounds, the same device as `MicroKernel8x32Avx512`. **Measured against ONNX Runtime, `Linear(784→10)`**: batch 16 **3,838 → 2,970 ns (1.29×)**, which turns a 1.25× LOSS into a tie (ORT 3,048); batch 64 **14,547 → 11,827 (1.23×)**, still behind ORT's 7,043 but 2.02× → 1.68×; batch 256 **48,716 → 47,344 (1.03×)**, essentially unmoved. Batch 1 is 253.9 ns against 232.2 before — it does not take the tiled path, so that is either code layout or run-to-run, but it is **not confirmed unchanged and should not be quoted as such**. **The shape is still wrong**: per row we now sit at a FLAT ~185 ns from batch 16 to 256, where the whole complaint was that the batch buys nothing. Intensity went 0.25 → 1.00 FLOP/byte and the time moved 1.25×, so intensity was not the binding constraint either. **WHAT THE INVESTIGATION FOUND INSTEAD, and it is worth more than the tile**: `Vector<float>.Count` is **8** on this machine — **256 bits** — while `Vector512.IsHardwareAccelerated` and `Avx512F.IsSupported` are both true. .NET caps `Vector<T>` at 256 bits unless `DOTNET_PreferredVectorBitWidth=512`, so **the entire `LinearKernels` forward path runs at half the width the hardware offers**, tiled kernel included. `Conv2DGemmKernels` escapes this by using `Vector512` and `Avx512F.FusedMultiplyAdd` explicitly; `LinearKernels` never got the same treatment. **That is the next step here and it is a bounded one**: mirror the conv kernel's explicit-width path behind `CpuFeatures.HasAvx512`, keeping the `Vector<T>` version as the portable fallback. Do it before widening the tile to 4×8 — doubling the lane count changes what fits in the register file, so the tile shape must be chosen against the width that will actually ship. **Tests**: six narrow-output batched parity cases including partial tiles on both axes at once (3 rows × 7 outputs), a non-vector-multiple input length, and batch 1 pinning that the gate still routes to the original kernel. **Three mutations, all caught**: duplicated tile row index, removed column clamp, store reading the neighbouring accumulator. **EXPLICIT 512-BIT PATH SHIPPED 2026-08-17 and it is the larger half of this row.** `ForwardOutputMajorTiled512` is the same 4×4 tile on `Vector512<float>` with `Avx512F.FusedMultiplyAdd`, gated on `CpuFeatures.HasAvx512`. **Measured, A/B in two processes** (`Linear(784→10)`, portable → 512): batch 16 **3,159.7 → 1,800.1 ns (1.76×)**, batch 64 **12,563.7 → 6,974.9 (1.80×)**, batch 256 **50,973.2 → 25,949.0 (1.96×)** — essentially the full factor from doubling the lane count. **Standing against ONNX Runtime is inverted**: batch 16 was a 1.25× LOSS and is now a **1.70× WIN**; batch 64 was 2.02× behind and is now **1.02×, a tie**; batch 256 was 2.05× behind and is now **1.12×**. Against this row's original baseline the two changes together are **2.13× at batch 16, 2.09× at 64, 1.88× at 256**, with batch 1 unmoved (232.2 → 234.8 ns, and it does not take either new path). **The gate is an ENVIRONMENT VARIABLE, `OVERFIT_LINEAR_AVX512`, not a test-settable static** — a process-wide bool that two kernels' numerics hang off is exactly the defect `XC-56` and `XC-57` were filed for, and this would have been the third instance. It also bought something a static could not: the portable path is otherwise **unreachable from a test on an AVX-512 box**, and it now runs its own parity arm in its own process — 16/16 on each. **STILL OPEN, which is why this row is PART**: batch 256 remains **1.12× behind ORT**, so the acceptance criterion *"beating ORT or at least not losing"* is met at 16 and 64 and missed at 256; and per-row cost is 112 / 109 / 101 ns at batch 16 / 64 / 256 — better than the flat 185 ns before, but still nearly flat rather than falling. **THE FINDING'S SCOPE IS WIDER THAN THIS ROW AND IS NOT YET FILED**: `Vector<float>` is 256-bit here, and **6 files in `Sources/Main` still use it across 103 occurrences** — `Adam.cs` (28), `Conv2DKernels.cs` (20), `OpenAiEsStrategy.cs` (13), `TensorMath.Activations.cs`, `TensorMath.Losses.cs`, plus the wide-output `ForwardInputMajorVector4` in this same file. Seven files already use explicit `Vector512`. **Each of those is potentially a ~2× left on the floor, and none of it is measured** — do not assume it transfers; the intensity work earlier in this row moved 4× on paper and 1.25× in reality. Suite 2705/0/279 before the 512 path; re-run pending. |
| `XC-79` | **DONE 2026-08-17 — shipped. VGG-16 FC layers 36.50 → 16.56 ms, whole model −17.3%; threshold measured (and the first guess was wrong). Suite 2694/0/278** | **`LinearKernels.Forward` — the inference path — has no parallel branch at any size, so one large dense layer runs on a single core** | **Measured**: in VGG-16, `LinearLayer[33]` (25088→4096) takes **31.34 ms with all workers and 31.51 ms with `OVERFIT_PARALLEL_WORKERS=1`** — a 0.5% difference, i.e. **it does not scale at all**, while the convolutions in the same run scale **9.98×**. The three FC layers are **36.9% of the whole model's time** (36.50 ms of 100.38 ms). **Confirmed in the source rather than inferred from the timing**: `LinearKernels.Forward` loops over `batchSize` and calls `ForwardInputMajorVector4` / `ForwardOutputMajorDot`, both vectorised and both sequential; the only `OverfitParallel.For` calls in that file are on the **backward** paths (`BackwardInputThreshold` 524,288 and `AccumulateWeightGradThreshold` 1,048,576). **The forward inference path has no threshold because it has no parallel branch to gate.** **Why it costs so much here**: at batch 1 a `Linear(25088, 4096)` is a GEMV that must read **411 MB of weights** to do 0.206 GFLOP, so it is bandwidth-bound — and one core cannot saturate this box's DRAM. Measured **13.1 GB/s**, against a machine that sustains far more across cores. **Do NOT simply parallelise unconditionally.** The opposite case is the repository's own headline: `Linear(784→10)` is **236.8 ns** and beats ONNX Runtime 8.3×, and dispatching 7,840 MACs to a worker pool would be pure overhead — `OverfitParallel.For` costs microseconds. So this needs a **measured threshold in the mechanism's unit**, and the unit is bytes moved, not FLOPs: the layer is bandwidth-bound, so the crossover is where the weight read stops fitting in cache, not where the arithmetic gets big. `LinearKernels` already documents exactly this reasoning for its backward thresholds (*"below threshold sequential SIMD is faster … Parallel.For has ~2-5 µs overhead"*) — the forward path needs the same treatment and never got it. **Blast radius is wider than CNNs**: every dense fp32 forward at batch 1 with a large layer — ONNX imports, MLP classifier heads, embedding projections. Quantised LLM decode is NOT affected; it goes through the Q4_K/Q8 GEMV kernels, which have their own parallel path. **Acceptance**: a threshold chosen from a measured crossover, `Linear(784→10)` unchanged at ~237 ns (it is a published number), and `LinearLayer[33]` moving materially. **DONE 2026-08-17. Suite 2694/0/278.** Forward inference now splits the output columns across workers above a threshold in weight bytes. **Result on VGG-16**: `LinearLayer[33]` **31.34 → 14.56 ms (2.15×)**, the three FC layers **36.50 → 16.56 ms**, whole model **100.38 → 82.99 ms (−17.3%)**, with convolution unchanged — so the gain came from where it was predicted. **Shape of the change**: the split is over output columns, not batch, so each worker owns a disjoint slice of the output and nothing needs synchronising; and the maths stayed in ONE kernel, which gained a `[columnStart, columnEnd)` range that the serial caller invokes with the full width. A second copy would have left two kernels to keep in step with a parity test able to check only one. **THE THRESHOLD WAS WRONG THE FIRST TIME AND THAT IS THE USEFUL PART.** 4 MB was chosen a priori and the sweep put it on the one size where parallel **loses**: 4 MB measured **0.59×**, while 2 MB was 0.97×, 8 MB 1.30×, 16 MB 1.80× and 392 MB 2.67×. Shipped at **8 MB**, the first size measured to win, with the tie at the crossover going to the path with no dispatch. **And the mechanism is not the one predicted**: the parallel arm costs ~0.22–0.25 ms at 4, 8 and 16 MB alike — four times the work for eight percent more time — so below that it is paying a **fixed ~0.22 ms dispatch cost**, not doing work. A hypothesis that each worker was losing the kernel's 4× unroll (at 32 workers a 1024-wide layer gives each half a block) was implemented via `minItemsPerWorker` and **measured neutral everywhere**, so it was reverted rather than kept — an unmeasurable refinement is a claim someone later has to re-check. The sweep is permanent: `Tests/Diagnostics/LinearForwardParallelThresholdDiagnostics.cs`. **Tests**: an independent naive-triple-loop oracle on both sides of the threshold plus a non-block-aligned width, a sentinel test for a column no worker writes, and a policy test that fails if the threshold ever drops far enough to dispatch `Linear(784,10)`. **Three mutations, all caught** — stride set to the range width instead of the layer width (2 red), range one column short (sentinel red), threshold to zero (policy red). *Trap worth keeping: the first harness reported the stride mutation as ESCAPED, and it had not — `[Theory]` names carry their parameters, so a `\w+\s*\[FAIL\]` regex matches nothing. Third time in one day that a "finding" was the instrument.* **BATCH > 1 DONE 2026-08-17, same day, and the shape left open above was measurably the wrong one.** The dispatch is now flat over the whole `batchSize × outputSize` rectangle instead of once per row, so a worker's range starts and ends part-way through a row. Two things that buys: the fixed dispatch cost is paid once rather than `batchSize` times, and every worker stays busy even when the batch is smaller than the pool — splitting on the batch alone would idle 30 of 32 workers at batch 2. **Measured on 2048×1024, per row: batch 2 0.2382 → 0.1267 ms (1.88×), batch 4 3.47×, batch 8 0.2332 → 0.0428 (5.45×), batch 32 1.56×, batch 64 2.02×; batch 1 unchanged at ~0.24 ms**, which is correct — one row is one dispatch either way. Per-row cost now falls monotonically with batch, the shape one amortised dispatch predicts. **The gate moved with it**: it now tests `batchSize × inputSize × outputSize × 4`, the bytes the call actually moves, so a 2 MB layer at batch 8 qualifies on its 16 MB of traffic instead of being judged on one row. At batch 1 that is identical to the old condition, so the measured 8 MB crossover still applies unchanged. **Tests extended to the new risk**: five batched parity cases, each row given its OWN input slice — a shared input would make every row identical and hide precisely the defect this code can have. **Three mutations, all caught**: input row offset dropped, output row offset dropped, and the column clamp removed (a worker walking past the end of its row). **One anomaly is left UNEXPLAINED rather than rationalised**: under the old per-row shape, cost per row was flat to batch 8 and then fell six-fold at batch 32, reproducibly across three runs. It does not match a per-row dispatch cost and nothing here accounts for it; the shape that produced it is gone, and inventing a mechanism for a dead code path would be worse than recording that it was never understood. |
| `XC-78` | **PART 2026-08-18 — diagnostic DONE and it REFUTED this row's own premise; the fix it pointed at is SHIPPED and measured. SIX fixes shipped, each ABAB'd against an ONNX Runtime canary: conv work-split (-7.0%), parallel pooling (-6.3%), dense row-split (-3.5%), fused patch gather (-13.7%), MR-major kernel packing (-8.4%), **and a full-tile micro-kernel body worth 3.46x on the kernel and -25.7% / -36.2% end to end — the micro-kernel was spilling its sixteen accumulators, which is why every memory hypothesis measured null**. **VGG-16 against ORT 4.87x -> 1.75x; the 60.9 MB CNN 5.98x -> 2.47x** (seven fixes). Conv is now 786 GFLOP/s; **the roofline denominator used earlier today was WRONG — see the correction note in `docs/measured-baselines.md`: the AVX-512 ceiling is 5136 GFLOP/s, not 2190, so every earlier percent-of-roofline figure was ~2.35x too generous**. Correctly: conv 15.3% of roofline, ONNX Runtime 33%. **The whole investigation, including the two benchmarks that turned out not to work and the reasoning behind each refutation, is written up in [`docs/conv-gemm-ceiling-investigation.md`](conv-gemm-ceiling-investigation.md)** — read that before proposing a fifth restructure. Parity unchanged throughout, suite 2728/0/279** | **the conv path is ~4.2× behind MLAS per core, the two obvious fixes are already measured-negative, and nobody has measured where the time actually goes** | **Position, in fractions of peak rather than in ratios, because that is what says whether headroom exists.** VGG-16 is ~15.5 GFLOPs/inference; single-threaded, Overfit takes 424.7 ms = **36.5 GFLOP/s** and ORT takes 101.0 ms = **153.5 GFLOP/s**. Against a single-core fp32 peak of ~275 GFLOP/s on this box (Zen 5, 2×512-bit FMA at ~4.3 GHz) that is **13.3% for us against 55.8% for MLAS** — so MLAS is not at the ceiling either, and the headroom is real rather than theoretical. **The capability is not in question**: our own Q4_K matmul reaches **1.70 TFLOP/s** (39% of 16-core peak) and beat llama.cpp's 1.56 on their own test shape, so this is a property of *this path*, not of what can be written in C#. **Two obvious moves are already MEASURED-NEGATIVE and must not be re-proposed.** (1) BLIS-style K-blocking + A-packing — `Conv2DGemmKernels.cs:298-302`: *"vgg 140→189"*, and the note explains why (most im2col K values are ≤ a few hundred, so a single K-block means no blocking benefit, while the A-pack adds single-threaded O(M·K) overhead). (2) **Winograd F(2,3)** — implemented, parity-correct (cos 1.0), and it **regressed deepcnn 119.7 → 214.4 ms (+79%)** with the ORT canary stable both runs; recorded at `Conv2DKernels.cs:19`. `Conv2DWinogradKernels.cs` is still in the tree with **zero call sites** (confirmed by `find_callers` and by grep) — it is a preserved negative result, not dead code someone forgot, and deleting it would destroy the evidence. **What the code itself names as the only remaining lever** (`Conv2DGemmKernels.cs:290-292`): parallel scaling *"collapses to 1–2× beyond ~2 MB per-core working set — so a parallel kernel that outruns its cache cannot be fixed by adding cores or by blocking, only by needing fewer bytes per FLOP."* That points at the im2col materialisation, and the arithmetic supports it: VGG-16's 256→256 layer at 56×56 expands to a K=2304 × N=3136 float matrix ≈ **28.9 MB**, written and re-read per layer, which no cache on this machine holds. MLAS avoids it by packing patches directly into the micro-kernel's buffer instead of materialising the matrix. **THE TASK IS THE DIAGNOSTIC, and it is one run**: split VGG-16's single-threaded 424.7 ms into im2col versus GEMM, and compute the GEMM's achieved GFLOP/s on those shapes. **The two outcomes point at different work and one of them may point at none**: if GEMM is already near 150 GFLOP/s and im2col dominates, the lever is fusing im2col into the packing step and the ceiling is knowable in advance; if GEMM is itself near 36 GFLOP/s, the micro-kernel is the subject and Winograd's negative deserves a re-run **on VGG-16 rather than on deepcnn**, since Winograd's efficiency rises with channel count and 512-channel layers are a different regime from the small model it was refuted on. **The product question is separate and should be answered by a human, not implied by this row**: dense fp32 ImageNet CNN inference is a supported import path, not the identity — the README now tells readers to use ONNX Runtime for exactly this shape if they can ship native dependencies. Closing a 4–5× gap on a workload that is not the product is a large investment, and the diagnostic above is what makes that a decision rather than a guess **DIAGNOSTIC RUN 2026-08-17, and it REFUTED the hypothesis this row was built on.** First, the instrument already existed and nearly got rebuilt: `Tests/Diagnostics/ConvGemmPartProfileTests.cs` is a `[LongFact]` that does exactly this split, plus a per-layer profile — *read the tree before writing a diagnostic*. It defaults to `cnn.onnx`, so it must be pointed at `vgg16.onnx` via `OVERFIT_CNN_ONNX` or it profiles a different model. **The split, all workers: im2col 18.0%, GEMM 82.0%** (9.69 ms and 44.16 ms per run). **So im2col is NOT the dominant cost and the "fewer bytes per FLOP / fuse the gather" plan above is not what the numbers ask for.** The 28.9 MB expansion is real and it is third-order. **Two corrections to the arithmetic in this row, both of which flattered us**: (1) VGG-16 is **30.94 GFLOP**, not 15.5 — the usual figure counts MACs, and the diagnostic's own docstring independently says ≈30.7 GFLOP for the conv stack, matching a hand rebuild of the layer shapes to 30.69; every GFLOP/s figure above was understated 2×. (2) the denominator should be the **measured** roofline from `MachineRooflineBenchmark`, **2190 GFLOP/s all-core**, not a clock-times-FMA-width estimate. Restated on that basis, all cores against all cores: **Overfit 307 GFLOP/s = 14% of roofline, ONNX Runtime 1497 = 68%.** **The real budget, per-layer, all workers (wall 100.38 ms)**: convolution **57.58 ms (58.2%)**, fully-connected **36.50 ms (36.9%)**, pooling 4.88 ms. Conv scales **9.98×** with workers and its GEMM runs at **695 GFLOP/s = 32% of roofline**; the FC layers scale **1.01×**, i.e. not at all, which is `XC-79` and is the single largest addressable item. **So the remaining conv work is the micro-kernel, not the gather** — 32% of roofline against MLAS's ~68% — and the Winograd negative deserves its re-run on VGG-16's 512-channel layers as this row already argued, now for a better reason than before. **Order of work, by measured size**: `XC-79` first (36.9% of the model on one core, and it is a missing branch rather than a tuning problem), then the conv micro-kernel, then im2col last. **CONV MICRO-KERNEL INVESTIGATED 2026-08-17 — one real defect fixed, three optimisation candidates eliminated, and the remaining question sharpened to something nobody had stated.** **(1) The pack-versus-micro-kernel ablation was DEAD on every AVX-512 machine.** `AblatePackB` / `AblateMicroKernel` were read only in `GemmNPanelWorker` (the AVX2 path) while this box runs `GemmNPanelWorker512`, so `ConvGemm_PackVersusMicroKernel_Split` printed three numbers that were pure run-to-run noise — measured before the fix: baseline 84.90 ms, *pack only* **86.50 ms (removing the arithmetic made it SLOWER)**, *micro only* 81.68 ms. **A diagnostic whose arms cannot move is worse than none, because somebody acts on it.** Both checks now exist in the 512 worker; suite 2699/0/279. **(2) With the lever live, the split REFUTES this file's own recorded suspicion.** `Conv2DGemmKernels.cs:50-54` says *"The pack is the prime suspect"*; measured on VGG-16 the micro-kernel costs **~38 ms against the pack's ~11.5 ms** (baseline 86.74, pack-only 48.58, micro-only 75.20; read as upper bounds, they overlap). **(3) Three candidates eliminated, two of them from measurements ALREADY IN THE REPOSITORY that would have been re-run had the file not been read first**: im2col is 18% of conv, not the cost; The gap is inside a single thread — production single-thread conv runs at **53.4 GFLOP/s** while this file records the micro-kernel measured in isolation at **132–148 GFLOP/s per core**. **That ~2.6× is the whole remaining story, and it is everything the isolated kernel benchmark excluded**: the A row-block reads, the C writes, the M-block loop, the per-panel setup. **Next experiment**: reproduce the isolated micro-kernel number on the 512 kernel at VGG's actual shapes, then add back one surrounding cost at a time until the 2.6× appears. Do NOT start from a kernel rewrite — three plausible rewrites are already refuted above. **PER-LAYER DIAGNOSTIC RUN 2026-08-18 — the premise this row rests on does not survive it.** Full table in `docs/measured-baselines.md` under *"VGG-16 convolution, per layer, against a MEASURED ceiling"*; raw output in `artifacts/xc78/`. **(1) The denominator was measured rather than estimated.** A per-thread claim needs a per-thread ceiling; dividing by the all-core roofline understates one thread by the core count, and correcting with a datasheet boost clock is a guess. `MachineRooflineBenchmark.PeakFmaFloat512` now takes `OVERFIT_ROOFLINE_WORKERS`: same kernel, 1 worker 2.139 ms, 32 workers 4.785 ms for 32x the work, so **all-core FMA throughput is 14.30x single-core** with clock drop and SMT already inside it; single-core ceiling **153 GFLOP/s**. **(2) PARALLELISM IS NOT FINE — this row's 9.98x is withdrawn.** Whole conv stack: 383.12 ms at one core, 60.33 ms at all, **6.35x = 44% of the available 14.30x**. The 9.98x was computed against a single-thread baseline of 574.74 ms which today measures **383.12 ms**; **what changed between the two runs is NOT identified and is an open question, not a win**. **(3) The single-thread gap is 1.6x, not 2.6x.** Best layers reach 92.5 GFLOP/s = 60% of the single-core ceiling against the isolated micro-kernel's 86-97%. The 2.6x came from a whole-stack average dragged down by conv1 (K=27) and conv2. **(4) THE CONCENTRATED LOSS IS DECOMPOSITION AND ITS CAUSE IS EXACT.** `Gemm` splits over N-panels — `nPanels = (n + nr - 1) / nr` with `nr = 32` on AVX-512, then `OverfitParallel.For(0, nPanels, 1, ...)`. conv11-13 have **N = 196, so nPanels = 7**: seven work items cannot occupy 32 workers whatever the kernel does, and the seventh panel is 4 columns wide against the others' 32.
