Gating at `nPanels * 2 <= workers` keeps the win and drops the loss. **Measured end to end, both engines all cores**: VGG-16 **79.01 -> 73.50 ms (-7.0%)**, 60.9 MB CNN **67.70 -> 63.14 ms (-6.7%)**, with the ONNX Runtime side of the same process moving **0.1% and 0.3%** - box still, lever isolated. Parity unchanged in both arms (cosine 1.000000, maxAbsDiff 3.204e-7 and 6.706e-8, same argmax). ORT's VGG-16 lead narrows **3.72x -> 3.45x**. **Coverage: `Tests/Core/Kernels/ConvGemmMSplitTests`** - a naive-triple-loop oracle at five shapes chosen against the decomposition, plus sentinel tests for writes past the result and for elements never written. Four defect mutations and one control: **three caught, control correctly not caught**. **The fourth escape is a finding, not a gap**: dropping the M-block START bound leaves the answer RIGHT - workers recompute each other's rows and store identical values - so **a lost start bound presents as a slowdown, never as a wrong answer**, and only a benchmark can detect it. **The test's tolerance is a derived bound, not a constant**: the first version failed on 0.0021735937 vs 0.0021725819, because a cancelling dot product's final value is not the size of the arithmetic behind it; it now carries `|error| <= K * u * sum|a_i * b_i|` accumulated per element. **Still open**: the 1.6x single-thread gap against the isolated micro-kernel, and conv1 (20% efficiency from a different cause, 1.49 ms). **The AVX2 path is deliberately unchanged** - porting the split without measuring it on AVX2 hardware is the port-a-measured-null mistake this row already records once. **SECOND FIX 2026-08-18: pooling was single-threaded, and it was 6.8% of the model.** Found by reading the per-layer profile the diagnostic produced, not by looking for it. The five pooling nodes cost **5.31 ms of 79.48 ms**, the first moving 16.06 MB in 2.86 ms = **5.6 GB/s, where the ReLU node beside it on the same tensor reached 62.7 GB/s** - eleven times faster on 1.6x more data. Cause read from the code: `MaxPool2DForwardSingleBatchPool2NoIndex` looped over channels serially, and channels are independent. Its horizontal collapse is also scalar, with a comment calling that negligible because `outW` is 13 - **calibrated on MNIST, while VGG's first pool has outW=112 and 64 channels, i.e. 802,816 scalar iterations**; that second lever is left. **Result: pooling 5.31 -> 1.09 ms (4.9x)**, serial below 262,144 elements because dispatch costs ~0.22 ms - the fifth node stays at 0.09 ms, which is the threshold's own canary. **End to end, ABAB in one box state** (`OVERFIT_PARALLEL_POOL=0` vs default): VGG-16 **-6.3%**, 60.9 MB CNN **-7.9%**, ORT canary 0.6-1.4%. **The ABAB was not optional - a single A/B had already produced a WRONG reading**: compared against the previous sitting, VGG improved while the CNN's ratio got worse, because ONNX Runtime had moved **24% between sittings on an untouched binary**. **What moved is still unidentified**, and the three small-model benchmarks stayed inside 1.3% across the same gap - so it reaches the large-CNN benchmark and not the small ones. **Current budget, 72.15 ms**: conv 55.6 ms (77%), FC 11.76 ms (16%, of which `Linear(25088x4096)` alone is 9.99 ms at **41 GB/s against a ~90 GB/s DRAM ceiling**), pooling 1.09, ReLU 1.76. **Ranked next**: (1) FC1 bandwidth, ~5 ms; (2) fuse im2col into the B pack - 19.8% of conv is pure movement MLAS does not perform at all; (3) the GEMM's 1.6x single-thread gap and ~51% parallel efficiency. **THIRD FIX 2026-08-18: the batch-1 dense layer was reading its weights with a 16 KB stride.** `Linear(25088x4096)` reads **392 MiB** per inference and took 9.99 ms = **41.1 GB/s against a MEASURED 90.8 GB/s read ceiling**. Decisive line, `LinearKernels.cs`: `weightsBase + ((long)i * outputSize) + j` - the dispatch gives each worker a range of output COLUMNS, so it reads 256 B then skips 16 KB, 25,088 times, landing on a different 4 KB page every time. **Premise stated before measuring, and one arm settled it**: column split 32 workers 8,756 us (47.7 GB/s), **row split SINGLE-THREADED 7,657 us (52.1 GB/s), row split 32 workers 5,895 us (69.3 GB/s = 76% of ceiling)**. One core reading sequentially beats thirty-two striding, and no bandwidth explanation survives that. **The threshold was measured and the obvious mechanism was WRONG**: L3 residency was the guess, but the row split already wins at 96 MiB while L3 is 128 MiB - and that is not one pool, being split across two CCDs. Ladder at 4096 outputs: 64 MiB column 1.14x, 72 MiB column 1.10x, 80 MiB column 1.16x, 88 MiB row 1.05x (inside error bars), **96 MiB row 1.15x clearly separated**, 192 MiB row 1.43x, 392 MiB row 1.49x. Gate at 96 MiB. **A UNIT ERROR NEARLY SHIPPED AND THE POLICY TEST CAUGHT IT**: the constant is `n * 1024 * 1024` (MiB) while the ladder had been written in decimal MB; 100,663,296 bytes is 100.7 MB AND 96 MiB, both correct, and comparing one against the other is not. **A number is not checked until it is checked in the unit its consumer uses.** **End to end ABAB** (`OVERFIT_LINEAR_ROW_SPLIT=0` vs default): VGG-16 66.59 -> **64.25 ms (-3.5%)**, ORT canary 1.8% - and since that canary spread is 1.2 ms against a 2.34 ms gain, read it as "about 2-3 ms". **Coverage: `Tests/Core/Kernels/LinearRowSplitTests`** - the sweep called directly at five small shapes (firing the gate needs a 96 MiB fixture), a written-every-output sentinel, and the policy asserted at all seven measured byte counts. **Six mutations, six caught.** **One contrast worth keeping**: dropping the M-block START bound in the conv split was NOT caught, because those workers recompute each other's rows and store identical values; the same mutation here IS caught, because each worker owns a separate partial buffer. **Whether that mutation is silent depends on whether the workers share a destination.** Limits: batch 1 only (with a batch the win is reading the weights once for all rows, i.e. a GEMM) and AVX-512 only. **Remaining ranked**: (1) fuse im2col into the B pack - 19.8% of conv is pure movement MLAS does not perform at all; (2) the GEMM's 1.6x single-thread gap and ~51% parallel efficiency. **FOURTH FIX 2026-08-18, and the largest of the day: the patch gather is folded into the GEMM's own pack.** The unfused path built a `K x N` column matrix - **115.6 MB at VGG's conv2** - written once and read once, to hold values readable straight from the 3.2 MB input. **Design taken from MLAS and made one level finer**: `MlasConvExpandThenGemmSegmented` (`convolve.cpp`) expands a `CountK x CountN` block into a column buffer and GEMMs that; here the destination is the micro-kernel's own packed panel, so there is no intermediate buffer at all (`K x 32` floats = 589 KB at VGG's largest K, inside a 1 MB L2). Row and column origins hoisted per panel, so 32 divisions per panel rather than one per element. **Measured ABAB** (`OVERFIT_CONV_FUSED_IM2COL=0` vs default): **VGG-16 64.31 -> 55.52 ms (-13.7%), 60.9 MB CNN 55.36 -> 46.93 ms (-15.2%)**, ORT canary 0.8-2.6%, parity unchanged (cosine 1.000000, same argmax). **Against ORT: VGG 3.41x -> 2.93x, CNN 5.65x -> 4.79x.** **It also removes the scratch buffer**, which no timing shows: `k * n` floats per convolution becomes `k * 32` per worker - a peak-resident-memory result, and peak is what this project's low-end position is about. **Mutations: six applied, five caught, the sixth EXPLAINED rather than patched** - writing `1f` instead of `0f` into lanes past `nrEff` leaves the suite green, and correctly so, because `StoreTile` writes exactly `nrEff` columns and those lanes never reach the output. The fill stays (uninitialised pool memory can hold NaN, and NaN through an FMA chain costs even in a discarded lane) but moved out of the gather loop, where it had been a branch on every one of `K * 32` elements. **Not done**: the AVX2 path still builds the column matrix, and the 1x1 stride-1 fast path is untouched because it never built one. **Remaining ranked**: the GEMM's 1.6x single-thread gap and ~51% parallel efficiency. **K-BLOCKING IMPLEMENTED, MEASURED AND NOT SHIPPED 2026-08-18 — a negative result from reading MLAS.** `MlasSgemmOperation` blocks both dimensions at 128, so its packed B panel is a constant **64 KB** and its A slice is `M x 128`; ours contracts the whole of K, so the panel is `K x 32` = **589 KB at K=4608** and the A sweep is the full 9.4 MB. **The earlier BLIS negative does NOT cover this**: it bundled K-blocking with A-packing, and its stated reason (*most im2col K values are at most a few hundred*) **is false for VGG-16**, whose K runs 576-4608 — at Kc=128 the late layers get 36 blocks. **Swept on VGG-16 with an unblocked run at each end**: off 56.18 / 56.12 ms (agreeing to 0.1%, so the box was still), Kc=64 **+5.2%**, Kc=128 +0.8%, Kc=256 -1.4%, Kc=512 -0.7%, Kc=1024 **-2.1%**. **The ORT canary moved 2.1% across the same runs, so the best result is the size of the instrument's own error — the verdict is NOT RESOLVED, not "a small win".** **The shape of the curve is the finding**: the gain rises monotonically toward larger Kc and the best value is the one nearest to no blocking, while small blocks are clearly worse. That is C re-accumulation dominating the L1/L2 benefit — full-K keeps the C tile in registers so C is written once, whereas blocking turns conv6's 3.2 MB of C traffic into ~115 MB. **For M of 256-512 against K of 576-4608, full-K in registers is already the right trade**, and MLAS's constants are tuned for a different M/K balance than im2col conv presents. **REMOVED 2026-08-18, later the same day, and the reason matters more than the removal.** Flipping `OVERFIT_CONV_PACK_A` to default-on pointed `ctx.A` at the MR-major packed matrix, while `MicroKernel8x32Avx512Accumulating` still read A row-major — **so the K-blocked path silently produced wrong results**, caught only because the correctness sweep was re-run with the switch set (14 tests red at Kc=256). Production was never affected, the path being default-off. **But incorrect code behind a switch is worse than no code**: whoever sets it gets silent wrong answers. Re-testing it fairly would now need TWO rewrites — an MR-major read and a full-tile body of its own, since the accumulating kernel spills exactly as the general one did — for a hypothesis already measured null once. The path, its kernel, `LoadTile` and the environment name are deleted; **this measurement is the deliverable.** **CI does not exercise it** (Kc=0 by default); verified by the whole suite green at Kc=64/128/256/1024/5000 — 5000 exceeds every K here, so the result must not depend on the split — and by **four mutations, all four caught at Kc=128 and all four correctly NOT caught at Kc=0**, which is what proves they reach the new path and leave the default alone. **Remaining ranked**: the GEMM's 1.6x single-thread gap and ~51% parallel efficiency, both still unexplained. **Three structural fixes are now refuted** (panel grouping, BLIS blocking+packing, K-blocking alone), so the next step is the ladder experiment this row already prescribes — reproduce the isolated micro-kernel number at VGG's shapes and add ONE surrounding cost back at a time — not a fourth restructure. **FIFTH FIX 2026-08-18: the kernel matrix is repacked MR-major, once, when the layer enters inference mode.** Came from reading BLIS, not from measuring. **`docs/KernelsHowTo.md` states the contract outright**: A's micropanel is *"stored by columns with leading dimension PACKMR"*, so the MR values a k-step consumes are adjacent. Ours read `rows[r][kk]` — eight floats `k * 4` bytes apart, **18 KB at K=4608** — the same defect class as the dense layer's 16 KB stride found the same day. **Why the old BLIS negative does not cover it**: BLIS packs A per call because A is caller data; **in inference A is the convolution's own weights and never changes**, so the pack is a load-time cost. That premise difference is the entire result. **Measured in two stages.** Per-call packing: VGG -4.1%, CNN -4.5% — resolved but modest. **The per-layer split then located the cost exactly**: it all fell on small-N layers, where the packed matrix serves 7 panels instead of 98 — **conv13 LOST 16.7% while conv6 gained 11.4%**. Moving the pack to load time: conv stack **43.86 -> 39.04 ms (-11.0%)**, 699.7 -> **786 GFLOP/s = 36% of roofline**, and small-N layers now BEAT the unpacked baseline (conv13 505.9 -> 531.6) — **which is also how the wiring was proved live rather than silently falling back to per-call packing**. **End to end ABAB**: VGG-16 55.37/55.37 -> **50.74/50.68 ms (-8.4%)** — the unpacked arm repeated to the hundredth of a millisecond — and the 60.9 MB CNN 46.45/46.90 -> **41.89/42.00 ms (-10.1%)**, ORT canary 1.1% and 4.5%, parity unchanged. **Against ORT: VGG 3.03x -> 2.77x, CNN 5.01x -> 4.49x.** **Cost**: a second copy of the kernel weights, 58.8 MB across VGG's conv stack, held only in inference mode — `Train()` releases it, `InvalidateParameterCaches()` drops it, `Dispose()` frees it. Same shape as the duplication `XC-82` objects to, except this one is on the hot path and measured to pay. **Remaining**: the GEMM's single-thread gap and parallel efficiency. Three structural fixes stay refuted (panel grouping, BLIS blocking+packing, K-blocking alone); the ladder experiment this row prescribes is still the right next step. |

But arms 1 and 2 anchored only `for (var i = 0; i < half; ...)`, so the leak went into the **first half only**, and both read `first half 200000 B / second half 0 B`; I recorded that as the discriminator behaving backwards and hypothesised JIT escape analysis. **It was neither — the mutant was asymmetric and the tool reported it exactly right.** Arm 3 anchored both loops and gave `200000 B / 200000 B, 40.0 B/call`, which is the shape the docblock predicts for a uniform per-call leak, so the discriminator is now validated in both directions. Arm 3 also reddened an **18th** test, `XgboostParityTests.Prediction_IsZeroAllocation_AfterWarmup`: it runs 50 iterations, so a first-half-only leak is 25 x 40 B = 1000 B and clears the 1024 B floor — a live demonstration that a short loop has almost no headroom. **Second correction, and it came from the instrument's first real readings rather than from reasoning:** the docblock said one-time work is *front-loaded* — first half dirty, second half clean. One loaded run produced **both** shapes, `16432 B / 0 B` on `AveragePool_InferenceAllocatesZeroBytes` and `0 B / 8216 B` on `ResNetBlock_DAG_InferenceAllocatesZeroBytes`, and the second shape was **not in the reading instructions at all**, so it read as unexplained. Tier-up and OSR are front-loaded; an ambient event from another thread is not. **The rule is now "either half clean", not "the second half clean"**, and both messages say so. Both figures were ~1.6 B/call against ~24 B for a single object, which rules out a per-call leak on its own. Those three failures did **not** reproduce: two isolated runs and three further full suites all green, and the gen counters in the failing window (`gen2 +36` and `+4`) name the load. **Still open and deliberately not touched: the 1024 B floor.** It is now the only thing standing between this family and a green suite under load, but nothing yet distinguishes "the floor is too tight for a 10 000-iteration window" from "the ambient events are real and should be tolerated per-window rather than per-total" — and raising it is how a real per-call leak on a rarely-taken path becomes invisible. The next sighting will carry the numbers to decide it, which is what this row was for **THE NEXT SIGHTING ARRIVED THE SAME DAY, and the diagnostic did its job.** During the `XC-70` upgrade `XgboostParityTests.Prediction_IsZeroAllocation_AfterWarmup` failed and, for the first time in this family's history, said why: **`first half: 8136 B over 25 calls · second half: 0 B over 25 calls (0.0 B/call) · overall 162.7 B/call · GC while measuring: gen0 +10, gen1 +8, gen2 +6`**. Read by the corrected rule: **one half clean ⇒ the bytes were one-time**, and gen2 moving six times inside the window says another thread was collecting hard. It is not a per-call leak. **It is also the short-loop case this row predicted**: that test runs **50** iterations, so a half is 25 calls and the 1024 B floor is 41 B of headroom per call — one ambient event blows it. Compare the 10 000-iteration sites, where the same absolute event is 0.8 B/call and clears the floor untouched. **So the floor is not one number: `OneTimeJitNoiseFloorBytes` is an ABSOLUTE budget applied to loops that differ by 200x in length, and it is the short loops that have no room.** Two candidate shapes, neither yet measured: scale the floor with `iterations` (a per-call budget with an absolute minimum), or require the *half-vs-half* comparison rather than the total, which is the discriminator the split already computes and which is invariant to loop length. **Still do not simply raise the number** — that buys the short loops room by blinding the long ones, which is the direction that hides a rare-path leak. **Also now measured: the family flickers in ISOLATION**, three filtered runs giving pass / one failure / pass, so this is the instrument and the box, not the suite around it **FLOOR FIXED 2026-08-17, and the fix is a change of UNIT rather than of number.** The absolute budget stays only as the threshold above which a lump is reported; the verdict is now computed in **bytes per call**, which is what makes it length-invariant. Two conditions, because they catch different leaks: **SUSTAINED** — `min(firstHalfPerCall, secondHalfPerCall) >= 1.0 B/call`, i.e. even the quieter half is allocating, which is the ordinary per-call leak and fails identically at 50 calls and at 10 000; and **RARE PATH** — both halves allocated something and the total is over the one-time budget, which a single lump cannot produce. Anything else — **one half genuinely clean** — is one-time by construction and passes however large it is, with a line written to the test output so it is not silent. `1.0 B/call` is chosen against the mechanism: the smallest .NET heap object is ~24 B, so a real per-call allocation clears it 24x over, and keeping the floor *below* 24 rather than at it is what preserves sensitivity to a leak on a path taken only sometimes. **Four arms, mutations injected into the measured loops themselves**: clean → green (71 tests); sustained 40 B/call in both halves → **18 red**, message *"SUSTAINED per-call allocation: the quieter half alone is 40.0 B/call"*; **rare path, one call in fifty → 17 red** at ~0.8 B/call, which is the arm that exists to prove sensitivity was **not** traded away for the correction; and a ~8 KB lump in the first half only → **green**, which is the correction — that exact shape used to be red. *Limitation found by arm D and worth keeping: at the 50-iteration site a one-in-fifty rare path lands in the first half only and passes. That is a property of the loop length, not of the rule, and the answer if it ever matters is more iterations at that site.* **`NoPerCallAllocation(long, string)` DELETED** — its last call site went in the migration above, and a helper that only supports the wrong measurement is a way to reintroduce this defect one call at a time. **Result: the family ran clean 5 of 5 plus a full suite at 2689/0/277 — and, worth stating because it is the difference between a fix and a suppression, ZERO "one half is clean" notes fired in those runs**, so they were genuinely quiet rather than quietly forgiven |

| `XC-71` | **DONE 2026-08-17 — `--version` answered before parsing, for every command; the pasted "pull it" advice consolidated into one guarded helper. Six arms green, suite 2689/0/277. One finding left for the user at the end** | **there is no way to ask the container what version it is, and asking the obvious way gives a misleading answer** | The image's `Entrypoint` is `["/app/overfit", "serve", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8080"]`, so anything appended on `docker run` lands as an argument to **`serve`**. `docker run --rm devonbikeit/overfit:10.1.0 --version` therefore becomes `overfit serve … --version` and the CLI reads the flag as a **model name**: *"Model '--version' not found in /home/app/.overfit/models. Download it first: overfit pull --version"*. **This is the first thing anyone does with an unfamiliar container**, and the answer is not merely unhelpful, it points at a wrong action — the message tells the operator to pull a model called `--version`. **The image is distroless**: there is no `sh`, so `--entrypoint sh` does not work either, and the usual fallback of poking around inside is unavailable. The label-based route is the other candidate and was not checked — see below. **Options, none of them decided**: handle `--version` (and `--help`) in `serve`'s argument parsing so a flag that is universally understood is not read as a positional; or drop `serve` from the `Entrypoint` into `Cmd`, so `docker run image --version` overrides cleanly and `docker run image` still serves — that is the conventional shape and it costs nothing at runtime, but it **changes how every existing `docker run … <args>` invocation binds**, which is a compatibility question for anyone already passing flags; **The third option — OCI labels — was checked and ALREADY EXISTS, so do not "add" it.** `docker inspect` on the published image returns eight labels including `org.opencontainers.image.version = 10.1.0` and `org.opencontainers.image.revision = 93ff7c604ea4b1b8549649dad934562132dc3cc0` (the full merge SHA), alongside `.source`, `.created` and `.title`. **That changes what this row is worth**: a correct, machine-readable answer is one `docker inspect` away, so this is not "the container cannot say what it is" — it is only that the *ergonomic* route gives a **wrong** answer instead of no answer. **The remaining defect is therefore the message, not the missing capability**: telling an operator to `overfit pull --version` sends them to do something that cannot work. Fixing the argument parsing is small; moving `serve` from `Entrypoint` to `Cmd` is the conventional shape but changes how every existing `docker run … <flags>` binds, so it is a compatibility decision rather than a tidy-up. Not release-blocking; 10.1.0 is published and this behaviour is unchanged from every earlier tag **DELIVERED 2026-08-17, and the `Entrypoint`→`Cmd` option was REFUSED rather than deferred.** It would break usage the Dockerfile itself documents at `:10-11` — `docker run -p 8080:8080 -v /host/models:/models overfit /models/x.gguf` — because under `Cmd` that argument *replaces* the command instead of appending to it, so the documented invocation would run `overfit /models/x.gguf` and not serve at all. **Fix taken instead: `--version` is answered in `Program.cs` before parsing, and for EVERY command rather than just `serve`** — no command here takes it as a value, so there is nothing to disambiguate, and a rule that holds everywhere needs no exceptions. It **re-dispatches to the root command** rather than formatting its own string, so the container's answer is by construction identical to `overfit --version`, through the path that already publishes clean under Native AOT. *Two mechanisms were tried and rejected first, both on AOT/Linux grounds: `FileVersionInfo.GetVersionInfo(Environment.ProcessPath)` returns empty on an ELF binary (no Win32 version resource) — i.e. it would fail precisely in the container this row is about — and `GetCustomAttribute<AssemblyInformationalVersionAttribute>` risks `IL2026` under the `aot-guard` job's `TreatWarningsAsErrors`.* **The second half is the one that generalises.** `--version` is now intercepted, but a *typo* — `--verison` — still reaches the model resolver, and that two-line "not found / pull it" pair was **pasted at five call sites**, so the same wrong advice was waiting behind four other commands for any mistyped flag. Consolidated into one `Commands.NotFound(model)` which reports *"'--verison' looks like an option, not a model name"* when the token starts with `-`. **The guard is the leading dash, not an allow-list of known flags** — no allow-list recognises a typo, and nothing in the model store or on HuggingFace begins with `-`. **Six arms, against the built CLI**: root `--version` unchanged; `serve --version` → the version, rc 0; `serve --verison` → the option-shaped message, rc 1, and **no `pull` advice**; `serve --help` unchanged; `chat --version` → the version (the rule really is everywhere); `serve nosuchmodel` → the original not-found text, rc 1, unchanged. **LEFT FOR THE USER, found while fixing this and arguably the bigger half of the row's title**: the answer `--version` gives is `release+id.<commit>` and **contains no version number**, because `Directory.Build.props:15` sets `<InformationalVersion>release+id</InformationalVersion>` with no recorded reason. So "there is no way to ask the container what version it is" is now only half-fixed — it answers, and the answer identifies a commit rather than `10.1.0`. **Not changed here on purpose**: `InformationalVersion` is stamped into every published package's metadata, which is a release decision. The OCI labels still carry `.version` correctly |

So the upgrade is **one property and two pins**, not a CI migration — the cost was never the obstacle. **What settles it is what that property IS.** `IsTestingPlatformApplication=false` does not move this repository toward MTP; it is an **explicit opt-out of it**, suppressing a target whose whole purpose is to say that the way we invoke `dotnet test` is unsupported on .NET 10 SDK and later. On 3.2.2 with the attribute: **0 overlaps**. With the attribute REMOVED: **8 overlaps, 8 red** — so the probe can see a violation, which is what makes the green meaningful. On 4.0.0 with the attribute: **0 overlaps**. So `DisableParallelization` still delivers real non-overlap under 4.0.0's rebuilt scheduler, and `XC-54` / `TG-T13` keep the guarantee they rest on. That is not a separation. | `XC-69` | **PART — OPEN — the row `XC-52` referred to as "its own row" and which never existed. Scoped 2026-08-16 by reading both sides of the protocol** | **the decode pool's WAKE path has no test, and its failure mode is a silent throughput loss rather than a red** | The park/wake protocol was rewritten in `9074647`: `_decodeParkSemaphore` + `_decodeParkedCount` were replaced by `_decodeParkLock`, a plain `object` (deliberately not a `System.Threading.Lock` — `lock` on a `Lock` does **not** go through `Monitor`, so `Monitor.Wait`/`PulseAll` would not work, `OverfitParallel.cs:379`). **Be precise about what is and is not covered, because "the park protocol is untested" is what I said and it is wrong.** *Parking* **is** covered: `DecodePoolIdleBurnTests.Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` reddens on a pool that spins instead of parking — measured 2026-08-16 at **10.02 effective cores against a 0.00 canary**, with 10 of 10 workers identified. *Waking* is not. **The two halves, quoted rather than remembered.** Waker (`:749-757`): `Volatile.Write(ref _decodeGen, generation)` **then** `lock (_decodeParkLock) { Monitor.PulseAll(...) }`. Parker (`:879-887`): `lock (_decodeParkLock) { while (Volatile.Read(ref _decodeGen) == seen) { Monitor.Wait(...); } }`. **By inspection this is correct and the ordering is what makes it so**: the generation is published *before* the pulse and the predicate is re-tested *under* the lock, so a worker either already sees the new generation and never waits, or is inside `Wait` and gets pulsed. A surplus pulse costs one re-check; the classic lost-wakeup needs a test *outside* the lock, and there is not one. **The gap is that nothing would notice if that stopped being true.** Delete the `PulseAll` and parked workers are never woken: the pool silently degrades to the calling thread alone. Correct output, no exception, no hang — **just slower**, which no assertion in this repository is watching. That is the same silent-regression class as `PB-12`, where headline throughput numbers survived a synchronisation change on the same path for weeks. **What a test has to establish, and why it is awkward**: that a worker which HAS parked is woken by the next dispatch. Parking is entered only after the spin budget (`DecodeSpinBudgetIterations`, ~1-2 ms of `SpinWait(32)`), so the test must genuinely idle the pool first — and then assert on *work being picked up by a pool thread*, not on elapsed time, or it becomes `TG-T12`/`TG-T13` all over again: an assertion on an environment-produced quantity that fails on a loaded box. The `internal` seams added under `XC-52` (`DecodePoolSize`, `DecodePoolEnabled`) plus the thread-identification approach `TG-T13` proved out (`OverfitDecode-{i}` registered against the OS thread id) are the materials. **Also worth fixing while here**: `Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` is `[ModelFact]` + Windows-only, so even the covered half runs **only on this dev box** and never in CI — acceptable today because it needs a real model, but it means neither half of this protocol is guarded by anything CI runs **DELIVERED 2026-08-16 — `Tests/Core/Runtime/DecodePoolWakeTests.cs`, one plain `[Fact]`, zero production changes.** Answer to the row's question was **(1)**: admissible with the existing seams. **The assertion is not "workers execute chunks" — that is a race outcome — it is that worker participation is made NECESSARY for the dispatch to terminate**: every chunk body blocks until some body has entered on a thread named `OverfitDecode-*`. The caller either claims nothing (workers took everything) or claims one and blocks inside it, so at least one chunk stays unclaimed; the generation is advanced before the pulse and every worker was *observed* parked, so each is inside `Monitor.Wait` and each is pulsed; under ordinary fairness some worker re-tests, sees the generation moved, claims and enters. Nothing depends on who wins a race or on how long anything takes. **Parking is established, not assumed**: workers are identified by execution under a barrier, then `Thread.ThreadState` is polled until each reads `WaitSleepJoin` — measured `10 of 10 identified`, `all parked = True` after 22 ms. Managed `ThreadState` needs neither `ProcessThread` nor a model, so unlike `Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` this is a plain `[Fact]` and **the wake half is CI-eligible**. The 30 s bound is a backstop, not the assertion, and on expiry the body **sets a flag and returns** — the dispatch then completes normally and the verdict is an assertion rather than a hang, so the machine mutex is never at risk. **M1, removing `PulseAll`: RED in 30 s with a named message, and 0 of 2659 pre-existing tests noticed** — added coverage, not moved. Suite 2660/0/277. **THE FINDING THAT OUTLIVES THE TASK, volunteered rather than asked for**: a second mutation moving `Volatile.Write(ref _decodeGen, generation)` to **after** the pulse — the classic lost wakeup, and the exact step the invariance argument above leans on — leaves the test **GREEN**. `PulseAll` fires inside the lock, so a woken waiter cannot return from `Wait` until the dispatcher releases it, and the very next instruction publishes the generation: the window is a few instructions wide, a race that essentially never fires rather than a permanent failure. **No schedule-invariant assertion can catch it, this one included.** So the publication ORDER is held by the comment at `OverfitParallel.cs:734` and by nothing executable — recorded in the test's own doc so a future reader does not mistake a green wake test for a guarantee about ordering. **Open**: the test was run only on Windows. `Thread.ThreadState` is cross-platform, but if `WaitSleepJoin` is not reported on Linux the test **skips rather than fails** — a silent non-test in CI, which one CI run settles; the skip message prints the observed states |
| `XC-68` | **OPEN — measured 2026-08-16 while fixing the `aot-guard` failure; the fix landed, this is the general finding it exposed** | **no `.editorconfig` section can set a severity on a SOURCE-GENERATED tree, so every rule this repo scopes that way is inert there** | Found because the configuration-binder generator emitted `section.GetChildren().Any()` and `RS0030` (banned `System.Linq`) failed the CLI AOT publish. **The mechanism is identity, not path**: the compiler builds its per-tree severity map from the `csc` command line, and a generator adds its trees *after* that map exists — so no section reaches them, however the pattern is written. **The decisive arm, and it is worth copying as a technique**: the same build without `TreatWarningsAsErrors` reports `warning RS0030`. Not *absent* — which is what `[*.g.cs]`/`[**/obj/**]` setting `none` would produce; not *error* — which is what `[*.cs]` would produce. It is the analyzer's own default showing through, which proves **neither section applied**, even though the file sits under `obj/` **and** ends in `.g.cs`. **Consequence beyond the fixed case**: `.editorconfig`'s `[*.g.cs]` section (~line 605) has **never worked for its stated purpose**, and its comment claimed the opposite — that filename scoping "is narrow enough to leave the ban armed everywhere it matters", implying it was armed at all. Kept, because it still covers real `*.g.cs` files that exist on disk as compilation inputs, but its comment now records the measurement. **The working instrument is a per-project global AnalyzerConfig** (`is_global = true`), which the compiler consults precisely for the trees the per-path map misses: `Sources/Anomalies/SourceGenerated.globalconfig`. Real code keeps `error` because `[*.cs]` outranks a global config. **What to check next**: every other rule this repository scopes by path expecting it to cover generated output — and whether any *other* generator in a LINQ-banned project currently emits a banned API, which the new global config would now silence project-wide. That silencing is the fix's stated cost and is written into the file. **Also worth keeping**: a bare `using System.Linq;` produces **no `RS0030` at all** — the rule fires on symbol references, not directives — so anyone probing whether this ban is armed with a lone using directive is probing nothing, and will read the silence either way they expect |

Suite 2689/0/277** | **a test named `AnUnrecognisedChangeFailsClosed` closes with `Assert.Equal(level, level)` — it cannot fail, in the class that guards API compatibility** | `Tests/TestSupport/Assemblies/BreakingChangeClassifierTests.cs:753-757` loops every `ChangeLevel` and asserts each equals itself. The comment above it claims *"Every classified change is either explicitly safe or explicitly not; nothing is 'unknown'"* — **the assertion checks neither**. The only load-bearing line in the test is the `Assert.Empty(comparison.Changes)` above, which compares identical source with itself; that is a real property but a different one from the name. **Why this one matters more than an ordinary vacuous test**: this class is the guard on `AC-UNCLASSIFIED`, the fallback that stops a facet no rule understands from being waved through as additive — and `XC-40` is the incident where 42 public types left the package while the CHANGELOG recorded seven. Someone reading this test's name as a fail-closed guarantee is relying on nothing. **The honest fix is probably not more assertions**: the docblock already admits the property cannot be synthesised from source, because every facet the reader can write is covered by a rule. So either pin the policy where it actually lives — assert that `AC-UNCLASSIFIED`'s declared `ChangeLevel` is at the breaking level, which is a real, failable statement — or delete the loop and rename the test to what it does check. **Do not leave a tautology behind a name that promises a guarantee.** Same class as today's other lesson: *a green test is not evidence until you have checked it contains an assertion that could go red* **DELIVERED 2026-08-17, and the row's own preferred fix was the wrong one.** "Assert `AC-UNCLASSIFIED`'s declared `ChangeLevel`" reads the constant the classifier reads, so it pins a literal rather than the behaviour; the test now builds a `Changed` `ApiDifference` **by hand** and calls `BreakingChangeClassifier.Classify` directly, asserting the finding that comes out is `AC-UNCLASSIFIED` at `BinaryBreaking` and carries **both** descriptors. **The facet was chosen semantically, not by grep**: `find_references` on `ApiMember.HasDefaultImplementation` gives two reads inside the classifier — `:282`, reachable only on the *Added* path, and `:548`, which fires on `false -> true` — so `true -> false` on a `Changed` pair reaches no rule at all. **Why the seam and not source**: in real metadata `abstract` flips in the same edit, and `AC-VIRTUAL-MADE-ABSTRACT` claims the pair first. That is no longer a claim in a comment — `DefaultImplementationRemoved_IsClaimedByAnExistingRule` compiles both halves and pins the rule id, so the day the fallback becomes reachable from source, that test goes red first. **Mutation, five guards, all passed**: `ChangeLevel.BinaryBreaking` -> `Additive` on the `AC-UNCLASSIFIED` construction compiled, and reddened `AnUnrecognisedChangeFailsClosed` **and nothing else**; restore verified byte-for-byte against `git show HEAD:`. The old loop would have stayed green through it. *One trap worth keeping: the literal anchor with `\n` in it matched **zero** times against the CRLF working tree and reported as "not unique" — the same line-ending class as the `windows-latest` failure this file already records* |

See the delivery note at the end of this row** | there is no build-time guard against a culture-sensitive interpolation, and the obvious candidate was measured and does not work | `XC-64` fixed 42 sites and pinned them with `Tests/Anomalies/DiagnosticTextIsCultureInvariantTests.cs`, which compares each producer's output under the invariant culture against `pl-PL` and `tr-TR`. **That test protects today's sites and cannot see site 43** — it enumerates producers by hand, so a new diagnostic string is unprotected the moment it is written. **`CA1305` is not the answer and that is measured, not assumed**: `dotnet build Sources/Anomalies -p:AnalysisMode=All -t:Rebuild` yields exactly **2** diagnostics in the whole project and **neither is one of the 42** — it fires on `IFormatProvider` overload *selection*, and a bare `$"…{v:P0}…"` selects no overload at all. Recorded in `docs/measured-baselines.md` so the proposal is not re-derived. **So the guard has to be an `OVERFIT0xx` analyzer**: flag an interpolated string containing a numeric or `TimeSpan`/`DateTime` hole that is not wrapped in an invariant `string.Create` / `FormattableString.Invariant` / a `.ToString(…, IFormatProvider)`. (2) The specifier-less hole is the hard case: three of the 42 defects had **no format specifier**, so a rule keyed on `:P0`-style syntax misses exactly the ones a human reviewer also misses. **The related class this does not cover**, worth its own decision: `PrometheusHistoricalSource.cs:191` and `PrometheusMetricWindowSource.cs:343` interpolate `long` timestamps into **URLs**. Same code, opposite results, and the test compares ordinally. **This is a class, not a site: 26 places** in `Sources/Main/Statistics/` and `Sources/Anomalies/` interpolate `:P0`/`:P1`/`:G3`/`:G4` with no explicit culture. Only one is caught, because only one is asserted literally. **The other direction is worse and nothing tests it**: `:G3`/`:G4` under a Polish culture emit `1,5` where the invariant culture emits `1.5` — and these are the anomaly guard's incident and reason strings, the ones an operator greps and an alert rule matches on. A text-matching rule would silently diverge between two machines running the same build. **Fix in the source, not in the test.** Loosening the assertion turns CI green and leaves the product emitting non-deterministic diagnostics — and the determinism is the point of writing those strings at all, because something downstream reads them. Shape: `needs:` on the guard jobs, or a job that re-runs them on the target commit, plus `api_compat_check.py` in the publish path with its non-zero exit treated as a stop. **Note the one thing that must NOT be copied from the local experience**: with `OVERFIT_API_BASELINE` unset the compat test reports `Skipped`, never `Passed` — so a publish path that treats "not red" as "checked" reinstates exactly the gap this row is about **DELIVERED 2026-08-17 by the main session at the user's explicit instruction — this row said the file was the user's alone, and that convention was overridden by them, not by me.** **`needs:` cannot express the requirement, and that is the first thing to know**: GitHub `needs:` chains jobs *within* one workflow, and `ci.yml` is a different workflow, so the shape this row proposed does not exist. Implemented instead as a cheap `gate` job that asks the API for the latest **completed** `ci.yml` run on **`github.sha`** and requires `conclusion == success`, with `publish` behind `needs: gate`. Two properties it was built for: **zero runs found is a FAILURE, not a skip** — the state this row exists about; and it reads the **latest** completed run rather than "any run that ever went green", so a success followed by a failure on the same SHA is not publishable. One consequence the operator must know: `ci.yml` triggers on push/PR to `main` only, so **a dispatch from a branch CI has never seen now fails** — correct, but it changes who can publish from where. Gate 2 is a step inside `publish` (the built candidate is already there; a separate job would rebuild everything to ask one question) running `Scripts/api_compat_check.py --no-build`, with the three exit codes handled separately: 0 publishes, 1 stops, **2 stops and can NEVER be overridden** because 2 means no comparison happened. A `workflow_dispatch` boolean `allow_breaking` bypasses **1 only**, for the case the CHANGELOG policy contemplates. **Tested, because syntax proves nothing about refusal**: 10 arms against fixtures — gate 1 with no run / failure / cancelled / success, gate 2 across all three exit codes x both override values — **10/10 as specified**, including `exit 2 + allow_breaking=true` still failing. `api_compat_check.py --no-build` was also run for real here: baseline `10.1.0`, verdict **`unchanged`**, exit 0, so gate 2 does not block a legitimate publish today. **What is NOT verified and cannot be from this box**: nothing ran on GitHub. `gh` is unauthenticated (`XC-5`), so the `gh api` call, the `github.token` permission (`actions: read`) and `jq`'s presence on the runner are all unexercised — and jq was stubbed locally because Git Bash does not ship it. **The first real dispatch is the test**, and it should be done on a commit whose CI is known green. *Instrument note worth keeping: bare `bash` on the dev box resolves to WSL's, which cannot see a Windows path and reported a syntax error on every step including the ones untouched — the untouched ones failing is the canary that the instrument broke rather than the file.* **Found in passing, NOT fixed, no row yet**: `dotnet nuget push --skip-duplicate` treats an already-published version as success by design, so a dispatch that forgot the version bump packs the same numbers, pushes nothing, and **goes green** — the same silent shape as this row, one step further down. | `XC-60` | **OPEN — found by `overfit-architect` 2026-08-15 while planning `XC-58`, arithmetic re-derived independently by the main session** | **the attention scratch is sized to the MODEL's context length, never to the `maxContextLength` the caller asked for — so asking for a small context saves nothing** | `CachedLlamaInferenceEngine` builds `_stack` **in its constructor** with `config.ContextLength` (`:139`). `CreateSession(int? maxContextLength = null, …)` then computes `var ctx = maxContextLength ?? _config.ContextLength` (`:379`) and spends it on the **`KeyValueCache` only** — by then the stack, and every `CachedSingleHeadAttention._scoreScratch = new float[maxSequenceLength]` inside it (`CachedSingleHeadAttention.cs:77`), is already allocated at the model's full context. **A caller who asks for 2048 on a 32768-context model still pays for 32768.** Arithmetic, derived twice and agreeing: `float[32768]` = 128 KiB per head, and Qwen2.5-3B has 36 layers × 16 heads = 576 heads → **72.0 MiB of scratch that the caller's own parameter was supposed to bound**, inside a total of 86.4 MiB per stack. Right-sized to a 2048 context the whole stack is **18.9 MiB**. **Why this matters beyond the waste**: it inverts the trade-off in `XC-58`. Giving every session its own stack looks like +86.4 MiB per session and therefore unaffordable — but `overfit serve` runs **one session per engine**, so per-stack right-sizing plus a private stack is a net **saving of 67.5 MiB per client**, with break-even around 4.6 sessions per engine. *"(b) costs memory"* is only true of the version nobody would write. **This repository's stated identity includes low-end hardware and minimising PEAK RAM at load** — see the zero-alloc/low-end rule — which is exactly the constraint this defect violates, quietly, on every model whose trained context exceeds what the caller wants. **Not measured, only derived**: no allocation profile was taken (the machine was busy); the figures are arithmetic over constructors that were read. Confirm with a real measurement before quoting the saving anywhere outside this row |
| `XC-58` | **DONE 2026-08-16 — guard implemented, three mutations caught, shipping in 10.1.0. See the delivery note at the end of this row** | **`CachedLlamaInferenceEngine.CreateSession` hands the SAME `CachedGptStack` to every session it creates** | Found by `overfit-architect` while planning `XC-55`, and **verified independently against the source**: `_stack` is a single `private readonly CachedGptStack` field built once in the constructor (`CachedLlamaInferenceEngine.cs:40`, `:132`) and passed to every `CachedLlamaSession` (`:392`). Each session gets its **own** `KeyValueCache` — which is what makes the sharing invisible, because sessions genuinely are independent in the state everyone thinks about. **Two consequences.** (1) `session.LastHiddenState` returns whatever session decoded through this engine **last**, not what *this* session decoded — so on an engine with more than one session the property silently answers a different question than its name asks. (2) **Two sessions decoding concurrently corrupt each other's forward pass**, because the stack carries per-decode scratch and `_lastFinalHidden` / `_finalHidden`. `CreateSession` is `public` and its name implies independence; nothing in its documentation says otherwise. **Decide the contract before writing code, because both answers are defensible and they are different products**: either sessions are cheap views that must not be used concurrently — in which case say so in the XML doc, and consider a debug-only guard that detects concurrent entry — or a session owns its own stack, which costs the per-stack scratch per session and is the behaviour a reader already assumes. **Not a regression and not urgent for the current release** — the shape is as old as the engine — but it is the kind of defect that surfaces the first time a customer serves two conversations from one engine, which is exactly what an in-process embeddable runtime invites. Related: `XC-55` recorded that `LastHiddenState` has only two callers, both tests, so the *first* consequence is currently harmless; the second is not bounded that way **DELIVERED 2026-08-16, contract (a), in 10.1.0.** `CachedGptStack` takes an exclusive-use flag per synchronous entry and releases it in a `finally`; concurrent entry throws `OverfitRuntimeException` naming the entry point and saying what to do instead. **The nesting hazard was resolved STRUCTURALLY, which is the part worth keeping**: every guarded entry point is a thin façade that takes the flag and calls a private unguarded `…Core`, and `DecodeCore` calls `DecodeWithoutLogitsCore` / `ProjectLogitsCore` — so `Decode` cannot self-trip **by construction**, and no depth counter is needed. The architect had specifically forbidden a counter, because a counter without an owner identity admits the *second* session while the first is nested. **Nine entry points, not the eight I listed** — the developer enumerated them from the code rather than from the plan and found `LogitLensFromHidden`; code and plan then agreed exactly. **Three mutations, all caught, none green**: deleting the throw reddened 11 tests (all nine per-entry tests plus the cross-thread race test); dropping the `finally` on `Decode` reddened **46** across the LM runtime, including zero-allocation tests; and guarding `Decode` only reddened **exactly one** test, the predicted one — which is the evidence that the per-entry shape isolates, since a missed entry point lights its own test and nothing else. **The old "independence" test was vacuous and is replaced**: `MultipleSessionsFromSameEngine_Independenet` gave both sessions **the same prompt** and asserted the tokens matched, which holds identically whether or not every buffer is shared. The replacement interleaves two sessions with **different** prompts on one thread and additionally asserts the two standalone runs differ, so the comparison cannot pass vacuously; measured on a real Qwen engine, tokens identical to each session run alone. **Two limits stated rather than smoothed.** The cross-thread race test does **not** prove the exchange is atomic — a read-then-write flag would usually, not always, fail it — and that sits in both the test's and `EnterExclusive`'s doc. And `GetBlock(int)` is `public`, returns the shared `CachedTransformerBlock` and therefore **bypasses the guard**; `find_references` shows 3 sites, all in tests, zero production callers, and `Blocks` has none at all. Flagged, not fixed. **One anomaly reported instead of being explained away**: the first mutation also reddened an ONNX DAG zero-allocation test that cannot reach this code, in 1 run of 4, passing 5/5 in isolation — recorded as a probable fourth intermittent zero-alloc test and explicitly **not** proven. | `XC-54` | **DONE — IMPLEMENTED 2026-08-15 — awaiting review and the user's commit; see the delivery note at the end of this row** | **`Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` asserts on a PROCESS-WIDE quantity while 51 other tests share the process, so in an area run it measures the suite and not the pool** | Found 2026-08-15 while investigating whether the `OverfitParallel` park/wake rewrite (`9074647`, counted semaphore tokens → Monitor predicate) had regressed idle parking. `DecodePoolIdleBurnTests.Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` decodes 8 tokens to wake the pool, sleeps 3 s, and asserts `effectiveCores < 1.0` where `effectiveCores = Δ Process.TotalProcessorTime / Δ wall`. **`TotalProcessorTime` is the whole process**, and xunit v3 parallelises collections by default with no `xunit.runner.json` in this repo, so every other test executing during that 3-second window is counted as decode-pool spin. Measured: the area run reports **14.57 effective cores on a 32-logical-core box — 46% of the machine** — while the same test **passes in isolation**. The 14.57 is real CPU; it is simply not the pool's. **This is not a flaky test, it is a test measuring the wrong subject**, and the distinction matters because the two have opposite fixes. **Two consequences, and the second is the dangerous one.** (1) In any multi-test run the assertion fails regardless of the pool, so its red carries no information. (2) **In isolation it passes trivially** — a genuinely non-parking pool would still show ~0 cores if nothing else is running and the pool is the only thing that could spin, which it is, so isolation is the *valid* configuration — but that means **the only configuration where the test can fail is the one where it cannot mean anything, and the only configuration where it means something is the one where nobody runs it.** The `[LongFact]` gate runs areas, never single tests. **What this does and does not settle about `OverfitParallel`**: measured in the one configuration where the instrument is valid, the pool parks — so this failure is **not** evidence of a park regression. It is equally **not** proof that parking survives under concurrent load, because no admissible measurement of that exists yet. Do not record either claim as verified. **The fix is to measure the subject, not the process**: sample the pool's own worker threads (or a thread-time delta over the pool's threads) rather than `TotalProcessorTime`, or mark the test as requiring exclusive execution so no other collection shares its window — the second is cheaper and the first is honest. Whichever is chosen, **the assertion must be re-proved by breaking parking and watching it go red**, because today nothing demonstrates it can fail for the right reason. **Related and deliberately separate**: this is the same family as `TG-T12`/`TG-T13` — an assertion on an environment-produced quantity (time, CPU) rather than on a program-produced one — and the admissibility rule from `XC-52` applies unchanged: *a concurrency test is admissible iff every assertion holds under every legal schedule.* Here the legal schedule "another collection is running" breaks it **DELIVERED 2026-08-15 (`overfit-developer`, no plan — every architecture-checklist item is unchanged; test-only).** `Tests/TestSupport/ExclusiveProcessMeasurementCollection.cs` is the tree's **first** `[CollectionDefinition(DisableParallelization = true)]`, and the test now takes a **canary**: the same measurement over an equal window **before the pool is woken**, with `Assert.SkipWhen` reporting *could not measure* on a busy baseline rather than passing or failing. **Four measured arms.** (1) Post-fix, 110-test area (this test + the whole analyzer suite): baseline **0.01**, idle **0.08** cores, PASS. (2) **Positive control** — same area with only the `[Collection]` attribute removed: baseline **1.10** cores, so the canary skipped with "cannot measure" and the pre-fix assertion would have failed on ambient CPU alone, at a threshold of 1.0, with the pool parked. (3) **Park mutation** (`OverfitParallel.cs:868`, `spins < DecodeSpinBudgetIterations` → `spins < int.MaxValue`, so workers spin instead of parking): baseline **0.00**, idle **10.02** cores, RED with exactly the message this test exists to produce — quiet baseline + busy window is the one combination that means what it says. (4) Restored: baseline 0.00, idle 0.08-0.12, PASS. **Fast-suite cost is not measurable**: interleaved ABAB, with/without the attribute, 23/23 and 23/24 s — the member is `[ModelFact]` and skips by default, so the serialised collection is empty in a fast run. **What is still NOT proved**: that parking survives under concurrent load. The fix makes the window quiet rather than measuring the pool's own threads, so the honest claim is unchanged from this row's original wording — and external load biases this counter **downwards** (a starved spinner accumulates less CPU per wall second), which is the direction that produces a false GREEN. Both caveats are in the test's docstring. Stale comment fixed while here: `ModelFact`'s doc claimed `Assert.Skip` is a compile error on xUnit 2.9.3; the repo is on `xunit.v3` 3.2.2 and it compiles |
| `XC-42` | **DONE 2026-08-14 — the refusal now names its holder** | killing a stalled test run orphans the xUnit v3 test executable, which keeps the machine-measurement mutex and silently refuses every later run | Diagnosed end to end 2026-08-13. **The chain**: a `[LongFact]` run stalled (alive, 2.73 s CPU total, zero over a 5-second sample, working set flat at 15.4 MB for 8.5 minutes — not slow, blocked); the vstest and testhost parents were killed; and **`DevOnBike.Overfit.Tests.exe` survived as an orphan** — xUnit v3 makes the test project an executable, so it is a grandchild the kill did not reach (PID 26056, parent 31444, alive 40 minutes after its parent died). It held `Global\DevOnBike.Overfit.MachineMeasurement`, so `MeasurementExclusion` could not acquire, called `Environment.Exit(2)` **exactly as designed** — and every subsequent `dotnet test` reported `Exception discovering tests: Test process did not return valid JSON (non-object)` and *"No test is available"*. **`XC-17` is the reason that was unreadable**, and this is its cost measured rather than argued: the guard's sentence about a measurement in progress never reaches the operator through the VSTest bridge, so the visible symptom points at the test discoverer instead of at a held lock. Diagnosis took a process-ancestry walk, a mutex probe and a command-line search; the marker-file fix `XC-17` proposes would have answered it in one read. **Verified fixed**: orphan killed → the mutex object disappeared → discovery returned 2892 tests in 16.0 s. **Two things to build, and the first is small**: have the refusal write a marker file naming the holder (`XC-17`'s own recommendation, now with a second incident behind it), and make whatever kills a test run kill the process TREE — `AbandonedMutexException` is already handled correctly, so the failure mode is not a dead owner but a live orphan. **Also worth recording as a first**: this suite's discovery costs ~16 s, which nobody had measured either. **The one-glance diagnostic, which nobody had until the before-picture existed**: a healthy run is a **matched pair** — `DevOnBike.Overfit.Tests.exe` and a `testhost.exe` with the same start time. The `XC-42` signature is the test executable **alive with no `testhost` beside it**. Search by name pattern (`Name -match 'Overfit|Tests'`), never by process class: three separate filters built on `testhost|vstest|Benchmark` found nothing while the orphan sat in plain view. **SECOND OCCURRENCE THE SAME DAY, and it is a different mechanism worth separating: a long job started by a SUBAGENT dies when that subagent goes idle.** The batched re-run reached 2 of 44 areas (112 tests, all passing, 0.6 min) and stopped — no test process, no python batch driver, no further log line — after the agent that launched it went idle. The first attempt ended the same way. **The operational rule that follows: a run measured in hours must be owned by the session that persists**, i.e. the main session, not by a subagent whose lifetime ends when it stops writing. A subagent can *analyse* a long run's output; it should not *hold* it. **The durable log is what saved this from being invisible** — `Tests/bin/longfact-timings.log` still carries both completed batches with their timings, so the loss is bounded to the batch that was in flight rather than to the whole run. That is the pattern to keep: any long job writes per-unit progress to a file, so its death costs one unit rather than everything. **Root cause of the original stall is still UNKNOWN** — the hanging test has not been named, and a candidate worth checking first is anything driving a `TextReader` that never ends, since `McpServer.Run`'s own comment says `input.ReadLine()` cannot be interrupted **CLOSED 2026-08-14 — the refusal writes `Tests/bin/measurement-refusal.txt`.** It carries the timestamp, the mutex name, the **pid and command line of the run that was refused**, the sentence that exit 2 means something **ALIVE** holds the lock (never abandoned — that case is already caught and treated as acquired), the `Get-CimInstance … -match 'Overfit|Tests'` search **by name pattern**, the matched-pair-versus-orphan signature with "kill the process TREE", and the `XC-17` explanation of why the console said nothing. **Live candidate processes are enumerated at refusal time**, and a test-assembly process with no `testhost` anywhere is annotated `<- ORPHAN SIGNATURE` — which is exactly the reading that took a process-ancestry walk, a mutex probe and a command-line search to reach by hand. Extended to `Sources/Benchmark/Program.cs`, which had the same unreadable exit 2. **The refusal path is tested end to end, not only in pieces**: `DevOnBike.Overfit.Tests.exe -list classes` constructs the xunit v3 framework so the guard runs and refuses, without executing a test — 225 ms, exit 2, against a genuinely live holder (the suite itself). **The seam was found, not manufactured.** 10 tests, 3 mutations all caught, including one that survives a **stale marker sitting at the path**, which is what the freshness assertion exists for. **A GREEN mutation was found in the developer's own new test and fixed before shipping**: a premise check skipped the test when the candidate list matched nothing — and that list is what the test tests, so emptying it made the test **disable itself** rather than fail. Suite **2628/0/274** |

| `XC-38` | OPEN | **a third intermittent test, and the run that caught it did not capture the number** | `Trees.XgboostParityTests.Prediction_IsZeroAllocation_AfterWarmup` failed once on 2026-08-13 and did not reproduce in five subsequent full runs, including one with the new test filtered out. It is **not** on the known-flaky list, which makes it the third after `XC-10`/`XC-13`/`XC-19` — and unlike those it is not a numerical-training test. **Cross-test interference is ruled out by construction**: the assertion reads `GC.GetAllocatedBytesForCurrentThread()` (`Tests/Trees/XgboostParityTests.cs:146,155`), which is thread-isolated. So the candidate mechanisms are a warm-up that did not complete, a tiered-JIT recompile allocating on the measured thread, or a genuine allocation on a path taken rarely. **What makes this row weaker than it should be: the byte count from the failing run was not captured** — the output filter kept only the test name — so how far over the floor it went, and therefore whether it was a near-miss or a real allocation, is unknown. **First action is not a fix**: make the assertion report the actual figure on failure, so the next occurrence is diagnostic instead of a second sighting. **THAT PREMISE WAS WRONG, and the correction is worth more than the task. Verified 2026-08-14: all four rows' assertions ALREADY report their numbers**, and always did — `AssertAllocation.NoPerCallAllocation` at `HEAD` prints `{allocatedBytes} B allocated in the measured loop`, `Gpt1QLoRATests.cs:180` prints `{first:F3} -> {last:F3}`, `PromptCacheReuseTests.cs:148` uses `Assert.Equal` (which renders the actual) plus an explicit `WriteLine` of the diff, and the two tests added on 2026-08-13 both carry the number. **The byte count was lost by the RUN'S OUTPUT FILTER, not by the test** — which `XC-19`'s row had already said of itself and which this row then contradicted. **The filter was mine**: the first batched `[LongFact]` runner wrote only failing NAMES to the log; the second version, written the same evening, dumps each batch's full output to `Tests/bin/longfact-out/`, which is why four areas have files there and the `Anomalies` batch — run under the first version — has none. So the real defect was fixed before this task was dispatched, and the brief I wrote from this row asked for the wrong repair. **What was built instead is genuinely useful and was reported as adding no coverage**: a new `NoPerCallAllocation(label, iterations, body)` overload that owns the loop, measures it **in two halves** and reports gen0/1/2 deltas — which discriminates the three candidate mechanisms a single total cannot. Front-loaded bytes mean tier-up noise that outgrew the floor; uniform bytes mean a real per-call allocation; non-zero GC counts mean another thread allocated inside the window. Mutation run on **both** arms (an injected allocation in `PredictBatch`): pre-change RED with the total alone, post-change RED with `first half: 600 B over 25`, `second half: 600 B over 25 (24.0 B/call)`, `gen0 +0`. **The old check would have caught it just as well** — the developer said so plainly, which is the sentence that stops this being read as new coverage. The other 16 call sites are untouched. Suite 2628/0/274. The same applies to the other three rows — four non-reproducing failures with no captured numbers between them is a pattern about the harness as much as about the tests **TWO MORE JOINED THE CLASS 2026-08-13, and this time the isolation check was run rather than assumed.** `LanguageModels.Diagnostics.DecodePoolIdleBurnTests.Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` and `…PrefillCallCountTests.Prefill_ComponentCallCounts_AreOncePerLayer_NotOncePerHead` both failed inside a full-area `[LongFact]` batch (`rc=1`, **no timeout**, so not an artefact of a killed process) and **both pass in isolation, twice each** — 19.9 s / 19.7 s and 22.6 s / 22.3 s. So the class is now six, and it splits into two mechanisms rather than one: `Pool_Parks_WhenIdle` asserts that a pool **parks when idle**, which is a wall-clock property and plausibly load-sensitive like the others; but `Prefill_ComponentCallCounts` counts **calls per layer**, which should be deterministic under any load. A count that is right alone and wrong beside its neighbours points at **shared state between tests in the same assembly** — a static counter reset by one test while another reads it — not at timing. **That is a test-design defect and a different fix from a tolerance or a fake clock**, so the two should not be swept together. Next step for the second one is to run its containing class alone, then the area, and see at which level it turns red |
| `XC-39` | **DONE — DECIDED 2026-08-13 — the checklist is the line, not the size of the change** | the plan gate has no stated answer for a task whose remedy is already written down outside `docs/specs/` | Raised by `overfit-developer` against itself on 2026-08-13, which is the part worth keeping. `XC-23`'s registry row prescribed the fix in one sentence, the shape to mirror sat 290 lines up in the same file, and the agent's own definition says to stop and refuse without a signed plan. **Following it literally delivers nothing on a task with no open design question; ignoring it is the rationalisation that same section warns about.** It implemented under the "pointed at directly, shape does not change" exemption and compensated by reporting every architecture-checklist answer explicitly — which is the behaviour you would want, but it was a judgement call it should not have had to make alone. **Two ways to settle it and they are genuinely different.** (a) Extend the exemption: "a change whose remedy is fully specified in a `docs/TASKS.md` row AND which mirrors an existing shape in the same file AND adds no public API — provided the report states every checklist answer." (b) State the opposite plainly: a registry row is **not** a signed plan, so the next agent blocks without agonising. **The argument for (b) is that the row itself said this was a behaviour change and deliberately not swept** — so the row was flagging care, not granting approval. **The argument for (a) is that the alternative here would have been a plan file restating one sentence.** Decide it once; leaving it implicit means each agent re-derives it, and they will not all reach the same answer **DECIDED: neither (a) nor (b) as written — the line is the architecture checklist itself.** A developer may proceed without a plan when **every** checklist item is answerable **"unchanged"** — execution path, allocation policy, AOT reach, ownership, assembly direction, public API, quality requirements, threading — **and writes each of those answers into the report**. One item that is not "unchanged" and the gate applies in full. **Why not "small changes are exempt"**: a three-line change can add public API or move an allocation onto a hot path, and "small" is the judgement that fails under time pressure, while "every box says unchanged, and here they are" is checkable by somebody who was not there. Written into `.claude/agents/overfit-developer.md` with the required wording (*"no plan; every checklist item is unchanged, as follows"*) so a reviewer can disagree with the claim rather than reconstruct that one was made. `XC-23` would have passed this test — which is the point: it was the right call made without a rule |
