This is the row's most persuasive datum. **(3) `Demo/VoiceClone`'s `OVERFIT040` pragma is LIVE, now measured rather than assumed** — 0 sites with it, **1 site with it neutralised** (`Program.cs:376`), so a sweep acting on the census's "0 sites" would have deleted a real suppression. **Recommendations**: ADD `Sources/AndroidBench` (after fixing the two `..` slices — plain `net10.0`, no workload, builds on Linux, highest value and cheapest), `Demo/SkillEvalConsole` (zero diagnostics), `Demo/VoiceClone` (zero diagnostics, and it brings its live pragma into every future census), and `Demo/VoiceLoop` (after the one-character fix plus `<EnableWindowsTargeting>true</EnableWindowsTargeting>`, the property `Demo/MnistWpfDemo` already uses so the ubuntu CI leg can build it). STAY OUT: `Demo/AndroidBenchApp` (needs the android workload and a machine-specific SDK path; both CI legs build the whole solution and would break) and both `Templates` projects (`NU1008`, now covered by `Scripts/check_template_pins.py` instead). **One premise of this row corrected**: `Demo/OverfitChatApp` is **not** unguarded — `.github/workflows/overthink-playstore.yml` compiles it with the analyzers wired in, though only on `workflow_dispatch`; the better lever there is a CI job that installs the android workload, not solution membership. **Why it stops here**: adding a project under `Sources/` to the build perimeter is an assembly-and-dependency-direction decision, and nobody has decided whether `Sources/AndroidBench` is expected to be AOT-clean or to meet the `Sources/` rules — adding it would answer that by accident. It also changes what CI builds on both legs. **The durable artefact is recorded in `docs/measured-baselines.md`**: the analyzer census covers 26 of 34 projects, so every "swept to zero" claim in this repository is a claim about the solution |
| `PB-ORT1` | **DONE — MEASURED 2026-08-12 (the numbers do not separate), and the PIN WAS THEN TAKEN 2026-08-17: 1.28.0 → 1.29.0. The decision to hold was reversed on its own evidence — see the reversal note at the very end of this row** | take `Microsoft.ML.OnnxRuntime` 1.28.0 → 1.29.0, or decide not to, by measuring | **This package is in no Overfit code path.** Its only consumer is `Sources/Benchmark`, where it is the **other side** of the comparison — so bumping it moves published Overfit-vs-ORT ratios **without a line of Overfit changing**. Analysed 2026-08-12 from the artefacts, not the notes. **The managed assembly is IL-identical** (the package ships no managed DLL; `Microsoft.ML.OnnxRuntime.Managed` differs by zero IL bytes), so there is **no compile risk** — the entire change is the native binary, `onnxruntime.dll` 15,809,848 → 16,149,344 (**+339 KB, unaudited**). Release dated by the GitHub API, not the rendered page: **1.29.0 shipped 2026-08-12**, eighteen days after 1.28.0, so it was hours old when analysed. **The MLAS work everyone points at is off our path**: all of it is `MatMulNBits` — 2-bit weight kernels, fp16 — plus Arm64/RISC-V, while every model these benchmarks generate is **dense fp32** (`Gemm`/`Relu`/`Conv`/`MaxPool` only). What *can* move us was not previously named: reduced small-MatMul batch allocations (#29085, #29690 — exactly Linear(784,10) and the MLP) and reduced eager session/process init (#29880, #29755, #30889 — aimed straight at cold start). The env-var item (#29688) does **not** change default thread counts; it lets `ORT_INTRA_OP_NUM_THREADS`/`ORT_INTER_OP_NUM_THREADS` set them, both absent here — **but machine/user-level environment was NOT checked, and that is a five-second check before the run.** Eleven classes construct a session; **nine pin `IntraOpNumThreads=1, InterOpNumThreads=1`, two do not** — `InferenceBenchmark:41` and `ColdStartBenchmark:46` are bare `new InferenceSession(path)`. **A forced departure from the discipline doc**: single-process ABAB is impossible, because one process loads one native `onnxruntime.dll` and the managed identity is unchanged — interleave at the **process** level. **The canary is free**: Overfit does not change by a byte, so each class's own `Overfit_*` arm is an untouched path measured in the same process; if it moves beyond its error bar, the box moved and that pair is discarded. `ColdStartBenchmark` gets a second: `MLNet_ColdStart` also routes through ORT. **Run**: `BatchScalingBenchmark`, `ImportedOnnxMnistCnnBenchmark`, `ColdStartBenchmark`, then `ConcurrentInferenceBenchmark` only if the first three are clean; N = 3 A/B process pairs, best-of-N both sides, `RatioSD > 0.15` is not evidence. **Prove the lever is live** before believing a null — print `OrtEnv.Instance().GetVersionString()` or check the resolved DLL size, because a stale `bin/` copy of the native asset would run 1.28.0 in both arms and produce a flat 1.00. **Hypothesis, stated before the number exists**: 1.29.0 leaves the fp32 dense CPU path unchanged and touches only quantized/fp16/Arm64 kernels and session init. **Refuted by** any consistent canary-clean movement in the steady-state `OnnxRuntime_Batch*` or `OnnxRuntime_MnistCnn_PreAllocated` arms; **confirmed by** movement confined to cold start. **Take it and restate every affected ratio** if canaries are flat and an ORT arm moves consistently — you are not entitled to publish against a stale baseline. Cost: 6 processes × 4 classes plus rebuilds; the binding constraint is exclusive access to the machine. **RESULT, 6 clean A/B pairs, alternating AB/BA, one box state, one runtime (.NET 10.0.11):** every steady-state ORT arm is faster on 1.29.0 — `Batch1` 0.9938, `Batch16` 0.9848, `Batch64` 0.9936, `Batch256` 0.9946, `MnistCnn` 0.9974 — **and it means nothing, because the canaries move the same way and `Overfit_Batch64` moves FURTHER than any ORT arm at 0.9825.** Movement that is not canary-clean cannot be attributed. **Refutation condition NOT met, so the hypothesis stands unrefuted; confirmation branch unanswerable** because `ColdStartBenchmark` cannot resolve it (BDN raises `MinIterationTime` on all three arms — one iteration is 1.07 ms against a 100 ms target, run-to-run spread 23%). `InferenceBenchmark` came in at 0.9648 with a **61% within-version spread**, exactly the "wrong job for the workload" failure predicted before the run. **RESOLVING POWER, stated before the verdict: median canary spread 4.26% on the clean box, ~1.3% on the best-behaved class. The ORT effect is 0.3-1.5%, entirely under the floor — the experiment is SILENT in that band, not negative.** **THE FINDING THAT OUTLIVES THE PACKAGE, and it refutes what the main session predicted:** quieting the box did **not** tighten the floor. Canary spread was **median 3.17% / worst 12.54% on the noisy box** (~30 processes, 3.8 GB of idle preview-SDK MSBuild workers, pending Windows updates) against **median 4.26% / worst 24.94% after a reboot with one process running**. Slightly *worse* clean. So the ±3-4% cross-process floor here is **intrinsic to the measurement, not caused by background load**, and no further quieting rescues it — an effect smaller than a few percent needs a different experiment shape, not a cleaner machine. Caveat stated: the retired set is not homogeneously noisy (p1-p2 loaded, p3 straddles the quieting, p4-p6 quiet), so this is suggestive rather than decisive — but no reading of it supports the memory-pressure hypothesis. **Three instrument findings kept**: (1) BDN builds a **generated child project carrying its own copy of the native asset**, and that is the copy the measuring process loads — the host's `bin` proves nothing, and with `--keepFiles` the previous arm's binary waits there for the next arm's check to pass on it; (2) **ABAB at pair level does not break the position/version confound** — every A precedes its own B, so "1.29 is faster" and "the second process is faster" are the same observation; alternating AB/BA is required, and this cost six pairs; (3) an A/B asserts the identity of the thing it varies and **assumes everything else** — the native DLL was asserted 48 times while the .NET runtime changed underneath the run and was caught only afterwards, from `HostEnvironmentInfo`. **Still unmeasured and now cheap:** .NET 10.0.10 was reinstalled alongside 10.0.11, so the servicing release's own JIT effect is measurable again — but pinning the patch runtime of a BDN child process needs a job or toolchain change in `Sources/Benchmark`, which is a deliberate edit to the measurement subject and was refused mid-experiment. Raw arms under `.claude/ort-measure/` (clean) and `.claude/ort-measure-retired/` (contaminated, kept as the floor evidence) **ONE OBLIGATION REMAINS OPEN, found 2026-08-13 while re-reading this decision: `docs/measured-baselines.md` carries no ONNX Runtime entry at all.** Every published Overfit-vs-ORT ratio was measured against **1.28.0**, and a reader will assume the version they would install — today 1.29.0. The measurement bounds the flattery at **≤1.5%**, i.e. under the noise floor, so no ratio needs restating; what needs fixing is that the version is not written down beside the number. That is the actual risk of holding a pin on the other side of a comparison, and it costs one line. **CLOSED 2026-08-13**: `docs/measured-baselines.md` now opens with a "what the comparisons are measured AGAINST" table naming ORT 1.28.0, the `PB-ORT1` result, and the resolving power that bounds the flattery at ≤1.5%. **Writing it surfaced the same defect on the other comparison**: the `~1.13× uniform` llama.cpp row records **no build, commit or quantisation** for the llama.cpp side, and nothing in this repository does. Left as a stated gap rather than guessed at — anyone citing that ratio should say they do not know which build it was against. The pin comment in `Directory.Packages.props` was corrected the same day — it had been left describing the re-run as pending after it had happened, and carried two claims this measurement refuted (the env-var item does not change default thread counts; the MLAS work is `MatMulNBits`/fp16/Arm64, off the dense-fp32 path these benchmarks exercise) **REVERSED 2026-08-17 — the pin is now 1.29.0, and the reversal came from re-reading this row's own reasoning rather than from new data.** The user asked why we were not bumping, which is the right question, because **the hold does not survive its own measurement.** Its stated reason was *"the honest reason to hold is that it moves the other side of every published ratio"* — true in principle and **refuted in magnitude by the paragraph above it**: the ORT-side effect is 0.3–1.5% against a 4.26% cross-process floor. Made concrete: the largest published claim is **7.31× faster than ORT**; a 1.5%-faster ORT makes it **7.20×**, a smaller move than the error bar on the 7.31× itself. **The pin was defending the numbers against a change nobody can measure.** Second, the remedy this row chose *instead* of bumping — *"state the ORT version beside any published number … NOT YET DONE"* — **was delivered on 2026-08-13**, so the alternative had been completed and what remained was a pin with no argument under it. **The question the row never asked, and which is the actual decision**: what is the pin FOR? A **current** comparison (a reader means today's ORT, and the baselines table's own header says exactly that) or a **frozen comparator** (tracking our progress against an unmoving other side, which our own baselines already do). Answered: current. **The five benchmark classes were deliberately NOT re-run** — the flattery is bounded at ≤1.5%, under the ±3–4% floor of the measurement that produced the ratios, so no published number changes detectably; `docs/measured-baselines.md` now says in as many words that the build is 1.29.0 while the ratios are 1.28.0 numbers. **The bump was verified live rather than assumed**, using this row's own rule about a stale native asset: `Sources/Benchmark/bin/Release/net10.0/runtimes/win-x64/native/onnxruntime.dll` resolves to **16,149,344 B**, exactly the 1.29.0 size this row recorded against 1.28.0's 15,809,848. `Microsoft.ML.OnnxRuntime` has **one** `PackageReference` in the whole solution (`Sources/Benchmark/Benchmarks.csproj:23`), which is what makes the blast radius of this bump exactly zero outside the benchmark project. *Also settled the same day: there is no version newer than 1.29.0 — checked against the NuGet flat-container index and the upstream GitHub releases, both give 1.29.0, published 2026-08-12.* **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-17 at the user's instruction, after the bump.** The reasoning above said a re-run was not *needed*; it was run anyway, and it confirms the reasoning rather than overturning it. **Both published ratios hold on 1.29.0**: `Linear(784→10)` **8.29×** (README says ~8.0×, so it is understated) and concurrent 8-thread **3.63×** (README says ~3.6×), with zero allocation on the Overfit side against 224 B and **117 MB** respectively. Full numbers, provenance and caveats are in `docs/measured-baselines.md` under *"Overfit vs ONNX Runtime 1.29.0"*. **One methodological point this run settles for future readers of this row**: the Overfit-vs-ORT ratio is a **within-process** comparison, both arms in one BDN process, so the ±3–4% cross-process floor this row spent six process pairs establishing **does not govern it** — that floor belongs to the version-vs-version experiment. The ratio's error bar is `RatioSD`, here 0.02 and 0.05. Conversely the *absolute* ORT figure moved 1,883 → 1,962.7 ns against the README's number, which **is** a cross-run comparison and sits squarely in the noise band, so it must not be read as a 1.29.0 regression. **Filed out of this run: `XC-77`** — the same suite measures 1.06× on a 3-layer MLP, and only the 8× is published |

It drifts silently and will keep drifting: the survey that just moved the rest of the Extensions.AI train to 10.9.0 could not touch it, because it is invisible to `dotnet list package`. Two further consequences worth stating: a scaffolded app therefore starts life on a version nobody chose, and if the template were ever added to the solution it would fail `NU1008` for being outside CPM. **Why it is deliberately excluded and must stay so**: template content may reference only published NuGet packages — a path into this repo would not exist in a scaffolded app, which is the reason recorded in `Directory.Build.props:53-54`. Offline by design, with the reason in its own docstring: "latest on nuget.org" is the wrong bar for a repo that holds packages back deliberately, and a checker needing the network is one CI turns off. Proven in three arms, the third being the one that matters — blinding `audit` to return no findings makes all six seeded self-test cases report silence and the script **refuses to print a clean tree**, exit 2. Nine seeded cases plus three parser round-trips including the `<Version>` **child-element** form, which a checker matching only the attribute form would have been blind to. **CORRECTION by the main session, from the number the checker cannot fetch: anchor #1 is the wrong anchor for template content.** Published on nuget.org is **10.0.31**; this repository builds **10.1.0, unpublished**. So the actionable bump is **10.0.29 → 10.0.31**, and following anchor #1 to 10.1.0 would make the template **unusable** — template content may reference only published packages. Comparing against `Directory.Build.props`'s `<Version>` therefore reports a drift that is the normal state during development; the cheap repair is in the message, not in going online. **And the gap underneath all of it is STRUCTURAL, not a missing gate**: `Templates/content/OverfitChat` **cannot be built in this repository at all** — `dotnet build` on it returns `NU1008`, because CPM forbids the inline versions template content is required to have. So no amount of wiring closes it here; the only thing that would catch a breaking change between 10.0.29 and 10.0.31 is **scaffolding from the published package and compiling that, outside this repo**. Until something does, every template pin bump is a change whose correctness nobody has checked. **Pins deliberately NOT changed — that is a release decision** |
| `AN-D14` | **DONE 2026-08-13 — and the prescribed fix was not sufficient** | an empty rule trace means "no built-in rule configured", never "no rule fired" — and nothing says so | Found 2026-08-12 while writing `<param>` docs for CS1573, which is the whole point of that being worth doing: the compiler cannot see this and neither could a reader, because the diagnostic **looks complete**. `ruleTrace` covers only `AnomalyGuardOptions.Rules`, the built-in metrics. A `CustomMetricBinding` carrying a `Rule` is evaluated by the **same detector** and reaches the **same pipeline** (`AnomalyGuard.cs:761-771`) and emits **no trace row at all**. So on a deployment whose rules are all custom — which the lab's five channels are — the rule trace is empty every cycle, and an operator reading it concludes no rule fired when the truth is that no built-in rule was configured. **This is the silence-reads-as-health shape the subsystem is organised against**, arriving through the diagnostic rather than the detector. `trendTrace` has no such gap: it reports custom channels, and that asymmetry is now documented on both params as the interim. **Why it is not a docs fix**: closing it changes what the guard emits, so it takes the nine-step protocol — a positive fixture (a custom rule that fires, producing a row), a negative (a custom rule that does not, producing a row saying so), and the distinction between an empty trace and an absent one. Deliberately NOT bundled into the CS1573 pass **DONE 2026-08-13. `ruleTrace` is threaded into `RunCustom` and a row is emitted per (pod, custom rule) for EVERY verdict, matching the built-in path. No contract change — `RuleDecisionTrace.Signal` is documented as a channel name and takes a custom binding's name verbatim; only its doc was touched.** **The brief the main session wrote was incomplete, and the developer caught it**: `RunCustom` `continue`s past a binding entirely when `PodsReporting(binding.Name) == 0`, so threading the callback into the `binding.Rule is { } rule` branch alone would still have left a **configured rule emitting no row on exactly the cycles where a broken exporter is the thing to find** — the property would not have held. Closed with `TraceUnreportedRule`, which emits one `InsufficientData` row per pod carrying the gates and **without reading the series**: `MetricWindow.Series(pod, name)` throws for a channel the window does not carry (`MetricWindow.cs:140-144`), which is the misconfigured-binding case, so the "make it symmetric with the built-in path" repair would have turned a silent channel into a failed cycle. There is a test for that. **So the trace now distinguishes: a rule that fired, a rule that did not, and a rule that could not be evaluated — and an empty trace means no rule of any kind is configured anywhere.** Seven tests; **seven mutation arms, all caught, none green**, including `ARM 0` — the pre-change file from `HEAD` run against the new tests, which went **5 of 7 red**, the two survivors being properties that genuinely held before. That arm is what shows the work added coverage rather than moved it. New lines **33/33 covered**; the last one needed a test written for it rather than left. Suite **2597/0/273**, verified independently by the main session. **Residual, deliberately not done and open for a decision**: the distinction is structural in the guard but not visible in the LOG — `AnomalyGuardService` emits rule-trace lines only when `OVERFIT_GUARD_PEER_TRACE` is set, so "trace off" and "trace on, nothing configured" are both zero lines. Proposed fix is one startup line naming how many built-in profiles and custom rules the trace covers; left because testing it means hosting the `BackgroundService` and an untested log line is worth little. Also pre-existing and unchanged: `LogRuleDecision` filters on an exact signal name, so a mistyped channel gives zero lines and reads like "no rule on that channel". **Not applicable, recorded rather than blank**: protocol step 9 (nothing deployed, no cluster contacted) and step 6 (no new metric, channel or query); no AOT publish, since `AotSmokeTest` does not reference `Anomalies`; no live arm — every positive is a fixture |

That task made the distinction **structural in the guard** — a configured rule now emits a row whether it fires, does not fire, or cannot be evaluated, so an empty trace means no rule is configured anywhere. **It did not make it visible to a reader of the log.** `AnomalyGuardService` emits rule-trace lines only when `OVERFIT_GUARD_PEER_TRACE` is set, so an operator looking at a log file cannot tell the trace was switched off from the trace being on with nothing to say — the same two-meanings-for-one-silence shape `AN-D14` closed one level up, arriving through the transport rather than the detector. **Proposed fix, and its size is the reason this is a separate row**: one startup line beside `_restored` naming how many built-in profiles and how many custom rules the trace will cover. Shared with the peer and trend traces, so a fix belongs in all three or in none |

The alternative — keep `internal` and add an IVT to the `overfit` executable — is explicitly called defensible and one line away if the reviewer prefers it. **No seam, no interface**: the testability is already in the constructor. **Arrival costs, measured rather than reasoned**: AOT reachability unchanged (`AotSmokeTest` references only `Main`), and `HttpListener` under Anomalies' `IsAotCompatible`/`IsTrimmable`/`TreatWarningsAsErrors` set produces **zero** new trim diagnostics — proved with a throwaway probe whose negative control (`GetType().GetMethods`, `Activator.CreateInstance`) failed as `IL2075`/`IL2057` errors, so the clean result is the code and not a silent analyzer. Two analyzer rules differ (`OVERFIT033`, `OVERFIT034`) and both are already satisfied; Anomalies bans 19 symbols to Cli's 7 and the file violates none. **Three real new warnings the row missed**: `Anomalies` sets `GenerateDocumentationFile`, so `TryStart`'s partial `<param>` coverage raises CS1573 for `telemetry`, `logger`, `clock` — write them as part of the move. Call-site churn is **zero**, not "one using". Also flagged: five entries in `Sources/Cli/BannedSymbols.txt` argue their case by naming `GuardMetricsEndpoint`, and that prose describes a type in another assembly after the move — rewrite the rationale, do not delete the bans. **FINDING 3, and it changes what the test can claim**: the comment justifying the `OVERFIT046` fix names a concrete race — a `/suppressions` scrape enumerating state while a cycle mutates it — and **that case is not reachable**. All three members the endpoint touches take the same lock (`RunCycleAsync` `AnomalyGuard.cs:462`, `Acknowledge` `:501`, `ActiveSuppressions` `:544`), so a concurrent scrape blocks rather than enumerating a mutating list, and `GuardTelemetry.Render` walks a fixed array. The backstop is still correct and the move is still right, but **the test must pin the property (a faulted serve task must not silently end the metrics channel), not reproduce that race**. Correcting the comment belongs to `overfit-reviewer`, separately. Not checked by the architect: whether every other writer of `SuppressionStore` takes `_gate` — three entry points were verified, not the whole assembly **IMPLEMENTED 2026-08-12, and the endpoint has tests for the first time.** `public sealed class GuardMetricsEndpoint : IDisposable` in `DevOnBike.Overfit.Anomalies.Hosting`; the Cli file deleted; **call-site churn zero** as the plan predicted. Four tests in `Tests/Anomalies/GuardMetricsEndpointTests.cs`, 132 ms. **The property is pinned, not the unreachable race**: a `ThrowingClock` raises `InvalidOperationException` inside `SuppressionsAsync`, `/metrics` must answer 200 BEFORE (the capability check, so a dead channel cannot pass by being dead all along) and 200 AFTER carrying `overfit_guard_last_cycle_timestamp_seconds`, with exactly one `LogLevel.Error` naming it as not a transport fault. The clock counts its own reads, so *"the handler actually ran"* is asserted independently — a fixed sleep there would have made the assertion pass for free on a slow box. **Mutation**: narrowing the catch-all to `when (ex is HttpListenerException or ObjectDisposedException)` — the pre-fix shape — fails **exactly that one test** of the four, anchor matched once, restore byte-verified, no green mutation. **CS1573 was measured, not assumed**: the three `<param>` tags were written, the `telemetry` one deleted, and the rebuild produced `GuardMetricsEndpoint.cs(99,28): warning CS1573` before it was restored. **One change beyond the plan, and it was forced**: the `ServeAsync` doc said `<see cref="Respond"/>` where the method is `RespondAsync` — Cli never checked docs, `Anomalies` does, so keeping the old text yields CS1574. **A rationale rewrite in `Sources/Cli/BannedSymbols.txt` was wrong on the first attempt and caught**: it claimed `AnomalyGuardCommand` takes an `IClock` from DI; after the move **no type in `Sources/Cli` reads a clock at all** (12 files, 0 hits), and the shipped text now says that, which is checkable. **The architect's open question is closed**: every production path to `SuppressionStore` goes through `RunCycleCore`, whose single caller `RunCycle:464` takes `lock (_gate)` — but `Suppressions` is `public`, so nothing prevents an unlocked path being added later. The box runs SDK 10.0.110 / runtime **10.0.10**. **No `PackageVersion` edit fixes this** — it is the shared runtime, so the SDK and runtime installs are what have to move, on every machine that matters including CI images and the guard's container base. **The instrument's limitation is the part worth keeping**: `dotnet list package --vulnerable --include-transitive` reports **clean across all 27 projects**, and that is not evidence, because it reads the GitHub Advisory Database, which routinely lags a same-day release by hours to days. A clean `--vulnerable` on the day of a servicing release means the database has not caught up, not that the tree is unaffected — re-run it in a day or two. **RUNTIME HALF CLOSED — verified 2026-08-14.** SDK **10.0.111** and runtime **10.0.11** (NETCore, AspNetCore, WindowsDesktop) are installed on this box alongside 10.0.10, with no `global.json`, so `dotnet` defaults to the patched SDK. `dotnet list Overfit.sln package --vulnerable --include-transitive` is **clean across all 26 projects, and this time the clean result carries information**: three days have passed, so the Advisory-DB lag that made the same-day result meaningless no longer applies. **The reminder in this row stands and is worth repeating**: that check reads NuGet packages and would never have reported `CVE-2026-62897` either way — it is a shared-runtime CVE, and installing the runtime is what closed it. It is the **only** file in `Sources/Main` shaped this way, so it is not a house style. **The cost is not the dead file, it is the live surface attached to it**: `Data/Contracts/FeatureImportanceAnalyzerConfig.cs` and `Data/Contracts/FeatureImportanceReport.cs` compile, ship in the public API of a published NuGet package, and are referenced by **nothing except the commented-out class** — a consumer can construct a config for an analyzer that does not exist, and `IFeatureNameProvider` still names it in its documentation. | `AN-D10` | **DONE** | `aiops-detection-pipeline.md` was stale on incident matching, in two places and in two different ways. Corrected 2026-08-10 against `IncidentTracker.cs:19-38`. The section opened by describing matching as **overlap of (subject, signal) pairs** — a design the same document refutes forty lines further down — and later as *"same primary plus subject overlap"*, treating the overlap as a gate. **The code matches on the primary subject alone**; overlap ranks competing candidates and does not veto. The veto was removed after it cost a shadow run an incident at an overlap of **0.33 against a bar of 0.34** — same pod, same fault, one hundredth short — and the fix was not a lower bar, because the bar measured the wrong thing: an incident's periphery rotates by design, which is the same property that killed the pairs key. Rewritten with the correction marked as a correction rather than silently patched, and with what removing the veto **costs** stated in place: two unrelated problems on one pod are one incident, which is the policy the grouper already applies within a cycle |
| `AN-D11` | **DEFER — DECIDED — kept as a roadmap item** | two execution paths that never meet at runtime | **Measured 2026-08-10**: `Sources/Anomalies/Live/` is two files, 416 lines; `find_references` returns 7 hits, five inside its own files and two in `LiveMonitoringPipelineTests`; no production consumer outside `Live/`, no CLI command constructs it, absent from every manifest. Reachable only from a test. **It is not dead code by accident**: it is the learned path — Prometheus → `MetricSnapshot` per pod → `AdaptiveAnomalyMonitor` scoring with a per-pod LoRA adapter → alerting on raw GPT surprise in nats, adapters persisted as `<pod>.lora.bin` and reloaded across pod restarts. **Decision, 2026-08-10: keep it, recorded as a roadmap item rather than left to be rediscovered.** The cost was never the 416 lines — it was that nobody could tell whether it was intent or residue, so every reader had to re-derive the answer. That question is now answered in `ROADMAP.md`. **What keeping it obliges**: it consumes `MetricSnapshot`'s feature vector, so it has to stay in step with `MetricIndex.Count` vs `FeatureCount` — the contract that must not shift when a channel is added. Nothing enforces that today, and that is the residual risk of choosing (a) over (c) |
| `AN-D13` | **DONE — contract fixed, query unchanged** | **`CpuUsageRatio` produces cores, not a share of the CPU limit.** Found 2026-08-10 by the unit-contract gate on its first run, in all three shipped configurations; invisible until then because every `lab-workload` pod carries `cpu: 1`, so cores and share-of-limit are numerically identical there. **The obvious repair was measured and refused**: cAdvisor exports no `container_spec_cpu_quota`/`_period` in this cluster at all (0 series), and while `kube_pod_container_resource_limits` does carry the limit, **24 of 42 pods have no CPU limit set** — including the guard itself, coredns, etcd and kafka. Dividing would return NO SERIES for those, so the channel would go dark on exactly the unlimited pods, and absence reads as health. **Fixed by correcting what the system claims instead**: `MetricSnapshot`'s feature-[0] comment and `MetricUnits.Declared` now say Cores, and the gate's accepted-deviation ratchet is deleted rather than left carrying a stale entry. Safe because the only consumers today are the RELATIVE families, which rank and are scale-indifferent — verified that no absolute rule targets this channel, neither in `AnomalyGuardOptions.DefaultRules` (`CpuThrottleRatio`, `OomEventsRate`, `ContainerRestarts`) nor in any shipped config (`ActiveRequests`, `LockContentions`, `Exceptions`, `CpuPressure`). **The enum member is deliberately NOT renamed**: `MetricIndex` values are persisted as the `MetricTypeId` byte on every stored series and the member names are the keys in `guard.json`, so renaming would invalidate historical data and every deployed configuration to correct a comment. **Deferred, not avoided**: the day somebody wants a threshold meaning "80% of the limit", this decision has to be revisited, and the comment in `MetricUnits` says so |
| `AN-D12` | **DONE** | **`ErrorRate` was deployed in the wrong unit, and the config file contradicted itself about whether it was bound at all.** Found 2026-08-09 by `overfit-analyst` writing the metrics catalogue; verified independently. `MetricSnapshot` documents feature `[8] ErrorRate — rate(5xx) / rate(total)`, a fraction in `[0,1]`. The deployed binding is `{"source": "labapp_errors_total", "kind": "Counter"}`, which `MetricMap.Build` renders as `sum by (pod) (rate(labapp_errors_total{S}[range]))` — **errors per second, unbounded**. Live in both `k8s/anomaly-guard/guard.lab-workload.json` and the ConfigMap in `k8s/lab/anomaly-guard.yaml`, so not a stray comment. Meanwhile the same file's own top comment says `ErrorRate` is *"left out and reported blind"* because the map cannot express errors-over-total as one binding — the comment and the block below it describe different configurations. **Why it matters beyond tidiness**: `ErrorRate` is a built-in `MetricIndex`, so it feeds `MetricSnapshot.WriteFeatureVector` and therefore the learned family's input contract; a threshold or floor reasoned about as a fraction means nothing against a per-second rate. This is the unit trap `CLAUDE.md` names, arriving through configuration rather than through a threshold. **Intent settled from git history 2026-08-10, and it is the comment that is deliberate.** `679653c` (2026-08-01) wrote the standalone file *with* the comment — ErrorRate left out, with a stated reason. `f7fcc46`, **the same day**, bound `labapp_errors_total` in the ConfigMap anyway. The two files disagreed from the day they were written, and `a294b79` (2026-08-09) — the commit that added `DeployedConfigParityTests` — resolved the disagreement by copying the binding **into** the standalone file, never touching the comment. **So the parity gate propagated the wrong side.** That is the finding worth keeping: a test that enforces sameness between two artefacts makes them agree, and has no opinion about which one was right. **Fixed 2026-08-10 on the user's decision — binding dropped from both artefacts, reported-blind restored**, and the file now agrees with its own comment for the first time since it was written. Read back from the parsed structures on both sides (not from the diff — the ConfigMap is JSON embedded in YAML): `ErrorRate` absent in both, `metrics`/`customMetrics`/`thresholds` still identical, 12 built-in + 5 custom channels. **The cost is stated and accepted: the deployed guard's `blind` count goes 0 → 1**, undoing a headline result from 2026-08-09. That is the honest reading — a channel fed a per-second rate while its contract says fraction is worse than a channel openly not measured. **Applied and verified the same day**: read back from the cluster, `ErrorRate` absent from the live ConfigMap, guard restarted, and the running guard reports `blind=1` while naming the channel — *"blind: ErrorRate has no binding — no query will be issued"*. One thing this did NOT fix: nothing prevents the next binding whose `kind` disagrees with `MetricSnapshot`'s documented unit — `MetricKindSanityTests` checks `_total` against Gauge/Ratio, not a channel's documented unit against its rendered PromQL |

## Transcribed from `ROADMAP.md` (2026-08-08)

Dropped as not-actionable, with reason: the **release-readiness snapshot** (2026-05-29) is a point-in-time
status table superseded by later sections; the **status snapshot** table is pure status, nothing to do;
**long-term ideas** are unscoped with no owner (graph compilation, mixed precision, GPU investigation — not
sized, not actionable as written). Shipped-and-accurately-labelled items are not transcribed at all (M.E.AI
adapter, OpenAI API, JSON-Schema, trailing-babble/acronym/LoRA-merge TTS fixes, embeddings, Whisper,
interpretability hooks, MCP server side) — the roadmap already tells the truth about these.

**Standing constraint, not a task list: decode throughput is closed.** Every lever tried — MHA consolidation
(+1-2%, shelved), Q8-KV (shipped, but for RAM not speed), register/cache-blocking (prefill 1.61x TTFT;
cache-blocking itself measured negative and reverted), AVX-512/VNNI (≈0), adaptive early-exit (≈4.6% ceiling
at unacceptable quality cost) — is measured. The residual ~1.13x gap to llama.cpp is uniform across context
length and memory-access-efficiency bound, not a missing kernel. Treat any new decode-speed proposal as guilty
until it is sized against its share of the decode step (`ROADMAP.md` §"Decode throughput catch-up").

### NASA Power of 10 follow-ups — `NR-`

| `NR-8b` | **DONE 2026-08-11** | OVERFIT039 — nothing in this repository checked for blocking on a task | Found by asking a question, not by a plan: the xunit v3 migration brought an analyzer that flags sync-over-async **in tests** and it found 7 sites; auditing `Sources` for the same shape found **16 more**, and none of the 38 existing OVERFIT rules covered it — `OVERFIT032` watches `ConfigureAwait`, `OVERFIT027` `async void`, `OVERFIT029/030` async naming, and nothing watched the block itself. **The worst two are in the redaction gateway's REQUEST PATH** (`RedactionGateway.cs:292` and `:575`), reading an upstream response body with `ReadAsStringAsync().GetAwaiter().GetResult()` on the thread serving the caller — thread-pool starvation in the component that sits between a customer and their LLM. The rest: 7 in `Cli/Commands.cs`, 3 more in `Cli`, 3 at gateway start/stop, 1 in `EndpointHelpers`. **The gate that makes the rule usable is the receiver's TYPE, not the method name**: `SemaphoreSlim.Wait()` and friends are blocking primitives, not tasks, and `Runtime/OverfitParallel.cs` has three correct ones in its decode loop — a rule that flagged those would be suppressed wholesale within a day. The ancestor set is what terminates a cycle; a cycle is now a catchable `ArgumentException`. **A regression nearly shipped: "on the current path" is not "seen before"** — one global visited set rejects `s.Add(relu); s.Add(relu)`, one stateless instance at two positions, which is a legal model. 10 new tests (none existed for this method) + 2 mutations: skipping a child reds 5 tests including both cycle tests (clean red, not a hang); reproducing the naive design reds exactly the 2 shared-instance tests and nothing else. Suite 2335/1/272, the 1 being `TG-T12` |
| `NR-5` | **PART — AUDITED 2026-08-11**, fixes OPEN | all 11 files of `Sources/Main/Audio/Mp3` read end to end | **Two crash-class defects on user-supplied input, and the first one I re-read and confirmed myself rather than relaying.** (1) `Mp3Decoder.cs:531` — `pos -= 4` ("overshoot — drop the last quad") has **no lower bound**. `_bigValues[g]` and `_part23[g]` are raw unvalidated side-info fields, and the scalefactor bit length is driven independently by `_scfCompress[g]`, so a corrupt file can leave `br.BitPosition` past `bitPosEnd` before the count1 loop runs even once: `bigEnd` = 0 keeps `pos` at 0, the `while (pos <= 572 && br.BitPosition <= bitPosEnd)` body never executes, and line 531 makes `pos` **-4** going into `for (; pos < 576; pos++) _is[isBase + pos] = 0f;`. For g=0 (`isBase` 0) that is a negative index → uncaught `IndexOutOfRangeException` and the process goes down; for g≥1 it lands **inside** the array in the previous granule's slot and silently zeroes four already-decoded samples of another channel — wrong audio, no exception, no signal. **The damning part is 40 lines above it**: the `bigEnd` clamp at `:490-495` documents this exact class ("a crafted or merely corrupt file therefore indexed past the buffer, and nothing on the path from `Mp3Reader` or `AudioFile` catches it — user-supplied input taking the process down"), and `ReadScaleFactorsLsf` in the same file guards its `sfb` index the same way. The fix was applied to two places in this method and not to the third. (2) `Mp3Huffman.Walk` `:115-166` — the chaining loops `while ((ht[off+point] & 0xff) >= 250) point += …` index `Mp3HuffmanData.Table` (2,804 entries) with no bound **inside** the inner loop; only the outer `do…while` checks `point < treelen`, and only after the inner loop has returned. Table select 33 has **zero slack** (`off` 2773 + `treelen` 31 = 2804 exactly). **REFUTED 2026-08-11, and the refutation is a proof rather than a failed search.** The reachability question is finite — the table is 2,804 fixed entries and the chase is deterministic — so it was settled by parsing the real `Mp3HuffmanData.Table` out of the source and breadth-first enumerating **every** reachable `point` for all 34 non-empty table selects, following both the low-byte and high-byte chases with no bound check, exactly as the code does. **No table can exceed its span, table 33 included**, and the result is sharper than "no crash found": for every one of the 34, `max_point` is **exactly `treelen - 1`** — every reachable leaf sits inside the table's own allotment with no waste. The reason generalises and is worth keeping: these are ISO-standard complete binary trees, and the `>= 250` chaining expresses an in-table jump too large for one byte, never an escape. A fuzz was deliberately not used — it could only ever have produced "not observed", which would have left the same decision open. **Bonus finding, also proven**: the outer `do…while (point < treelen)` check is **dead** — not one reachable chase in any table lands with `point >= treelen`. Keep the guard (it is what would catch a future edit to the table) but the site deserves a comment saying it has never bound, because a guard nobody can point to as having fired is a guard the next reader will assume is load-bearing. Also confirmed the enumeration was not truncated by the real 32-iteration `bitsleft` cap: maximum depth reached was 20 and no node was cut off. Lower severity: `Mp3Reader.cs:63-64` `frames`/`samples` are `int` with no overflow guard (~13 h at 32 kbps ≈ 1.86M frames wraps negative, and `DecodeMono`'s `SampleCount > 0 ? … : 0` then returns an **empty** array instead of decoding or throwing); `SkipId3` `:72-81` does not mask the ID3v2 syncsafe size to 7 bits per byte, so a non-compliant tag yields a too-large skip and the file degrades to "all tag, no audio". **Checked and sound**, so nobody re-audits them: `Mp3BitReader.ReadBits` (soft-fails to 0 past EOF by design), `Mp3FrameHeader.TryParse` (validates every index before any table lookup), the bit-reservoir bounds, the frame-walk loops (monotonic progress, no infinite loop), the `stackalloc int[4]` sites. **Scope, stated**: local-file parser only — no `Sources/Server.AspNet` usage, reachable via CLI, `WhisperTranscriber` and `VoiceCloneDatasetBuilder`, all local paths, so this is not endpoint-exposed. **(1) is confirmed REACHABLE through the public entry point, with the frame in hand** — established 2026-08-11 by re-implementing `TryParse`, `ParseSideInfo`, `ReadScaleFactorsMpeg1` and the `ReadHuffman` prefix in Python and **running** them, rather than by asserting the arithmetic off the C#. Mono MPEG-1 Layer III, one 104-byte frame: header `FF FB 10 C0` (32 kbps, 44100 Hz, mono — `FrameLengthBytes` 144×32000/44100 = 104, `SideInfoBytes` 17), then side info carrying granule 0 **`part2_3_length` = 1, `big_values` = 0, `scalefac_compress` = 4** and everything else zero, then 83 bytes of irrelevant filler. `scalefac_compress` 4 gives `slen1` 3 / `slen2` 0, and granule 0 reads fresh scalefactor bits whatever `scfsi` says, so `(6+5)×3 = 33` bits are consumed while `bitPosEnd = 0 + 1 - 1 = 0`; both decode loops are then skipped (`bigEnd` 0, and `33 <= 0` false), line 529's `33 > 1` holds, and `pos` becomes -4 with `isBase` 0. **The general defect, which is the part to keep**: there is **no cross-field validation between `part2_3_length`, `big_values` and `scalefac_compress`**, so a frame that legitimately clears `Mp3FrameHeader.TryParse` can claim fewer bits than its own scalefactors consume. **(1) FIXED 2026-08-11** — `pos = Math.Max(pos - 4, 0)` with a `BOUND:` comment. **`_count1[g] = 0` is the count, not a fallback, and the file already contained the precedent**: lines 474-478 handle `_part23[g] == 0` by setting `_count1[g] = 0` and returning, i.e. "the frame claimed no bits"; clamping makes "the frame claimed fewer bits than its own scalefactors consume" agree with that instead of inventing a third policy. One correction to the diagnosis while fixing it: **the throw is in the zeroing loop at `:534`, not at the subtraction** — `_count1[g] = -4` alone would not throw, because `Requantize` uses it only as a loop bound. **Mutation executed**: clamp removed → the g=0 frame throws again, restored → it decodes to silence; restore verified byte-for-byte. **What is NOT covered, and it is the more insidious half**: the silent-corruption variant is **byte-identical with and without the clamp**, so it remains unpinned — and the reason was corrected twice on the way to being understood, which is worth recording. First reading: "any later granule lands inside the array in the preceding slot." True in general, **false for a mono fixture** — `GC(gr, ch) = gr * 2 + ch`, so mono (always `ch = 0`) puts granule 1 at `g = 2`, `isBase = 1152`, and the underflow writes `_is[1148..1151]`, which is granule 0 / **channel 1** — a slot a mono decode never reads. That fixture corrupted nothing at all, not merely nothing observable. The mechanism needs **stereo**, where granule 0 / channel 1 is `g = 1` and the underflow reaches back into channel 0's live region. Second reading, on the stereo frame: still identical, because channel 0 there carries `part2_3_length = 0` and `ReadHuffman` clears `_is[isBase..isBase+575]` **before** its early return, so 572-575 are already zero when `g = 1` clobbers them. Zero over zero. Pre-seeding a sentinel through internal access was proposed and **checked and refuted**: channel 0's own clearing loop runs before channel 1's underflow and wipes it. Observing it needs channel 0 to decode genuinely nonzero samples into 572-575, i.e. valid codewords out of `Mp3HuffmanData.Table`. **The reproduction itself is unusually well established**: two independent derivations — one from a Python re-implementation of the header/side-info/scalefactor reads, one from the side-info field table, neither author having seen the other's — agree **byte for byte** on both the mono and stereo frames, and a fourth test now asserts the builder still emits exactly those bytes, so a later edit to the bit packing cannot silently turn the other three into tests of a different frame. The clamp fixes both by construction (`isBase + pos` can no longer go below `isBase`) but **only the crash is pinned by a test**, and the class doc-comment says so, so nobody later reads two green tests as covering two defects. 3 new `[Fact]`s in `Tests/Audio/Mp3MalformedFrameTests.cs` — crash case, g≥1 shape case, and a **consistent-frame control** proving the hand-built frame is accepted, without which the two above could pass on a frame rejected for an unrelated reason. The two defect tests assert **exact silence** rather than mere finiteness, deliberately, because that pins the *choice* of `_count1 = 0`; the value was measured before the assertion was written, not predicted. **Worth knowing**: `Mp3ReaderTests` is `[LongFact]` and needs a real `jfk.mp3`, so **MP3 decoding had no default coverage at all** — these are the first `[Fact]`s ever to run over that decoder. **Open**: the other three — the dead outer bound in `Mp3Huffman.Walk` (documented, keep), the `int` overflow in `Probe`, the unmasked ID3v2 syncsafe size |
| `NR-6` | OPEN | census the lengths `Simd.Add`/`Simd.MulAdd` actually receive before moving `Avx512Threshold` | verified: `Sources/Main/Intrinsics/Simd.cs:19` comment still says the threshold is unmeasured and wrong at the low end (128 floats: 512-bit won every op in the microbench that exists) |
| `NR-7` | OPEN | re-run `ElseRefactorBenchmark` under .NET 10's larger inlining budget | verified: `Sources/Benchmark/ElseRefactorBenchmark.cs` last touched 2026-07-23, before the .NET 10 note was written (2026-08-04/05). `OVERFIT021`'s 2.25x extraction-penalty justification has not been re-measured against the new JIT |
| `NR-8` | **DONE 2026-08-10** | `QwenTokenizer`'s decoder growth is bounded against the tokenizer.json byte length; the last `#pragma warning disable OVERFIT038` in the tree is gone | **The bound is bytes-in-the-file, and the alternative was measured before being rejected.** `new string[id + 1]` at 8 bytes an entry on an id from the file makes a declared `int.MaxValue` a 16 GB allocation the caller cannot catch. The obvious bound `vocab.Count + added.Count` clears the real Qwen file by exactly ONE (151,664 against 151,665), so one gap in the added-token ids would refuse a valid model. **Measured on that file**: 7,031,645 B for 151,665 tokens = **46.4 bytes per token**, so a 4-byte floor clears the highest id by **11.6x** and caps the allocation at 14 MB whatever the file claims; a malicious `int.MaxValue` would need an 8.6 GB file to pass. **Verified**: 6 tests that write their own tokenizer.json and need no fixture, both sides pinned — refusal AND that an id with a gap above the vocabulary still loads; the real 7 MB Qwen tokenizer loads unchanged (checked with a one-shot probe, since `QwenTokenizerTests` is `[FixtureFact]` and skips on this box); mutation loosening the bound 1000x reds `TheSameIdIsRefusedInASmallFileAndAcceptedInALargerOne` and only it. ### Performance backlog — `PB-`

shipped/closed and accurately labelled — not transcribed except where a follow-on remains open.

| id | status | task | note |

| `PB-7` | **DONE 2026-08-14** (status cell corrected 2026-08-14: it still read `OPEN` while the body below already recorded the work as done, and that contradiction gave a reader the wrong answer within the hour) | make `dotnet build` join the three-level machine-exclusion mutex scheme | verified: only test (`Tests/MeasurementExclusion.cs`) and benchmark (`Sources/Benchmark/Program.cs`) hold `Global\DevOnBike.Overfit.MachineMeasurement`; `Directory.Build.props` has no `OVERFIT_MEASUREMENT_OWNER` guard. The roadmap's anomaly-guard section specifies this design in full but it is unbuilt **THE INCIDENT THIS ROW WAS WAITING FOR ARRIVED 2026-08-13.** The intent is that nothing builds, benchmarks or tests while anything else does; measured, the mutex has **exactly two participants**, so benchmark↔benchmark, benchmark↔tests and tests↔tests are guarded and **build↔anything is not**. A `dotnet build Sources/Benchmark` ran inside a live `[LongFact]` window, rewriting assemblies a test host had loaded, and is the prime suspect for the stall diagnosed in `XC-42`. **The behavioural rule is not enough and the agent that broke it is the proof**: it checked for a live `testhost`, saw one, and built anyway, because its rule covered *measuring* and not *building*. **The shape of the fix already exists in this repository** — `Main.csproj` runs `RoslynCodeTaskFactory` tasks `BeforeTargets="CoreCompile"`, so a task that probes the mutex and fails the build with the guard's own sentence is the same construct. **Two traps to design around rather than discover.** (1) `dotnet test` and `dotnet run --project Sources/Benchmark` **both build first**, so "the build takes the lock and holds it" deadlocks the very commands it protects — the build's check must finish and release before the test host's `MeasurementExclusion` runs. (2) A probe fires **once per project** and this solution has 26; gate it to run once or the message becomes wallpaper. **A cheaper 80% version worth considering first**: make the build *check* rather than *lock* — fail fast when the mutex is held, covering the direction that caused harm, and leave the reverse to fail loudly on its own as a broken assembly does. **One diagnostic detail, from the agent that hit it**: `AbandonedMutexException` means the owning thread exited without releasing, so the existing catch handles a process that DIED mid-hold and does **not** handle a live orphan — exit 2 with no exception means "something alive is holding it", never "something died holding it". **Filed by me as a duplicate (`XC-43`) the same day and withdrawn** — hours after I had written the "check the series before filing" rule into this file's own header, which is the kind of thing worth recording rather than quietly deleting **DONE 2026-08-14 — the cheap variant, and its keystone was measured rather than assumed.** `OverfitBuildExclusionCheck` in `Directory.Build.targets`, gated on `MSBuildProjectName == 'Main'` so it fires **once per build** rather than 26 times, probes the mutex with a small inline task, **never takes it**, and **fails open on any exception**. Seven arms, all as designed: nothing held → builds; held → `error OVERFITMEASURING` with the written message, exit 1; held plus incremental (Main already up to date) → still fires, because `BeforeTargets="CoreCompile"` runs anyway; and three independent escape hatches (`OVERFIT_ALLOW_CONCURRENT_MEASUREMENT=1`, `OVERFIT_MEASUREMENT_OWNER=<pid>`, `-p:OverfitBuildExclusionCheck=false`) each let it through. **The ownership handshake is load-bearing and that was proven by breaking it**: `Sources/Benchmark/Program.cs` now sets `OVERFIT_MEASUREMENT_OWNER` after acquiring, and mutating that one line out makes the guard **break every benchmark on the box** — `executed benchmarks: 0` — because BenchmarkDotNet's generated per-job build does reach `Main.csproj` and does inherit the parent environment. **A correction to this row and to the brief that dispatched it, both mine**: *"the shape already exists — `Main.csproj` runs `RoslynCodeTaskFactory` tasks"* is **stale**. `Main.csproj:82-101` records that all three were moved out on 2026-08-05 and that the file no longer contains code. The live precedent is `OverfitPrereleaseCheck` in `Directory.Build.targets`, which is what was copied, gate and all. The reason for re-introducing an inline task at all is written at the site: MSBuild property functions cannot call `Mutex.TryOpenExisting`, and an analyzer runs inside the compiler — already too late to stop a build overwriting a loaded assembly. Suite **2628/0/274**, verified independently. Cost disclosed by the developer rather than hidden: proving the handshake took **7 min 31 s** of box time, because the benchmark class's own config overrode `--job Dry` and 44 benchmarks ran; those numbers are not usable for anything **TWO GAPS LEFT, NAMED 2026-08-14 rather than left implicit.** (1) **The lock's name exists as three independent string literals** — `MeasurementExclusion.MutexName`, `Program.SingleRunMutexName` and the `MutexName=` attribute in `Directory.Build.targets:161` — and `find_references` shows the first has exactly one use, its own constructor. Three copies of a string that must match character for character: edit one and the guard stops guarding **silently**, because both sides simply take different mutexes and both proceed. Nothing tests that the three agree; a test that asserts it is cheap and is the only thing that would catch the edit. (2) **The build only PROBES, it never TAKES**, which was the deliberate cheap-variant choice — so a benchmark or a suite started while a long build is in flight is not refused, and the build it collides with is the one that loses. Worth revisiting only if that direction actually bites; the direction that did bite is covered |
| `PB-8` | **DONE — REFUTED** | huge pages / TLB tuning for the decode weight mmap | closed 2026-07-05 — Windows has no large-page support for file mappings (only private commits), and the 96 MB vs 32 MB V-Cache CCD measured identically (13.75 vs 13.96 tok/s); a 2 GB model dwarfs any L3 |
| `PB-9` | DEFER, explicitly | per-CPU/per-RAM worker-count lookup table | the mechanism (never take every CPU) is already one line in `ResolveDecodeMaxWorkers`; a table would overfit to the single measured machine — build an opt-in `overfit tune` profiler instead if a materially different box ever shows up |
| `PB-10` | OPEN, unsized | misc backlog: `LinearKernels` threshold retuning, `Adam`/`AdamW` parameter-parallel update audit, AVX2/AVX-512/AVX10 SIMD audit, thread-scaling stabilisation for large training workloads | grouped because none is individually sized; revisit when GPT-2-scale batched training (`PB-1`) lands |
| `PB-11` | OPEN | **on-device decode rate declines ~20% within a single answer, and nothing explains it** | Observed 2026-08-14 on a Motorola Edge 50 Fusion (Snapdragon 7s Gen 2), SmolLM2-135M F32, AOT, threads pinned to the big cluster, 4 workers. The per-32-token series falls monotonically across every run: `11.3 10.4 10.6 10.1 9.9 10.0 9.5 9.1 9.2` (303 tokens), and earlier `11.5 10.6 10.3 10.4 9.9 9.7 9.4 9.4 9.0 8.8` (335 tokens) — roughly **-20% from first window to last**, reproducible across four configurations. It is small next to the day's 2.4x, which is why it is filed rather than chased. **What it is NOT, each ruled out by measurement on the same device (see `docs/measured-baselines.md`):** not the cumulative-average display artefact — the windowed series is what shows it; not context growth — `DecodeProfiler` puts 92% of per-token time in `attention`+`ffn` while `other` measures ~0, and the decline appears in configurations whose attention share differs; not memory reclaim — `VmSwap` 5 MB and zero major faults during generation; not cpuset confinement — `top-app`, cpus 0-7. **Leading candidate, unmeasured: thermal.** CPU zones read 30-39 C while idle-ish and 41-48 C during pinned generation, and the pinned runs are the hot ones — but no run has correlated the per-window rate against a temperature or frequency sample taken *in the same window*, which is the whole measurement. **Second candidate: KV-cache growth**, cheap to separate — attention cost rises with position while FFN does not, so a per-window profiler split would show `attention` climbing and `ffn` flat if that is it, and both climbing together if it is thermal/DVFS. **How to run it:** log a temperature, a big-core frequency and the `DecodeProfiler` component split **per 32-token window** rather than once per generation, then plot rate against each. Needs one generation and no cluster. **What would refute the thermal explanation:** a run started from a cold phone showing the same first-to-last decline, or the decline persisting after a cooldown pause mid-generation |
| `PB-12` | **PART — AUDITED 2026-08-14 — 3 of 4 published numbers NOT SUPPORTED, and the regression question is CLOSED by measurement: the 2026-08-14 claim/park fix did NOT cost throughput** | **the decode pool's headline throughput numbers predate the synchronisation change on that exact path** | Raised by `overfit-reviewer` on 2026-08-14. `OverfitParallel`'s own comments cite +11% (Bielik), +28% / +3% (Qwen3-0.6B / Phi-3.5) and `455 us / 0 B against Parallel.For's 2059 us / 925 KB` — all measured against the **buggy** claim protocol, which has since been replaced by a CAS loop, and the park path by a monitor. `find_references` puts **8 production call sites** on `ForDecode` — every quantised GEMV/projection path plus both `CachedMultiHeadAttention` paths — so this is every decode. The fix is not optional and its cost is not the question; what is at stake is that four published numbers now describe code that no longer exists. **Owner is `overfit-perf-claim-auditor`**, best-of-N both sides, once the machine is free — the release gate owns it as this is written. Either the numbers survive and are re-stamped with the new date, or they move and the comments are corrected **VERDICT, 24 measurement processes, ABAB at process level, best-of-3 within each, a single-thread canary before and after every timed block, and a dispatch-count liveness probe in every process (Ryzen 9 9950X3D, .NET 10.0.8, Release, HEAD `e21e7c3`).** **(1) `455 µs / 0 B` vs `2059 µs / 925 KB` = 4.5x — NOT SUPPORTED.** Measured 2.43x against the capped path and 3.60x against uncapped `Parallel.For`; the published pair sits on the *uncapped* arm, but with `OVERFIT_DECODE_POOL=0` today's `ForDecode` falls back to the **capped** path — so **4.5x describes a comparison the product no longer makes anywhere on the decode path**. Honest figure: 2.3-2.7x at dispatch level. `0 B` confirmed; `925 KB` confirmed shape-dependent (862 KB-1.13 MB). **(2) Qwen3-0.6B +28% — SUPPORTED**, re-stamp: +25.1% (Q8_0), +23.0% (Q4_K_M); the original comment records no quantisation. **(3) Phi-3.5 +3% — NOT SUPPORTED, sign reversed: −1.9%.** The untouched arm reproduces to 0.8% while the treated arm fell 4.2%, so the treatment moved, not the box. **Mechanism is a wrong denominator**: on Phi-3.5 the pool captures only 75% of dispatches — **52 per token still go through `OverfitParallel.For`** via the GQA `For(0, KvHeadCount, ...)` at `CachedMultiHeadAttention.cs:284`. The point estimate is itself under the ±3-4% floor, so the correct statement is *neutral-to-negative on Phi-3.5*, not +3%. **(4) Bielik +11% — NOT SUPPORTED as stated.** Isolated to its original mechanism (pool off, cap 10 vs 32) it is **+3.8%**, every paired cycle positive, so the sign is real and the magnitude is not. At HEAD's default the same knob is worth **+87%**, because the cap now also sizes the spin pool — and `OVERFIT_DECODE_WORKERS=32` costs **−47%** today against the −11% the comment at `:179-180` documents. **THE FINDING THAT OUTLIVES THE NUMBERS: `ForDecode` has never had a benchmark class.** `git log -S "ForDecode" -- Sources/Benchmark` returns zero commits. All four figures came from `[ModelFact]`-gated diagnostics that `dotnet test` never runs (`ModelFact : LongFact` sets `Skip` at discovery), single-arm, one process, no canary. **None of them was measurable by anything in `Sources/Benchmark`, before this audit or after it.** `OverfitParallelBenchmark` exercises `OverfitParallel.For`, not the decode pool. **Do not cite** `Sources/Benchmark/BenchmarkDotNet.Artifacts/results/Benchmarks.OverfitParallelForBenchmark-report-github.md` (2026-05-15, class deleted, `InvocationCount=1`, `RatioSD` to 11.83) — it is not the source of any published figure and it shows the opposite result at dispatch-bound sizes. **The protocol change's own cost is DERIVED, not measured**, and deliberately so: one uncontended CAS plus one monitor enter/pulse per dispatch is tens of ns against a ~3.4 µs dispatch and ~14 ms/token — **0.02-0.05% of token time**, three orders below the ±3-4% cross-process floor. No experiment on this box can resolve it; building the pre-change revision would have produced a number with no meaning. **OPEN WORK: the `455/2059` figure is written in FIVE places** — `Sources/Main/Runtime/OverfitParallel.cs:782-783`, `docs/measured-baselines.md:375`, `docs/performance-discipline.md:145`, `docs/code-patterns.md:98`, `Sources/Main/Runtime/README.md:16` — **and not one carries model, quantisation, dispatch count, comparator configuration, date or build**, in a repository whose own rule (`docs/measured-baselines.md:8`) is that every number carries what it was measured on. The `+28% / +3% / +11%` figures are **not in `docs/measured-baselines.md` at all**; they live only in source comments at `:359` and `:178-183` **REGRESSION QUESTION, RAISED BY THE CLIENT AND CLOSED BY EXPERIMENT (36 processes, AB/BA alternated so build position is not confounded with build, best-of-3 within each).** The hypothesis was that the claim/park fix — which replaced a *conditional* semaphore release with an **unconditional `lock` + `PulseAll` on every dispatch** — had cost throughput. **NOT SUPPORTED, and the predicted ordering is inverted.** A per-dispatch cost must hurt most where the dispatch census is highest; Bielik (383.6/token) is the one model where the **new** protocol is faster, while Qwen (183.1, the lowest census) shows the largest sub-1% advantage to the old code. Old/new ratios, pool ON: Qwen **1.007**, Phi **1.004**, Bielik **0.995** — each no larger than its own untouched pool-OFF canary (0.999 / 0.995 / 1.008), with the per-cycle sign flipping in every model. At dispatch level any per-dispatch cost is bounded **below ~90 ns against a 2.7 µs dispatch**, and that bound is now measured rather than derived. **Which branch of the fork: the numbers were always weaker than stated.** The published figures do not reproduce **on the old code either** — Qwen +23.3% against a published +28%, Phi **−0.8%** against a published +3%. So the documentation correction stands and there is no regression finding. **A near-miss worth more than the result, caught by the auditor in its own method**: it had established "these commits touched one file" from `git log -- <path>`, which is true and misleading — a path filter hides intermediate commits, and `git diff f8dd016 e21e7c3 -- Sources/Main` lists **17 files**, including `SingleTokenProjectionKernel.cs`, a decode hot-path file changed the same day for unrelated Android work. A whole-tree old build would have measured the sum of all of them and reported it as the protocol's cost. The arm actually built reverts **only `Runtime/OverfitParallel.cs`**, compiles the same harness against both, and asserts each binary's self-reported protocol matches its arm label. **Open hypothesis, flagged as such and NOT a finding**: the published `+11%` cap figure may be a **mislabelled lever** — the pool and the cap first appear in the same squashed commit (`8a28bdb`, 2026-06-11) and history cannot separate them, while today the pool is worth +8.8-10.2% on Bielik and the cap alone +3.8%. The published figure sits close to the pool's value, not the cap's |
